Zcash Just Dropped Below $1,000 for the First Time Since September. Is This a Buy-the-Dip Opportunity?

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Most of its recent decline has been caused by a turbulent market, which probably won't be improving much in the very near term.

By Alex Carchidi – Nov 21, 2026 at 6:03AM EST

With Zcash (ZEC -7.27%) falling to $922 during its first big drop below $1,000 since September, it's natural for investors to wonder whether it's time to buy the dip, especially considering that the coin is still up 73% this year so far.

Price action alone doesn't make for a good investment thesis, so let's take a closer look at this privacy coin and determine whether it's worth buying right now, despite the turbulence and uncertainty in the crypto market and the world.

Zcash's recent decline from its highs is to be expected, given the bumps in the road it has faced.

For instance, on Oct. 29, hackers stole $6.3 billion from Binance, the largest crypto exchange, marking the largest-ever crypto theft and worsening the climate of fear that has been intensifying since a spate of other crypto hacks earlier in the year. Similarly, when October's Consumer Price Index (CPI) data came in above market forecasts, it sparked a flight from risky assets like crypto amid expectations that the Federal Reserve will hike interest rates at its next meeting in December. Separately, investors pulled $46 million from Grayscale's Zcash exchange-traded fund (ETF) in October, followed by another $15 million in early November.

None of those issues were caused by anything related to Zcash itself. Its main investment thesis, namely that investors will continue to seek to conceal their transactions and account balances from prying eyes, is still valid.

Now, let's turn to whether it makes sense to buy this coin right now.

Zcash just launched its latest network upgrade, NU7, which makes the chain significantly faster. It cuts the target block time, the gap between each batch of its confirmed transactions, from 75 seconds to 25 seconds. It also sequesters 60% of the network's transaction fees into a reserve that will disburse its funds to pay miners starting in around 2031. Those changes are well and good, but they don't imply any need to rush with a purchase.

One interesting factor that might make it a better buy is the spread of privacy in crypto. On Nov. 10, Helius, a Solana (SOL -4.23%) infrastructure company, launched a privacy protocol for use on Solana. Its CEO signaled the design was inspired by Zcash's. This imitation validates Zcash, but it also means more competition in the future.

Overall, I believe in Zcash's long-term investment potential, but the market is a bit too fragile for me to buy. A coin that lost over 41% in just eight weeks can do it again, and I do not want to be taking risks like that in the current environment of uncertainty.

But for those with a very high risk tolerance and a five-year-plus holding horizon, not to mention already having a diversified portfolio, buying a small amount of Zcash right now could still pay off.

Colson Whitehead's Brilliant Thriller Lands On Amazon's List Of The Best Books Of 2026

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As 2026 draws to a close, many publishers and vendors are rounding up their list of the best books of 2026. It's no surprise that some incredible thriller books have landed on the Amazon Editor's list of the best books of 2026, including one of Colson Whitehead's most recent works.

The author of some of the greatest literature of the 21st century, readers who haven't yet delved into his expansive body of work are missing out. Whitehead's Pulitzer Prize-winning novel The Underground Railroad was adapted for the screen and is a perfect Prime Video miniseries for any viewer. His latest thriller is the conclusion of a trilogy Whitehead began in 2021, offering a stunning finale to a fun, atmospheric action-mystery series.

Whitehead is a two-time winner of the Pulitzer Prize, as his 2019 novel Nickel Boys was also awarded the top prize. The book was adapted into the critically acclaimed 2024 film of the same name. As one of the biggest book vendors of the modern era, the Amazon editors keep their fingers on the pulse not only of what's popular and attention-grabbing, but of landmark additions to the literary canon.

The third and final installment of Whitehead's Harlem Trilogy, Cool Machine, is number 10 on the Amazon list of 2026's best books. Featuring novels like American Hagwon by Min Jin Lee and Fruit Fly by Josh Silver, the list has a wide array of genres and novel types for readers of all kinds. However, thriller lovers will be glad to see that Cool Machine is featured prominently on the list.

Since 2016, some of the best thriller novels audiences have seen in years have come out, giving fans of the genre plenty to sink their teeth into.

The first two books in the series, Harlem Shuffle and Crook Manifesto, introduce the reader to Ray Carney, the compulsively compelling protagonist of the novels. Spanning the early 1960s through the '80s, the Harlem Trilogy is a vivid portrait of New York City during this period, digging into the historical events. The novel explores this through the lens of Ray's connection to the hidden underbelly of exciting heists and the people who want to control Harlem.

In Cool Machine, the reader picks up with Ray, his wife Elizabeth, and the future of his furniture business at the beginning of the significant economic and social changes of the 1980s. The changing landscape of New York is brought into sharp relief thanks to Whitehead's writing style and ability to craft characters the audience adores spending time with. Just as thrilling as his first two books in the series, without giving up the emotional poignancy Whitehead is known for, Cool Machine deserves its spot on the list of best books of 2026.

It doesn't come as a surprise that the Harlem Trilogy has been tapped for a screen adaptation at Apple TV+ (via Variety). Whitehead's work has been brought to life for both television and film, and his crystal-clear prose makes it easy to visualize the characters and settings of his stories. The Harlem Trilogy will make for a fantastic addition to Whitehead's filmography, as period pieces with elements of crime, especially heists, never go out of style.

Many readers, not just thriller fans, have found themselves falling in love with the Harlem Trilogy and blown away by the way Whitehead concludes the series in Cool Machine. Anyone who hasn't yet joined Ray on the streets of Harlem, or read Whitehead's work, should dive into these books as their next great read. Though 2026 isn't over yet, it's safe to say that Whitehead's Cool Machine will retain its spot as one of the best works of the year.

Cool Machine is available for purchase here.

Best Laptops for Travel (2026): Which to Take on the Road

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As a laptop reviewer, I have plenty of devices to choose from when I travel for work. In some cases, my favorite overall laptop doesn’t translate into the one I want to travel with. When I’m squeezed into a plane, I’ll choose maximum battery life and portability over features like a bigger screen or more ports.

Fortunately, there are many great travel laptops, and you’ll still appreciate them when you aren’t on the road. Of the hundreds of laptops I’ve tested in my life, here are the ones I always choose to travel with.

If there’s an obvious pick for the best travel laptop, it’s the Dell XPS 13. It’s been the best compact laptop you can buy for years, and that’s even more true in its latest incarnation. The key dimension to pay attention to on the XPS 13 is the depth, which is crucial for air travel. At 7.9 inches, it’s among the smallest laptops you can buy. Those economy-class seat-back tray tables on airplanes are only around 10 inches deep, and while that’s technically enough to accommodate even a 16-inch MacBook Pro, whether you can actually open the lid and use the laptop is another question. The XPS 13 is even smaller than the MacBook Neo, despite having a larger screen.

The XPS 13 achieves this by using a 13.4-inch display (measured diagonally), compared to many of the 14-inch laptops on this list. Second, it has very narrow bezels around that screen, minimizing the device’s overall footprint. While 14-inch laptops have become more popular in recent years, that bigger display size also increases the overall footprint—and every millimeter counts when working on a plane. The XPS 13 still has enough room for your palms when typing (unless you have huge hands), and the keyboard is among my favorites to type on.

The other two major advantages for travel are weight and battery life. This little laptop only weighs 2.2 pounds, a full half-pound lighter than the 13-inch MacBook Air. You might forget you have it in your backpack while traveling.

Now, the XPS 13 isn't the most powerful laptop in the world—at least not the cheaper models. The standard version comes with only 8 GB of RAM and an Intel chip with only two Performance cores. It’s good enough for basic web tasks, but for real work, you might run into limitations. You can address this by opting for the 16 GB model (which keeps the price under $1,000) or upgrading to the much more powerful Core Ultra 7 355 processor. The latter is much more expensive and comes with twice the storage, but it doesn’t add extra weight or size. Regardless of the chip you choose, the XPS 13 gets absolutely fantastic battery life. It depends on what you're doing with it, but you could easily work through a long international flight without worrying about plugging in.

If weight is your primary concern, the Asus Zenbook A14 is another contender. Despite being larger overall than the XPS 13 (with a bigger screen), the Zenbook A14 weighs 2.16 pounds—a hair lighter than the Dell. That’s seriously impressive, and I still remember how astounded I was when I first picked it up. Asus debuts its “ceraluminum” material in the chassis, which gives you the sturdiness of aluminum with the lightness of plastic. It’s not quite as rigid as metal, but it’s not as flimsy as ultra-light laptops in the past.

If you’re less worried about working on the plane and more about lugging a laptop around, the Zenbook A14 is a good choice. The Zenbook A14 has several advantages over the XPS 13 that can be helpful on the go, including built-in ports like a headphone jack, USB-A, and HDMI. If you’re working out of a remote office or presenting to a client, these can definitely come in handy. The XPS 13 only has two USB-C ports and nothing else.

There are a number of different versions of the Asus Zenbook A14 floating around out there. I’d stay away from the 8 GB model Asus sells and opt for the 16 GB model instead. There’s also a newer model that uses the Snapdragon X2 Elite chip, but I don’t think it’s worth the extra $450—especially not for a laptop focused on travel.

The 13-inch MacBook Air also makes a great travel laptop. It’s relatively small, thanks to its 13.6-inch screen.

In terms of portability, its main advantage over the XPS 13 and Zenbook A14 is its thinness. At 0.42 inches, it’s the thinnest laptop out there—and it doesn’t stick huge rubber feet on the bottom to compensate like other laptops do, such as the Razer Blade 14. You might not think thickness matters much in a travel laptop, but if you’re squeezing stuff into a backpack, every extra millimeter matters.

The MacBook Neo is also worth mentioning here. It’s not quite as thin as the MacBook Air, but because its screen is 0.4 inches smaller, it has a more compact overall footprint. Battery life is the bigger issue when taking the Neo on a trip, as it doesn’t last as long as the MacBook Air or Dell XPS 13. The 8 GB of RAM limitation doesn’t affect its use as a travel laptop much, but it's something to consider for a work laptop in general. That said, it has a very pretty screen for a laptop at this price.

Budget laptops aren’t typically known for being particularly lightweight and travel-worthy. Most of them struggle, especially with battery life. The HP OmniBook 5 might be an exception. You can usually find it on sale for under $700, even with 16 GB of RAM and an OLED display.

The OmniBook 5 isn’t quite as compact as the MacBook Neo because of its larger screen, but it is also half an inch thick and weighs only 2.8 pounds. The Snapdragon X Plus also delivers solid battery life, plenty to feel comfortable traveling with. It’s not a laptop without compromises (like the poor touchpad), but it comes with 16 GB of RAM, unlike some other laptops at this price. That’s a solid deal if you need something to travel with on a budget.

Good question. Tablets can make for great travel devices because of their size and ease of use. If you need a proper keyboard and touchpad—and the app support to handle a typical workday—you’re probably better off getting a laptop.

The Surface Pro 12 is the two-in-one laptop I’d recommend first. It’s absolutely tiny for a computer, even though it runs the full version of Windows 11. Even with the detachable keyboard connected, it weighs only 1.8 pounds. The only real problem with bringing this on a plane is the kickstand, which adds some extra depth. It’s just small enough that it should fit while open on an airplane tray table, unlike the 13.8-inch Surface Pro model.

Of course, that kickstand is also a huge advantage for the Surface Pro 12 if you’re using it without the keyboard. While that would make work difficult, it’s ideal for watching movies. Set this up on your tray table with some movies downloaded, and you’ll be the envy of your aisle. And when you need to get some work done in a coffee shop or at a smaller table, you’ll be happy to have access to the keyboard and touchpad—both of which are very comfortable to use. Just know that it's sold separately.

The iPad Air, paired with the Magic Keyboard, avoids the Surface Pro 12’s kickstand issue. And with only an 11-inch screen, it’s perfect for use in small spaces—with or without the keyboard (note that there is also a 13-inch version). If you can accomplish all your work on an iPad, more power to ya! The iPad Air should give you everything you need, so long as you get the Magic Keyboard. If your travels involve more leisurely activities, the iPad Air is a great device for reading by the pool or for playing games.

One benefit of this latest version of the iPad Air is not only its performance (thanks to the M4 chip inside) but also the front-facing camera, which has been moved to landscape for more natural-feeling video calls. Of course, you could also opt for the 11-inch iPad Pro for extra performance (and the incredible tandem OLED screen). It’s also slightly thinner and lighter, though I don't think it's worth the significant price difference.

Lastly, there’s definitely room here for laptops with 360-degree hinges—a different class of two-in-one laptops. I haven’t tested a recent Windows device with this form factor that I truly loved, but there’s no question it works well on an airplane. Just fold the screen back with the keyboard face down, and you have the perfect screen for watching movies. The Lenovo Yoga 9i 14 is a gorgeous option, though it’s incredibly expensive right now. But if you can afford it, you’ll love the 2-in-1 action combined with one of the slickest designs imaginable. The Framework Laptop 12 is also worth considering. While it’s a bit thick and heavy for a travel laptop, its smaller screen, touchscreen, and 360-degree hinge make it a good option. It’s also one of the cheaper options available, and it’s user-upgradeable.

Whether you’re packing for a trip across the world or across the state, a proper travel laptop shouldn’t be heavy or overly large. That rules out 15-inch and 16-inch laptops, which are so big you can’t use them on airplane tray tables or at coffee shops. But even when you narrow it down to 14-inch laptops or smaller, there are three portability factors to consider.

When you’re on the go, you want a laptop that doesn’t constantly make you worry about finding an outlet. Bringing a laptop power bank can help, but the biggest factor in staying sane while traveling is a device that can stay on. Many factors affect how long a laptop can last, including CPU efficiency, battery wattage, screen brightness and resolution, and more.

Long-lasting laptops, such as the Dell XPS 13, will get you through a long flight. The processor is the main thing to look out for. Qualcomm’s Snapdragon X series of chips are battery-efficient, as are Apple’s M-series chips. Intel also has some highly efficient CPUs in its most recent lineup, though you’ll need to pay close attention to the model number, as it can vary wildly depending on the specific CPU you’re using. To dig deeper, read my extensive guide on How to Buy the Right Laptop, which breaks down different CPU options.

The size of seat-back tray tables varies. There’s no exact size because of various factors, such as the airline, aircraft, cabin, and seat. Remember: Just because a laptop can physically fit on a tray table doesn’t mean you’ll have space to open and use it. With that in mind, here are the general averages to keep in mind. Airlines do not list tray table sizes, but you can expect one in economy to measure around 16 x 9 inches, while premium cabins will have larger tables depending on the seat.

I’ve included both tablets and laptops in my recommendations, but your choice will depend on why you’re traveling with a device. If it’s for a vacation and you really only need a device to read, watch, and play on (perhaps with the occasional email check), a tablet works perfectly. Lounging with a tablet, whether on a plane or by the pool, feels much more leisurely than using a laptop. More importantly, it'll help you avoid dipping back into work mode. Whether an Android tablet, iPad, or Windows tablet, just pair it with a compatible keyboard case—and you’re good to go.

However, if you’re traveling for work (or need to work while traveling), a proper laptop will be better. The one you already have may work just fine, but frequent fliers know they prefer a more compact device. And more specifically, a small laptop with a keyboard and trackpad will work better on your lap or on small desks than a tablet.

Can Washington steal the NFC East crown? Hogs Haven readers weigh in after Thursday’s in over the Giants - poll results

📅 Nov 21, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Here's what Ben Standig had to say earlier this week:

The 2026 NFL regular season is past the halfway point, but the finish line remains too far away to make sweeping declarations about how the NFC East race will unfold.

Suggesting that the eventual division winner will have at least eight victories doesn't seem like a stretch, in theory.

There's a solid argument that the Washington Commanders are the best all-around team among the remaining contenders. (Step aside, Eagles.)

When Washington sat at 1-3 following a dismal overseas loss to Indianapolis in Week 4, talk of an NFC East title felt like pure fantasy. Yet six games later, the burgundy & gold have quietly stitched together a 3-2-1 stretch highlighted by Thursday night's 24-21 win over the New York Giants at MetLife Stadium that has breathed a sense of renewed optimism into at least part of the fan base.

Now at 3-5-1, Washington still occupies third place in the division, trailing New York by 2 games. Even so, the underlying progress is real. Quarterback Jayden Daniels engineered a clutch late drive last Thursday night — connecting with Stefon Diggs on a 32-yard touchdown with 1:05 remaining — while Washington converted 7 of 14 third downs and held New York to 4-of-12 on third down.

Moreover, over the past 6 games, the Commanders have largely protected the football — carrying a +7 turnover differential on the season while leaning on young contributors like tight end Ben Sinnott, wideout Antonio Williams, and cornerbacks Trey Amos and (the not-so-young) Amik Robertson.

In this week's Hogs Haven Reacts survey, we asked readers two questions to gauge the community's temperature entering Week 11. The first was our weekly benchmark: Are you confident in the overall direction of the team?

This week, 46% of respondents answered "Yes," while 54% voted "No".

That 32-point surge from last week's 14% approval mark mirrors the 21-point jump we saw in Week 9 after Washington beat the Eagles. Nothing cures midweek angst quite like beating a division rival in prime time!

Still, the fact that 54% of voters remain in the "No" camp shows that skepticism runs deep. As several commenters and fans have mentioned, Washington has yet to beat a clear playoff contender outside of Seattle in Week 3, and they let winnable games against the Colts and Giants slip away earlier in the fall. Until the Commanders can achieve sustained winning, a majority of the fan base is keeping its guard up, and the heat will stay turned up on head coach Dan Quinn and GM Adam Peters.

Our second question asked which team respondents believe will win the NFC East division title in 2026. Despite Washington sitting behind both New York and Dallas in the standings, the results were fairly decisive:

That 54% vote represents a dramatic reversal from earlier in the season, but it stems less from sudden perfection in Ashburn than from the vulnerabilities of Washington's rivals.

First, Washington owns the head-to-head tiebreaker over the Giants (5-3-1) after Thursday's win. Second, the schedule-makers did New York no favors. Over the final 8 weeks, the Giants must run a gauntlet that includes Jacksonville, San Francisco, Seattle, and Detroit, whereas Washington gets much softer matchups against Arizona, Tennessee, and Atlanta.

Meanwhile, Dallas (4-6) has been unable to win back-to-back games since November 28, 2025, and its defensive struggles reached a boiling point this week when head coach Brian Schottenheimer stripped first-year coordinator Christian Parker of play-calling duties in favor of senior defensive assistant Mike Pettine. To beat out Washington for the division title, the Cowboys will have to win as many times in their remaining 7 games as the Commanders win in their final 8. Don't forget that the Commanders still have a chance to inflict a loss on the Cowboys when they meet in Landover for the final game of the regular season in Week 18.

The math is straightforward: if Washington can match the win totals of both the Giants and Cowboys, the Commanders will be the division winner!

Washington holds the tiebreaker over the Giants, and, because of the Week 5 tie, matching Dallas in the win column would put Washington a half-game ahead of the silver & blue.

If, for example, the Commanders can win 5 of their final 8 games to finish 8-8-1, they would be division champs if the Giants go 3-5 and the Cowboys, who have only 7 games remaining, finish out the season 4-3. Of course, with 8 weeks to go, there are sure to be a lot of twists & turns before things are decided, but the Cowboys' games in Week 17 vs the Giants and Week 18 on the road against the Commanders could prove to be decisive in determining the ultimate division champ.

Readers in the survey article comment thread broke cleanly along familiar lines.

The optimistic Pollyanna camp — voiced by Savo_UK, Redskins21Life, and BillWard among others– pointed directly to the schedule imbalance. Their consensus is that if Washington takes care of business against Arizona, Tennessee, Atlanta, and Dallas (in Week 18), stealing just 1 upset against Cincinnati, Houston, Minnesota, or Jacksonville would be enough to reach 8 wins, which could be enough to claim the division in this 2026 season.

The skeptical Cassandra camp — led by theFan4Life, WillThomas, and Greengmork among others — warned against mistaking a single prime-time escape against the Giants for a turnaround. They argue that Washington's defense still struggles to generate consistent pressure, that the roster lacks the talent to beat playoff-caliber offenses, and that finishing around .500 risks trapping the franchise in draft-position purgatory without building a true contender.

I think the Commanders defense has looked completely different since the return of Nick Cross and Trey Amos to the lineup.

In the first 5 games of 2026, the Commanders surrendered an average of 29.8 points per game. Since Week 6 in San Francisco, when Amos and Cross took the field together for the first time, Washington has given up 18.5 points per game. While that stretch did include games against the struggling Eagles and last week's road trip to New York, it also included games against the Niners and Rams.

While passing yards surrendered since Week 6 are not dramatically improved (255 per game in Wks 1-5; 232 per game in Wks 6-10), the number of passing TOUCHDOWNS surrendered has changed dramatically, from 2.8 per game (14 total) in the first 5 weeks to 0.75 per game (3 total) in the four most recent games; the secondary has not allowed more than one passing touchdown in any of those contests.

Week 1 at Eagles (L 24–22): 182 net yds (203 gross), 3 TDs

Week 2 at Cowboys (L 37–20): 266 net yds (279 gross), 4 TDs

Week 3 vs. Seahawks (W 33–31): 393 net yds (393 gross), 4 TDs

Week 4 vs. Colts (L 30–13): 129 net yds (143 gross), 0 TDs

Week 5 vs. Giants (T 27–27): 305 net yds (326 gross), 3 TDs

Week 6 at 49ers (L 23–17): 249 net yds (264 gross), 1 TD

Week 8 vs. Eagles (W 27–10): 177 net yds (212 gross), 0 TDs

Week 9 vs. Rams (L 20–13): 219 net yds (228 gross), 1 TD

Week 10 at Giants (W 24–21): 283 net yds (293 gross), 1 TD

Anyone looking at the full-season stats for Washington's pass defense would question the Commanders' ability to limit the Bengals' high-powered attack this week. But a look at the most recent 4 games — which include playing against Matt Stafford & Puka Nacua as well as Brock Purdy and the Kyle Shanahan offense — might well bolster the confidence of Commanders fans with respect to improvements in Washington's pass defense since Cross and Amos took the field together.

If you only look at the scoreboard, it is easy to argue that Washington's offense is still spinning its wheels. To my eye, though, the underlying efficiency and rhythm of David Blough's unit have taken a noticeable step forward over the past month.

Across the four-game stretch straddling the Week 7 bye, the Commanders have pushed their average to 7.6 yards per pass attempt—a marked jump from the 4.8 to 6.3 yards per attempt they managed during the opening month—while completing 66.7 percent of their throws. Even in the Week 9 loss to Los Angeles, Jayden Daniels posted a 101.4 passer rating (24-of-35 for 253 yards) with explosive strikes of 42 yards to Stefon Diggs, 21 yards to Van Jefferson, and a 45-yard burst from Rachaad White, before following that up on Thursday night in New York by going 23-of-33 for 235 yards and three touchdowns in a 24–21 road win over the Giants.

That progress reflects a deliberate coaching emphasis on sharper situational execution; prior to Week 10, head coach Dan Quinn noted that the staff added extra practice repetitions specifically targeting red-zone spacing against man and zone looks so that "the decision-making process happens quickly" and the offense stops settling for field goals.

Just as encouraging has been the growing trust between Daniels and his first-time play-caller, paired with Daniels' emergence as the unit's vocal leader. There were plenty of justifiable questions early on about Blough's learning curve, yet Daniels emphasized ahead of the Giants game that their weekly game-planning loop is actively tailoring the playbook to his strengths: "I think just stuff I don't like throughout the week, he'll take out. Stuff I really do like, he'll call… If we got all 11 people going in the same direction, we have a chance."

After some early-season failures, that buy-in has shown up recently when it has mattered, most notably during Week 8's 27–10 win over Philadelphia, when Daniels gathered the offense during a sluggish 10–0 start—calling their opening series "complete BS"—and sparked 27 unanswered points.

Rookie wideout Antonio Williams credited Daniels' command in the huddle for uniting the group ("All the guys respect what he has to say, because we know the work he puts in"), while Quinn praised the quarterback's resilience in Thursday night's comeback after overcoming a turnover on downs and two interceptions to engineer the game-winning drive, saying, "To see us battle and stay in the fight, that's what I was looking for."

All in all, I see a team that struggled early and is still finding its feet, but one that is on the rise. The Commanders still haven't put together that full 60-minute game that was the hallmark of the 2024 season, but I believe that they're moving in that direction, and, with 8 games left to play, there's plenty of time to finish putting it together.

Meanwhile, in a different SB Nation poll this week:

Outside the DMV, barely one in six voters (15.7%) wanted a piece of Washington at +350—a stark contrast to the 54% of Hogs Haven readers projecting a division crown, and a gap the Commanders can start closing under the lights on Monday night.

The final 8-game push begins Monday night at Northwest Stadium against Joe Burrow and the AFC North-leading Cincinnati Bengals.

Anyone who watched the last regular-season meeting between these clubs on Monday Night Football in September 2024 remembers a clinic in offensive efficiency. Washington won 38-33 in a game that featured zero punts and zero turnovers — a feat the NFL hadn't seen since 1940.

If Washington wants to get the win again this Monday, the Commanders may need to be ready for another track meet. Burrow has Cincinnati rolling on a five-game winning streak, averaging 30.9 points per game behind Ja'Marr Chase and Tee Higgins. For Washington to pull the upset, pass rushers Dorance Armstrong, Odafe Oweh, and K'Lavon Chaisson will have to disrupt Burrow's timing while the secondary continues to improve, and the Daniels-led offense keeps pace on the scoreboard.

Moneyline: Bengals -270 / Commanders +220

Winning on Monday night is a tall order, although, with Arizona and Tennessee up next, even a loss wouldn't derail Washington's plan for a division title. Still, catching fire under the Monday night lights would likely turn the current cautious optimism into genuine belief across the DMV.

Harry Kane's Bayern blow: England captain ruled out for MONTHS after tearing ankle ligaments - but Ballon d'Or winner sends upbeat message from hospital

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Published: 17:58 EST, 20 November 2026 | Updated: 06:31 EST, 21 November 2026

Bayern Munich fear Harry Kane will be ruled out for months after suffering an ankle injury in their Bundesliga draw against Cologne - with the striker revealing he will only return for 'the business end of the season'.

Kane was forced off just 20 minutes into the match at the Allianz Arena following a challenge from Joel Schmied.

The defender had won the ball but caught Kane with his follow through on his ankle.

Kane had initially tried to play on after treatment, before eventually needing to be replaced.

The Ballon d'Or winner was upbeat on Saturday morning however, sharing a message with his fans that stressed his readiness to come back better than ever.

'The highs and lows of life!' Kane captioned a picture of himself wearing a protective boot. 'Obviously very disappointed to get this injury but as always it's an opportunity to show resilience and improvement going in 2027.

Harry Kane was forced off during Bayern Munich's draw against Cologne on Friday night

Bayern confirmed the England captain is set to be out for months following the injury

But despite his injury, Kane was upbeat as he spoke to fans for the first time on Saturday (Source: Harry Kane/X)

'I'll be back fresh and ready to go for the business end of the season!

'"Tough times don't last, tough people do" - my guy (Julian Edelman) told me this one!' Kane added, quoting the NFL icon.

'Thank you for all the messages! Alles gut (red heart emoji, strong arm emoji).'

Speaking post-match, Bayern sporting director Max Eberl revealed that Kane has suffered ligament damage.

A Bayern club statement confirmed the blow, reading: 'Harry Kane suffered a tear of the syndesmosis ligament in his left ankle in Bayern Munich’s Bundesliga home match against 1. FC Köln on Friday evening.

'This was confirmed by an examination by Bayern Munich’s medical department.

'The 33-year-old will therefore be out for the next few months. Get well soon Harry.'

Kane's absence will be a major blow for Bayern, with the Ballon d'Or winner having scored ten goals in 16 matches so far this season.

Bayern, who are already without Luis Diaz, Saibari and Serge Gnabry, also saw Michael Olise come off at half-time.

The German giants later confirmed Olise had suffered a 'contusion of the forefoot', with the Frenchman set to undergo treatment in the coming days.

It remains to be seen whether Kane - who became England's most-capped player last week when making his 126th appearance in the 2-1 win over Croatia - is at risk of missing the Nations League quarter-final against Norway in March.

Vincent Kompany, who was back in the dugout for the first time since his collapse last month, admitted other players will have to step up for Bayern in the absence of star names such as Kane.

'These are world-class players, you can't replace them, but we have the mentality and we have to bring that now,' Kompany said, as per Bild.

'We need incredible performances from players who may not yet be used to this pressure at FC Bayern.

Bayern were held to a 1-1 draw by Cologne on Vincent Kompany's return to the touchline

'We will all try together to make this situation a positive one for ourselves. FC Bayern does this time and time again, and that's what we want to do now too.'

Bayern were ultimately held to a 1-1 draw by Cologne, with Tottenham loanee Mikey Moore giving the visitors a shock first half lead at the Allianz Arena.

Nathaniel Brown drew Bayern level before half-time, but the hosts were unable to find a winner on Kompany's return.

The draw gives Borussia Dortmund the chance to move to the top of the Bundesliga table on Saturday.

‘He Walked So I Could Run’: ‘Project Hail Mary’ Breakout James Ortiz on Andy Serkis, Becoming Rocky and the Art of Puppetry

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Awards Circuit Podcast: The roundtable discusses “The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth” and the casting of Robert Pattinson as James Bond.

The reason so many of us cried at “Project Hail Mary” has five legs, no face and a voice that belongs to James Ortiz. And he’s so damn effective.

In April, Variety exclusively reported that Amazon MGM would submit Ortiz for best supporting actor at the 99th Academy Awards for his performance as Rocky. We’ve written plenty of consideration stories. None has produced what that one did: TikToks, Instagram Reels and a corner of film Twitter demanding, with real passion, that someone “get this adorable rock creature nominated for an Oscar immediately,” as Mike Sawicki of the “Mike, Mike and Oscar” YouTube channel and podcast put it. Related Stories Priyanka Chopra Jonas, Russell Crowe Sci-Fi Thriller 'Bluefly' Adds 'Beast' Star Daniel MacPherson (EXCLUSIVE) 'Beast in Me' Season 2 Casts Riley Keough, Lorelei Olivia Mote, Aaron Paul, Taip Ceman, Matthew James Thomas

The groundswell even made it to NBC’s “Today,” where Carson Daly spotlighted it.

Months later, sitting in the Variety Awards Circuit Podcast studio, Ortiz still sounds like he’s processing all of it. “Did this actually happen?” he says with a wince. “It feels like it happened to somebody else two years ago.”

On this episode, Ortiz talks about bringing Rocky to life through his puppet artistry, from the eight weeks his team spent turning subtext into movement to the hours of reference footage that guided Framestore’s animators. He also lays out what he wants from Hollywood when it comes to recognizing and protecting puppetry work, why Phil Lord and Chris Miller’s set felt like “putting on a play in their parents’ garage” and the “Batman Forever” obsession that shaped him as a kid.

Lord and Miller’s adaptation of Andy Weir’s novel opened in March to $80.5 million domestically, a record for Amazon MGM and went on to gross more than $680 million worldwide, the non-franchise phenomenon the theatrical business had been praying for. At its center, opposite Ryan Gosling’s Ryland Grace, sits Rocky, the Eridian engineer built by Neal Scanlan’s creature shop and performed on set by Ortiz and a team of puppeteers he assembled. He was hired to operate the character. The voice was supposed to come later, from someone more famous. At the 11th hour, it was his.

Born in Albany and raised in Texas, the Puerto Rican and Italian artist trained classically at Purchase College and found puppetry when the acting jobs didn’t come. His first agent told him he wouldn’t work until he was 40. He turned 40 a couple of months before cameras rolled. Onstage, he won an Obie for “The Woodsman,” designed the puppets for Lincoln Center’s “The Skin of Our Teeth” and created Milky White, the scene-stealing cow of the 2022 Broadway revival of “Into the Woods.” “Project Hail Mary” is his first film.

What strikes me is how plainly he describes the work as acting. He breaks down a script the way he once broke down Prospero and Oberon. The only difference, he says, is that he funnels those choices “through an external shell.” That’s why the conversation around him matters.

We’ve had versions of this debate before, with Frank Oz’s Yoda, Andy Serkis’ Gollum and Zoe Saldaña in “Avatar.” Ortiz is eligible for both the Oscars and the Actor Awards, where SAG-AFTRA covers puppeteers. In theater, he points out, puppeteers are most often classified as crew.

He isn’t campaigning for a trophy so much as for recognition of a craft. “The goal is to create a character that you get lost in,” he says. “But I think there’s value in talking about the folks that made that.”

Excerpts from the episode, edited and condensed for clarity, are below.

The way we approached making that thing, even though it was my first film ever, felt, from what I understand, quite avant-garde. In a funny way, it felt like I was doing some downtown play every day. But I left every day going, “I don’t really know what we just made.” There was such an emphasis on crafting and improvising on the take that there were so many different versions of every single second of that film. So two years of waiting was pretty brutal.

I was so pleased and surprised when I got to see the final thing, especially when, at the 11th hour, I was told I was going to be the voice. And then the studio and the team said, “Why don’t we talk about this?” That’s a really unusual thing for suit performers and mocap and puppeteers.

My goal was always to be an actor. I went through this classical training program at Purchase, and it was all Shakespeare and Chekhov, all the things I’m still really passionate about. Then it’s that funny thing: What do you do with that information? How do you sell cereal? How do you do a bite and smile?

The first agent I signed with, in my early 20s, the ink was literally still wet on the contract, said, “You know, you’re not going to work until you’re 40.” And I turned 40 a couple of months before we started shooting “Hail Mary.” Isn’t that nuts? So maybe he was right. Who cares?

I was always drawn to painting and drawing and sculpting. My father was always engineering and tinkering and building things in the house. My mother is an academic, really literary and taught me about story structure. Long story short, all of those elements go directly into puppetry. I loved acting, performing, directing, engineering and fine art and it felt like I gamed the system by finding something where I didn’t have to pick one. When the acting gigs weren’t coming in, one thing accidentally led to another. I said, “Yeah, I can do that for this wacky downtown show for $200″ and it kick-started this thing.

It’s become a way for me to play characters I would never, ever be cast as. That’s the thing I found about puppetry: It ends up being the most freeing form of acting because I don’t have to start from how you perceive me.

On any other role I’ve played, sans puppet, I would have ripped the script apart trying to figure out who this soul is. How do they think? How do they love? What are they terrified of? Then the exercise becomes how you funnel that through your human shell, physically and emotionally. The only difference with puppetry is that I’m funneling it through an external shell. I’m doing all of the homework.

My approach, maybe because I started as an actor, whether it was creating Milky White on Broadway or “The Skin of Our Teeth” for Lincoln Center or the operas I’m doing, was always: What do we want the audience to feel? The precision will come later. This is a tool to play a character that should not be played by a person. So let’s take advantage of it. Let’s not let it be pedestrian.

I was originally going to play the puppet entirely by myself on set. Then we saw the scale of it and how many legs he had. We knew that from the book, but seeing the puppet, I raised my hand and said, “Guys, I do a lot of ensemble puppetry and ensemble direction in my theatrical career. I would love to bring in other local puppet artists and build a team.” Not people who are just there to hold a leg. I needed to get them inside my head so we could play this character together.

For about eight weeks before we shot, we focused on how to use movement to express subtext. This is a character coming from a pretty traumatic 80 years alone in space. This character has never been alone before in their life. They’re a eusocial species, so they truly don’t know what it is to be solo. How does that existential pain manifest physically? That’s the delicious stuff. And I’m making it sound like we did a Bergman film. There was a lot of humor and silliness, but all of that has to come from something that feels grounded.

This might sound a little crazy, but there is no such thing, especially on film, as an actor’s performance being entirely their own. There are costume people and other artists collaborating with them to create this character. The way the DP shoots you, the way the editor crafts it, that creates the final character.

After we wrapped, I got to see a cut and Phil and Chris asked, “Do you have any thoughts?” I said, “Well, I do. Are you sure?” I sent about seven or eight pages of notes about who Rocky is and how he moves, so the animators had more information and Framestore implemented that.

When you’re getting a puppet on camera, you’re focused on the frame. But if they need to go back later and add a CG counterpart, what are they basing it off of? So I gave them hours and hours of me and my team with a smaller puppet covered in dots, playing full scenes without any breaks. It was a chance to throw in every good idea or any bad idea I had left that we wouldn’t have been able to shoot on the day.

My team and I had worked out little bits of funny business. There’s a moment when Rocky has an epiphany and runs from one place to the other, and he falls ass over teakettle down this ramp. That was something we did on the reference table. When he sits on the beach at the end, his little legs wrap around him like a cinnamon bun. One of my puppeteers said, “It’s so cute. I’m desperate to find a way to put this in there.” I went, “Yes, this is it.” And they did it.

It’s one of the few unions ever that recognizes puppetry as performance. Theatrically, there’s actually no puppet representation, union-wise. Most of the time, puppeteers are considered crew and that means a variety of other things.

Generally, when there’s on-set puppetry in a Star Wars movie or some big-budget genre thing, it’s been storyboarded within an inch of its life. The puppeteer is brought in: Here’s page five of scene six, he takes three steps and he turns. OK, that’s lunch. It’s much more technical. Phil and Chris and Ryan and all the producers were never interested in that. Working with Phil and Chris was such a, I’m trying not to curse, a friggin’ gift. It felt like being in a writers’ room, a fluid pitch meeting.

I don’t know if credit is the point, but I do think acknowledging the effort, acknowledging that C-3PO was actually Anthony Daniels, there’s something to that. The goal of all of this is to create a character that you completely believe, so you don’t think about the other things. But from an industry standpoint, there’s going to be more genre storytelling, more characters we currently cannot imagine. How do we keep having these conversations? I have to keep giving flowers to Amazon MGM because they said this was such an unusual way of working, so why don’t we talk about it?

He was such a touchstone for how we approached Rocky. I was of the age where I ate up all those “Lord of the Rings” docs and special features. There’s probably a two-hour doc just about Gollum. I logged it away years ago. He walked so I could run. I am standing on the shoulders of many giants. I did not invent anything. But sometimes it’s a huge gift that someone else came before you, and you can point at something and go, “Like this.”

It’s overwhelming. I can’t process any of it. Who knows if any of this conversation leads to more? I don’t even want to say awareness. It’s not like a disease. Get tested for rocks. But this art form has always been there. It’s always going to be there. Obviously, the goal is to create a character that you get lost in, but I think there’s value in talking about the folks that made that.

It’s a collaborative art form. There’s no version of it that happens by one person. Many singular people are on the poster at the end of the day, but that’s why the credits are so long. Every single one of those people bled and sweat and cried and probably got a divorce while they were making this thing.

As a kid, I was such a fan of “Batman Forever.” I was so nuts about Jim Carrey in that movie. It’s a great film, but there’s also a longer conversation there about the queer identity that I discovered in myself from that film, which is not rapid fire.

Oh, my God. Easy. I always run toward “Un-Break My Heart” by Toni Braxton, and occasionally, if we’re feeling frisky, I might do “Orange Colored Sky” by Natalie Cole.

“This Rock and This Man: What an Adventure!” It’s wordy, but I think it gets us there.

I can clap with one hand. That’s going to sound disgusting, I know. It’s pretty terrifying.

Probably my Apple Pencil, because I’m drawing all the time.

God, am I only going to talk about Batman? “Batman: The Animated Series.” It’s a show I still go back to all the time.

Honestly, the only thing that came to mind is Rocky.

The Minnesota Vikings have been dealing with a whole lot of injury issues recently, and now, another problem has surfaced before their Week 11 matchup with the San Francisco 49ers.

Vikings cornerback Isaiah Rodgers, who has developed into a crucial part of the team's secondary, is now questionable to play this Sunday as a result of a calf injury that limited him in practice both Thursday and Friday.

The development was revealed by Minnesota head coach Kevin O'Connell while speaking to reporters on Friday.

O'Connell certainly didn't seem overly pessimistic, but Rodgers' condition is obviously something to monitor heading into the Vikings' showdown in Mexico City.

Dec 25, 2025; Minneapolis, Minnesota, USA; Minnesota Vikings head coach Kevin O'Connell looks on in the second quarter against the Detroit Lions at U.S. Bank Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Matt Krohn-Imagn Images

The Vikings looked fantastic during Kirk Cousins' debut against the Green Bay Packers last Sunday, coming away with a 30-10 win at Lambeau Field and completing a regular-season sweep of their NFC North rivals.

Now, Minnesota will have a chance to send another strong message this weekend.

Minnesota acquired Cousins before the NFL trade deadline, bringing him back for his second stint in the Twin Cities. He initially played for the Vikings between 2018 and 2023, but failed to lead the Vikings to a Super Bowl appearance.

That could certainly change this year, as Minnesota boasts a dominant defense and a plethora of weapons offensively.

A victory over San Francisco would go a long way in proving that the Vikings are, in fact, for real.

Why 30 Ideas for Fixing U.S. Schools Missed the Plot

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 20, 2026 | Reviewed by Tyler Woods

In September 2026, the New York Times published an article titled “30 Ideas for Fixing US Schools, Ranked by Experts” to address the problem of declining literacy and math rates across the United States. The experts identified solutions based on effectiveness and relative cost, highlighting the top five solutions as small reading groups, science of reading, universal pre-K, increased funding, and increased teacher pay for under-resourced schools and communities.

To be clear, these ideas are backed by science and arguably would be welcomed policy changes for supporting U.S. students.

But even these interventions on their own will continue to fail students if we don’t address the bigger underlying concern: Schools were never designed for children’s development.

The model of our public education system was informed by an 18th-century Prussian military regime with the intended goal of preparing students for work in factories. The United States, deep into the Industrial Revolution, needed workers who could follow instructions, keep a schedule, and perform quality work. U.S. public schools were a solution to overcrowded, one-room schoolhouses and the need to support a new workforce.

It’s no wonder then that reform efforts date back literally to the inception of public education itself. While lots of positive changes have come from those efforts, including accommodations for students with disabilities and scientific advancements in how to educate, the problem persists: Schools don’t align with child development.

The research is clear on what works for children, especially young learners from preschool through eight years of age. Children learn best:

The harsh reality is that when developmentally appropriate practice is stripped from education, then all the best curricula in the world will have minimal effects, at best. The list of 30 best ideas for fixing U.S. public schools? That is phase 2.

We must begin with the root cause of the challenges we are seeing. The kids are letting us know in no uncertain terms that they are done with the 18th-century structure that wasn’t even designed for them in the first place.

Put child development experts at the helm. Reimagine public education from the ground up for kids, not in spite. When we let them learn from a developmental perspective, then we leverage the innate gifts and talents our children already have. They want to learn. They have the capacity to think deeply and creatively. The problem is that we have removed every ounce of developmentally appropriate learning from the schools. And then we wonder why it isn’t working.

Transform the environment. Bring in child development research to guide the way. That is where we begin. That is where we will find the solutions.

Mashburn, A. J., Justice, L. M., Downer, J. T., & Pianta, R. C. (2009). Peer effects on children’s language achievement during pre-kindergarten. Child development, 80(3), 686-702.

Mervosh, S., Paris, F., & Miller, C. C. (2026, September 28). 30 Ideas for Fixing U.S. Schools, Ranked by Experts. The New York Times. https://www.nytimes.com/section/education/2026/09/28/30-ideas-for-fixing-us-schools-ranked-by-experts

Petrigna, L., Thomas, E., Brusa, J., Rizzo, F., Scardina, A., Galassi, C., ... & Bellafiore, M. (2022). Does learning through movement improve academic performance in primary schoolchildren? A systematic review. Frontiers in pediatrics, 10, 841582.

Schmitt, M. B., Justice, L. M., & Logan, J. A. (2017). Intensity of language treatment: Contribution to children's language outcomes. International Journal of Language & Communication Disorders, 52(2), 155-167.

Mexico’s President Spent 21 Hours In Coach Getting To China—And Sat In A Middle Seat

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mexico’s President Claudia Sheinbaum spent about 21 hours getting to China in economy on China Southern Airlines. She reportedly sat in 33B. A middle seat, on a trip across the Pacific, to represent her country at a summit of world leaders. That’s quite a contrast with Donald Trump’s enthusiasm for accepting a Boeing 747 in royal configuration from Qatar. But while Sheinbaum is winning praise for saving taxpayers money, she should have flown business class. The 64 year old president traveled from Mexico City through Tijuana to Shenzhen for the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit. Here’s her departure update: Claudia Sheinbaum’s update on her trip to Shenzhen, November 15, 2026 21 Hours Includes The Stop In Tijuana She left Mexico City early Sunday, November 15, on China Southern flight CZ8032 and arrived in Shenzhen on Monday. The Airbus A350-900 made its scheduled technical stop in Tijuana along the way. The first flight took 3 hours 12 minutes and the transpacific segment clocked in at 14 hours 56 minutes, with about two and a half hours on the ground between them. China Southern operates the route nonstop from Shenzhen to Mexico City, with the Tijuana stop in the other direction. Several stories report that seat 33B is in the back of the aircraft. In fact, it’s the front of standard economy. On arrival she posted video of her welcome in Shenzhen. An airport bus brought her to the red carpet, where an honor guard and performers waited. She received a presidential welcome. Which reminds me a little bit of when I’ve booked first class awards on Lufthansa, but the intra-European segment was only available in coach. Nobody ever could quite figure out how a coach passenger had a vehicle waiting for them on the tarmac. She returned on China Southern flight CZ8031 and shared a photo with the crew: Claudia Sheinbaum thanks the crew of flight CZ8031 on her return from Shenzhen Mexico Sold Its Presidential 787 Sheinbaum is continuing a practice established by Andrés Manuel López Obrador. In July 2020, I wrote about Mexico’s president flying Delta coach and connecting in Atlanta to meet Trump. He had an exit row aisle seat on a Delta flight from Mexico City. Delta was blocking middle seats at the time, too, due to the pandemic. Reforma’s video of López Obrador boarding Delta for his Washington trip, July 7, 2020 López Obrador refused to use Mexico’s presidential Boeing 787-8. After years of trying to find a buyer, Mexico sold it to Tajikistan for about $92 million in 2023. He said the proceeds would fund two hospitals. This isn’t Sheinbaum’s first long trip in coach, either. In April I covered her economy trip to Europe, flying through Madrid to Barcelona and returning to Mexico City on Aeromexico. She posted a photo of herself working at her coach seat: Claudia Sheinbaum working during her April 2026 trip to Barcelona I thought that was performative then. A president’s time has value, and so does being rested for the work the country is sending her to do. Aeromexico business class would have provided better rest and opportunity to work. Pringles, Princes, And A Qatari 747 I’ve covered plenty of politicians and royals flying coach. Prince William flew American Airlines economy from Memphis to Dallas in 2014, in a bulkhead window seat on an Airbus A319. He’s also flown Ryanair and taken the family on Flybe, carrying their hand luggage. Prince Harry and Meghan Markle flew British Airways coach to Nice for New Year’s in 2017. They and three bodyguards occupied the last three rows, with empty seats around them. T Argentina’s Javier Milei flew coach on his trip to Davos in January 2024. Given Argentina’s finances and the spending cuts he wanted the public to accept, there was a political purpose for the symbolism. David Cameron was photographed eating Pringles and working on his iPad aboard easyJet in 2015, while traveling to Portugal. I liked that one. William and Harry’s grandfather Prince Philip, though, described the improvements in air travel over his lifetime with the qualification, “provided you don’t travel in something called economy class, which sounds ghastly.” Meanwhile, thhe Pentagon accepted Qatar’s Boeing 747-8 in May 2025 for Trump’s use as Air Force One. It was widely described as a $400 million gift. I covered the Qatari jet’s conversion this summer, including the decision to preserve its luxury interior and the plan for it eventually to go to Trump’s presidential library. The U.S. president needs secure communications, protection, and the ability to keep working while traveling. That can justify dedicated aircraft. Sheinbaum’s coach trip earned publicity, including favorable attention in China while she was there to meet its leaders. But saving the difference between coach and business class is simply a poor value tradeoff when the cost is productive time and sleep for someone with a high opportunity cost. NFL great Brady could buy Chelsea stake

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Tom Brady is a seven-time Super Bowl champion

American football great Tom Brady is looking to invest in Chelsea through ownership group Clearlake Capital.

The seven-time Super Bowl winner has recently divested his stake in Championship club Birmingham City, which he bought into following Tom Wagner's Knighthead Capital takeover in 2023.

It now appears he could move into Chelsea, where he has a positive relationship with influential co-owner Behdad Eghbali, having been a star guest at the after-party following the club's Club World Cup triumph in 2025.

Brady would have no voting rights or control at Chelsea, although the ownership group could look to appoint him to the board if it suited all parties.

The move is not said to be linked to Todd Boehly and Mark Walter's Â£950m sale of their stakes to Clearlake Capital, which must be completed by the end of the year.

Meanwhile, Swiss billionaire Hansjorg Wyss remains a significant minority stakeholder at Stamford Bridge.

The investment is also not part of a funding round, according to senior sources, and is not intended to dilute Clearlake Capital's control.

However, other interested parties are said to have approached them about investing since Boehly and Walter agreed a deal to leave.

Former quarterback Brady, a three-time NFL most valuable player, retired in February 2023 after 23 seasons in the NFL, having won six Super Bowl rings with the New England Patriots and one with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers.

Following the end of his playing career he acquired a stake in the Las Vegas Raiders, while also becoming part of the ownership group of the WNBA's Las Vegas Aces.

On top of that, Brady's commitments include him being a lead NFL analyst for Fox Sports.

Arsenal manager Mikel Arteta says he is very happy with Kai Havertz and trusts club chief Andrea Berta to sort out a new contract for him.

The Germany international has been a key player since joining the Gunners from Chelsea back in the summer of 2023, scoring 40 goals in 125 appearances for the club and helping them win the Premier League title.

Still, there has not yet been much of an update on Havertz and a new contract with Arsenal, with the 27-year-old’s current deal set to expire in 2028.

That’s not exactly imminent, but it would still be ideal for Arsenal to get Havertz tied down to a longer contract so as to avoid any uncertainty by the time he has just a year left by next summer, which would lead to his market value tumbling.

The Couple Created a Giant Sunflower Walkway For Their Multicultural Sun Valley Wedding

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Parker Selman and Karan Rai’s love story was born from a sunset. “Our paths crossed a few times before, but the first time we remember speaking was on a roof as we watched the sun go down our senior year at Tufts University,” Parker recalls. A few weeks later, they found themselves alone on a dance floor. “I looked down at my phone to see it was midnight and, therefore, officially my birthday,” Parker says. “I told him he should give me a kiss because it was my birthday, and he did. We were both smitten, and constantly in one another's orbit.” Despite the two lining up jobs on opposite coasts, they quickly realized being practical was not an option, and Karan managed to convince his company to transfer him to New York.

The couple got engaged on a crisp autumn walk around Governors Island—Parker’s favorite spot in the city. She owns and operates a wedding photography business, and as they strolled along, she asked to take a picture of Karan on her medium-format camera with their apartment building and skyline in the background. “While I was snapping the photo, he got down on one knee,” she says, “and I had the privilege of capturing my own proposal.” They rode the ferry back to Manhattan and went to Della’s, a local wine bar, where their families and friends were waiting to celebrate.

Two months later, she proposed back. “While we were walking in Sun Valley, I requested he take a picture of me sitting in the snow with our dog Grumpy,” she says. "I proposed the same way he proposed to me, the moment captured on my Rolleiflex.” Parker used diamonds and emeralds from her late great-grandmother’s engagement ring and gold from Karan’s late grandmother’s earrings to create his signet ring.

Their multi-day wedding unfolded in early September 2026 near Sun Valley, Idaho (where Parker’s family lives). They chose Central Idaho 4-H Camp, located 30 minutes outside of town, for its series of cabins and a large field up in the mountains. “The space was a blank canvas with no sound ordinance,” she says. “Studio Sully transformed the campsite into something magical.”

Going into the planning process, the couple knew they wanted the entire event to feel like a fusion of their cultures. In the days leading up to the Sun Valley celebration, Karan’s parents threw a series of traditional Indian wedding ceremonies and celebrations in his hometown of Pleasanton, California. Once guests arrived in Idaho, they all checked in at The Sun Valley Lodge, where each day the couple had organized group activities.

Festivities kicked off on Friday with a Western-themed welcome party at their favorite après-ski bar, Grumpy’s. Parker wore a tweed corset and skirt by Wiederhoeft accessorized with brown leather Manolo Blahniks and a wooden horse purse by Vin. When the weather cooled down, she tossed on a vintage suede fringe RRL jacket. Karan followed a similar script and wore a Bode suede fringe jacket and One of These Days pants with a painted horse and cowboy detail on the knees. His bandana was made by his mother. “She created a piece inspired by Marie Lichtenberg’s jeweled bandana, utilizing earrings and jewelry of Karan’s grandmothers, mother, and sister,” Parker says.

Guests were encouraged to wear textiles, paisley, and eclectic patterns to evoke a Wild West meets India atmosphere. In one corner, Boise Thread Studio set up shop to embroider initials onto trucker hats, shirts, and bandanas. Meanwhile, guests drank schooners (the name for Grumpy’s beer chalices) on the patio and ate burgers and hot dogs. “Karan and I named our beloved miniature dachshund Grumpy after the bar’s corn dogs, so it is only fitting that we ordered them until they ran out,” Parker says. Her mother welcomed the group with a sunset toast before moving inside. “We took over the electronic jukebox and closed down the restaurant until Karan had to stand up on the booth to usher everyone out the door.”

The next afternoon they hosted a sangeet by the Big Wood River. “The estate has a series of winding paths that lead to the riverside, and we wanted the afternoon portion to feel like a mountain garden party with activities and new discoveries tucked into each corner,” Parker says. The flowing river and live music by Boise-based Ashley Rose Band served as the soundtrack as guests played croquet, horseshoes, and badminton. Near the band, Henna by Sanjana created patterns on guests' hands.

Parker wore Danielle Frankel’s cream Mariela dress featuring two layers of hand-painted wildflowers. “I wanted a long braid with flowers woven in, and Jasmine Burnside helped bring the vision to life,” she says. Karan wore a light blue Jatin Malik sherwani with juttis from his personal collection.

As the sun set, guests were escorted to a clearing where a buffet was set up for dinner, and the dance floor was built around a large willow tree. The vignette was inspired by a 1905 Impressionist painting called Nighttime Festivities that depicts a turn-of-the-century party beneath the trees of a French palace. “I told Melissa [Sully] I wanted the sangeet to be based on this and we became obsessed with bringing the painting to life,” Parker explains. Lights and flowers were strung into the branches that draped overhead. Karan’s parents and Parker’s best friend, Ana, gave speeches before a series of traditional dances were performed by various family members and friends, followed by a lively dance floor courtesy of DJ Anupya.

The morning of the wedding, Parker and Karan got ready with their families and friends. “We are terrible at surprises, and he had seen my dress many times before, so I saw no purpose in a first look,” she says. The couple set off in a convertible (Parker’s hair protected in a vintage scarf from the year she was born), stopping at a vista for photos before arriving at the venue.

For her wedding look, Parker worked with Patricia Voto of One/Of to create a custom gown inspired by Christian Dior’s 1955 Soirée de Lahore. Early on, she knew she wanted something that felt like a combination of Karan and herself. “The original gown was made by the designer two years before his death and was based on a sari with the one-shoulder design and gold embroidery,” she explains. All of the embroidery was done in India, and Parker paired the gown with a custom One/Of veil featuring gold embroidery along the edge, as well as light green shoes by Paula Nadal.

Karan wore a custom Bode tuxedo with a golden sunflower detail, and as a boutonniere, he wore his own emerald kalgi (a traditional Indian headpiece for turbans). “His cufflinks and studs were my late grandfather’s, his cummerbund was unbranded vintage ’70s made in India with embellished sunflowers, and his shoes were suede Hermès.”

Guests arrived to the ceremony dressed in jewel tones and metallics, walking down a pathway with giant sunflowers on either side. Parker and her mother, an amateur florist, had collaborated with Madison of Hart Floral to create the dramatic entrance. “When I spoke of our love for sunflowers and our desire to have them serve as a recurring visual throughout the day, Madison and Melissa joined forces,” Parker explains.

For the ceremony, they stood within a chuppah structure with draped gold chainmail and burgundy sunflower motifs. Karan’s best friend Drew officiated the ceremony, and the fathers of her late friends Alex and Josh read an excerpt by Ovid of the Greek myth of Baucis and Philemon. “Karan’s vows and my own were mirrors of each other. Unknowingly, we made many of the same references, and we each ended by quoting the story of Baucis and Philemon, who, when they died, received the honor of becoming intertwined as trees so they could live on forever.”

Coming from mixed religious backgrounds (Karan’s parents are Sikh and Hindu and Parker’s are Episcopalian and Jewish), the couple chose to honor both. “Karan arrived on horseback as a nod to traditional Punjabi weddings. St. Christopher medallions were placed on each program, and my sister, Alison, shared a short speech about the patron saint of travelers, the namesake of the church we attended as children, and spoke about the new journey Karan and I were embarking upon,” Parker says. “My sister-in-law, Kareena, shared a speech about the jaimala ceremony as a nod to the Hindu wedding tradition, and spoke about the importance of seeing each other as equals.” Karan’s maternal uncle led the group in a Sikh Ardas prayer, and the ceremony ended with the Jewish tradition of the breaking of the glass. As the couple exited, guests threw dried bougainvillea from Karan’s family garden in Chandigarh.

Cocktail hour kicked off with oysters shipped from Island Creek Oyster Co. (the site of the couple’s first two dates). “We really wanted textiles to be an important decor motif throughout the celebration,” Parker says, noting that there was leveled seating overlaid with rugs, a rug-covered bar, and a rug-covered makeshift bandstand. Later on, their sisters shared speeches, and the couple danced to “Can’t Take My Eyes Off You,” then launching into “Dancing in the Moonlight” as tambourines were passed to guests. “Guests followed Karan and I through the field to reveal the tent, set among the cabins.”

The band continued to play as guests entered the tent and danced. At each seat, Parker and Karan placed a letter explaining what their friendship meant to the couple. After the buzz died down, Parker’s father opened the evening with a speech. “We then had a family-style dinner of our favorite bites: kale salad, focaccia, Idaho trout, pesto gnocchi, and vodka meatballs,” she says.

After dinner wrapped up, Parker and her father danced to “Wide Open Spaces” (a song they listened to as she was growing up) before everyone joined in. The couple snuck away to cut the cake—a 30-inch round flourless chocolate confection covered in raspberries—which was passed out on the outskirts of the dance floor. Indian sweets called ladoos were also passed around.

After a full week of wedding events between California for the traditional Indian celebration and four days in Sun Valley, the two were understandably exhausted. “It took weeks to recover, but the whole time all we could talk about was how deeply happy we were.”

Trump FCC declares California isn't allowed to enforce phone service rules

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

FCC preempts state rules that require AT&T to keep offering home phone service.

The Federal Communications Commission ordered California to stop enforcing its Carrier of Last Resort (COLR) rules against AT&T, setting up another battle between the Trump administration and California over the state’s right to enforce its own telecom laws and regulations.

AT&T’s status as a Carrier of Last Resort in California requires it to offer telephone service to any potential customer in its territory. AT&T wants to stop offering basic phone service to new customers and discontinue service for 184,000 residential customers and 15,000 business customers on June 1, 2027.

The order released today granted AT&T’s request to preempt state rules, following up on the FCC’s previous approval of AT&T’s petition to discontinue service. AT&T had tried to achieve the same result in court, but a federal judge rejected its request to override California’s rules in July.

The FCC order probably isn’t the last word, as California could sue the commission. California has stood alone against AT&T, which has received relief from COLR obligations in 20 of the 21 states in its wireline service territory.

“California’s Carrier of Last Resort framework may have made sense when it was established 30 years ago,” FCC Chairman Brendan Carr said in a statement released with today’s order. “But those rules were designed for a bygone era and no longer make sense in today’s competitive, advanced marketplace.”

If AT&T ends service on its old copper lines, many homes and businesses would not be offered modern fiber networks. AT&T claims a wireless home phone service that connects to the cellular network will be good enough, and the FCC agreed.

“The FCC has granted AT&T’s applications to replace legacy voice service with a modern, IP-based alternative in certain areas of the state. California’s state requirements are blocking AT&T from proceeding with a federally authorized transition,” Carr said.

California says its rules allow wireless replacements if they meet the state’s standards for basic phone service, but that AT&T must demonstrate that its wireless phone service would meet the COLR obligations. California’s requirements include flat-rate options for unlimited voice calls, discounted Lifeline rates for eligible low-income customers, and telephone relay service for people who are deaf and hard of hearing.

FCC Democrat Anna Gomez voted against the order, saying that state telecom commissions “are often the last line of defense for consumers. They know their communities, they take their complaints, and they answer to the people who rely on these services in a way that a federal agency in Washington cannot always match.”

Gomez said the California Public Utilities Commission (CPUC) has an open proceeding on how to modernize its COLR rules, and that the FCC should let that process play out. Large technology transitions work best “when the state regulator is a partner with a real role,” but the FCC is treating the CPUC “as an obstacle to be cleared,” she said.

The CPUC “proceeding is the right place for California to weigh what a modern basic service requirement should look like, with input from the people who will actually be making that switch,” Gomez said. “AT&T has said it does not plan to discontinue service before June 1, 2027, which leaves time for the CPUC to finish its work. Rather than wait, this item tells the CPUC to write its new consumer protections around a federal ruling it opposed.”

In March, the FCC issued an order that made it easier for carriers to discontinue copper networks. The order asserted that state rules are subject to preemption if they conflict with the FCC’s discontinuance authorizations and authority. AT&T cited the order in its lawsuit against California.

Trump officials and telecom companies have been trying to block California’s rules for years. The first Trump administration lost an attempt to preempt state net neutrality laws throughout the country in 2019, and California later defeated ISPs in a lawsuit that challenged its net neutrality law in court.

California’s net neutrality law and other telecom regulations are again being threatened by rules the Trump administration applied to federal grants, but California rejected US demands to stop enforcing its laws this month. In that case, California may have to choose between enforcing its telecom laws and receiving $1.42 billion to deploy broadband to 270,571 locations.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Education Department faces management challenges from recent changes, watchdog says

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An annual federal report cited interagency agreements and a handful of executive orders as key factors that will challenge the agency in 2027.

Since early 2025, the second Trump administration has outsourced many of the Education Department’s core functions such as grant management, monitoring and technical assistance to other agencies through interagency agreements. The department has also reorganized many of its offices and laid off a bulk of its staff.

"Change of this magnitude can result in challenges associated with employee resistance, fatigue, and uncertainty — such as decreased productivity, operational disruption, and lost efficiency — and have an impact on the organization’s ability to respond to stakeholder questions and concerns," the Nov. 13 report from OIG stated.

The Education Department has not yet issued an annual performance plan for fiscal year 2026 and has yet to issue its annual performance report for FY 2025, strategic plan for FY 2026-2030, or annual performance plan for FY 2027, according to the report.

Other reports through various federal offices have flagged similar concerns with the Education Department's output amid McMahon's efforts to close the agency, which ultimately requires Congressional approval.

In June, another OIG report found that layoffs at the Education Department appear to have impacted units that were performing legal duties.

The staffing changes were made to 16 of the department's 17 offices, resulting in an overall reduction of at least 40% of the more than 4,000 employees who were working for the Education Department when Trump reentered office in January 2025.

Many lawmakers, civil rights groups and lawyers have raised concerns with output from at least one of those offices, the Office for Civil Rights, which saw a slowdown in case resolutions that included no sexual harassment or assault cases being settled in 2025. A later report from the office of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., showed that OCR resolved only 1% of its cases overall in 2025, the lowest percentage from any OCR in over the last decade.

Responding to a draft of the most recent OIG report, the Education Department said it had made progress in many of the areas of concern, including change management. According to OIG, the department said it has "continued efforts to improve operational processes and maintain program delivery during a period of substantial organizational change." Additionally, the agency said it has "engaged in contractor support to assist with resolution for external audits," among other adjustments.

The department has previously maintained that its staffing and organizational changes will not affect the statutory functions it is legally obliged to perform.

The department also stressed in a comment to K-12 Dive that the OIG report is an annual update. In a January OIG report, the office flagged similar oversight challenges stemming from executive orders and resulting organizational changes.

"It is critical that the Department strategically plan for and effectively manage these and other major operational changes that it may decide to undertake," it warned at the time.

Past reports have flagged challenges under previous administrations, such as monitoring grantees' use of COVID-19 pandemic relief funding.
