Pfizer's Launched and Acquired Drugs Grew 32% in Q3 — and 3 Other Reasons to Buy the Stock Before 2027

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Pfizer remains deeply unloved on Wall Street, but it is slowly turning a corner.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 20, 2026 at 5:15PM EST

Pfizer (PFE -0.51%) faces a large patent cliff, and investors worry it won't be able to offset the revenue it loses as older drugs face generic competition. That's a problem, but the drugmaker is aware of it and is making important strides in its efforts to address the issue. The big story, however, is that this issue is pretty normal for a drug company.

In fact, revenue from recently launched and acquired drugs rose 32% in the third quarter of 2026. That shows the pharmaceutical giant's efforts are bearing fruit. But there's more reason to be excited, including the company's strong balance sheet, its large dividend, and management's commitment to continue paying that dividend. Here's what you need to know.

One of the biggest worries about Pfizer is its upcoming patent expirations. Two big ones are Eliquis in 2028 and Vyndamax in 2031. But patent expirations are a normal part of the pharma sector. And research and development efforts in the highly technical and intensely competitive healthcare sector don't always align perfectly with a company's patent expiration schedule. It's just a fact of life.

The 32% revenue growth Pfizer achieved from launched and acquired products is evidence that the company is seeing some success in its efforts to address the issue. And it sets up three more reasons to consider buying the stock.

Pfizer ended the third quarter of 2026 with $1.8 billion in cash and $12.4 billion in short-term investments. That gives the company significant firepower to support its business through this patent-expiration cycle. In fact, the company's cash hoard is slightly higher than at the start of the year, so it isn't rapidly burning through its savings.

Despite the company's results with new drugs and its financial strength, investors are deeply negative on Pfizer's stock. That's highlighted by its huge 6.1% dividend yield as of this writing. To put that yield into context, the S&P 500 index (^GSPC -3.70%) yields roughly 1.1%, and the average pharma stock yields 1.3%.

The negativity highlighted by the elevated yield is echoed by more traditional valuation metrics. Pfizer's price-to-sales and price-to-book ratios are below their five-year averages. And while the price-to-earnings ratio is elevated, it is largely due to one-time charges. Looking at the whole picture, Pfizer's stock looks attractively priced.

Given the lofty yield, Pfizer is likely to be most appealing to dividend-focused investors. But right now earnings aren't covering the dividend. That's not a great sign, but one-time charges are part of the issue. If you look at cash flow, the company can still support the dividend. That's highlighted by a cash dividend payout ratio of roughly 85%. While a lower number would be better, there's still some room for adversity. And don't forget about the company's large cash pile.

Both of these facts help support management's strong backing of the dividend. Indeed, one of Pfizer's key capital priorities is to "maintain and grow our dividend." Obviously, the company's priorities could change, but it wouldn't make a statement like that if the board of directors wasn't on board with protecting the dividend.

At the end of the day, Pfizer is facing very real headwinds today. But it is an industry-leading company with a long history of success. If history is any guide, it will muddle through this difficult period. The success it is having with new drugs, its financial strength, attractive valuation, and dedication to its large dividend are all strong reasons for investors to take a deep dive before 2026 is over. If you wait too long, the opportunity here may pass you by.

Russian strikes damage historic buildings in Kyiv and Odesa, including World Heritage sites

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

St Volodymyr’s Cathedral in Kyiv Photo by Wavemeart, via Wikimedia Commons

Multiple heritage sites in Ukraine were damaged by Russian strikes on 19 and 20 November, including the Unesco-listed St Michael’s Golden-Domed Cathedral and Monastery complex and St Volodymyr’s Cathedral in Kyiv, known for its frescoes, as well as 12 cultural heritage sites in Odesa, the strategic port city on the Black Sea, which was hit a day earlier. The entire historic centre of Odesa was added to Unesco’s list of endangered World Heritage sites in 2023.

At least nine people were killed in Kyiv and other regions of Ukraine during the hours-long attack on 20 November.

“Today, Russian drones damaged one of the most famous monuments of our country, the Volodymyr Cathedral,” Tetyana Berezhna, Ukraine’s deputy prime minister for humanitarian policy and minister of culture, wrote in a Facebook post on 19 November after the cathedral in Kyiv was hit, breaking windows and frames and causing “some damage to the paintings and structural elements”.

The neo-Byzantine St Volodymyr Cathedral, built in the 19th century, is under the jurisdiction of the Orthodox Church of Ukraine (OCU), which broke with the Moscow Patriarchate and was granted autocephaly by Constantinople in 2018. Three of Russia’s most famous artists of the 19th century and early 20th century—Viktor Vasnetsov, Mikhail Vrubel and Mikhail Nesterov—created religious art for St Volodymyr’s, as did the Ukrainian artist Mykola Pymomenko, a teacher of Kazimir Malevich.

The Austrian embassy, located next-door to St Volodymyr's Cathedral, was also damaged. Kyiv's municipal cultural heritage department published an inventory of damage from the attack, which also includes the embassy of the Netherlands.

A Russian drone was shot down over St Michael’s, which is the headquarters of the OCU, on 19 November.

Berezhna’s post cites the cumulative damage from Russia’s accelerating attacks: “Since the beginning of this year, the number of monuments damaged by Russia has increased by more than a third, and by the end of October it was almost 2,200. Almost 2,700 cultural institutions were also affected.”

The minister was in Lutsk, a historic city in northwestern Ukraine, this week to plan for a conference marking the 600th anniversary in 2029 of the Congress of Lutsk, which brought together the regional monarchies of the time. A Russian strike on Lutsk in October damaged Ukraine’s largest painting, Cosmogony, a 2,000 sq. m work by the contemporary artist Petro Antyp.

Unesco has condemned the latest attacks, especially on Odesa, “which hit the centre of the city, within the boundaries of the World Heritage property ‘The Historic Centre of Odesa’” and “reportedly caused damage to several heritage buildings, including the Kalio Palace, a monument of national importance,” according to a statement released by the organisation. The Kalio Palace, built in 1834, is attributed to the Swiss Italian architect Giorgio Torricelli.

The strikes came amid escalating tensions and a diplomatic chasm that appears to be widening. The Russian President Vladimir Putin said this week that the Kremlin would not negotiate any partial ceasefire or deal with Ukraine after it struck and incapacitated the Kerch Bridge linking mainland Russia to Crimea, a symbol of Russia’s annexation of the Black Sea peninsula in 2014, on 16 and 17 November. At the same time, US President Donald Trump claimed that peace, largely on Russia’s terms, is within reach and reportedly urged European leaders to stop funding weapons for Ukraine. Trump, Putin and the Chinese President Xi Jinping signed a “peace declaration” on 20 November at a trilateral summit hosted by Xi in Shenzhen, China.

A petition to “safeguard Ukraine’s cultural heritage and artistic integrity” was launched last month by the curator Konstantin Akinsha and Bjorn Geldof, the artistic director at Kyiv’s PinchukArtCentre. It calls “for immediate sanctions and the suspension of Russian state participation in international cultural events”. Rapporteur, a Brussels-based policy publication, reported this week that ten European countries are seeking to keep Russia out of the 2027 Architecture Biennale. Russia’s participation in the 2026 Venice Biennale set off a series of protests.

The bombing of several buildings including in the historic city centre—a World Heritage Site—has been strongly condemned by Unesco

The 11th-century World Heritage Site was damaged by a strike on Tuesday

A monastery in Kyiv was set ablaze by a missile strike in the latest offensive against the country, with residential buildings also targeted

The UN body is monitoring heritage sites by satellite and plans a meeting with Ukrainian museum directors to safeguard collections

TV Ratings: CMA Awards Score Seven-Year High

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The show delivers its biggest audience of the 2020s so far.

The 60th CMA Awards delivered their largest TV audience of the decade.

The Nov. 18 awards averaged 7.62 million viewers on ABC and Disney+, the best same-day total for the awards since 2019 (when it brought in more than 11 million viewers). Wednesday’s show finished just ahead of the 7.57 million viewers for the 2022 awards — with the caveat that Nielsen has made several changes to the way it measures ratings in the years since then.

The CMA Awards drew a 1.01 rating among adults 18-49 (equivalent to about 1.37 million people in the demographic), the first time the show has cleared a 1.0 rating since 2022. The telecast grew by 26 percent in viewers and 55 percent in adults 18-49 from a year ago (6.04 million viewers, 0.65 rating in the 18-49 demo). Related Stories TV Streaming Ratings: Rough Week for Original Series, 'From' Remains No. 1 Overall Music Taylor Swift Performs "Our Song" and "I Knew It, I Knew You" at the CMAs

Ella Langley set a record with six wins at the awards, including entertainer of the year. Her long-running No. 1 hit “Choosin’ Texas” won CMA Awards for song, single and music video of the year.

Along with Langley’s big night, the CMA Awards featured a performance by Taylor Swift — her first at the ceremony since 2013 — and a tribute to Dolly Parton that featured Vince Gill and host Lainey Wilson performing a duet of “I Will Always Love You.” Several other artists paid tribute to Parton during the show and on the red carpet beforehand.

ABC’s annual Countdown to the CMAs special on Tuesday also earned multi-year ratings highs. Hosted by Robin Roberts, the show drew just under 3 million viewers, its best showing since 2023.

The Venice Biennale's Sham Awards Cannot Artwash Genocide

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This year’s historic actions demonstrated that Israel’s unspeakable assault on Palestine will not be ignored — and that the power of boycott has never been greater.

This Sunday, the Venice Biennale will award its new “Visitors’ Lions.” But no matter who receives this poisoned chalice, the results are void.

Stripped of the silver and gold they wore in previous iterations of the Biennale, the new Lions have been thrown to the tourists; “Best Artist” and “Best National Pavilion” will be decided by a popularity contest. Why this surprising democratic turn? The expert jury initially appointed to give these honors resigned en masse in April of this year. These resignations were, in part, the result of the Art Not Genocide Alliance’s (ANGA) nearly three years of campaigning to exclude the Israel pavilion from the art world’s most important biennial. After the jury publicly stated their intention to omit Israel and Russia from consideration for the Golden Lions, they were visited by representatives from Italy’s Ministry of Culture. The contents of the meeting are not public, but we can surmise how it went from the jury’s subsequent unanimous resignation. Shortly after, the Venice Biennale Foundation announced the Visitors’ Lions, inviting ticket-holders to cast their vote for awards to be announced at the end of the show’s run.

“Our exclusion has been thwarted, and the choice now passes to the general public,” crowed Israeli Foreign Minister Gideon Sa’ar, whom human rights groups accuse of orchestrating the siege of Kamal Adwan Hospital and the notorious imprisonment and torture of the hospital’s director, Dr. Hussam Abu Safiya, in 2024. Firmly siding with torture and against principle, the Biennale made it clear that both Israel and Russia were back in the running for the Visitors’ Lions. As Antonia Majaca observed, the jury dared to apply a criterion, and the Biennale responded by dissolving judgment itself.

Many artists have rejected this sham from the start. On the day the 2026 Biennale opened to the public, 54 artists in the international exhibition and 22 national pavilion teams withdrew from consideration in solidarity with the jury. In June, more than a hundred artists threatened legal action unless their names were removed from the ballot. Sunday’s ceremony awards a prize that few want to win.

The massive public pushback against the Biennale also demonstrates how close we have come to actualizing the moral principle that a genocidal, apartheid state has no legitimacy on any international stage. This year, we participated in a series of unprecedented protests. Biennale artists and cultural workers went on strike three times in seven months — nothing on this scale has ever happened before (not even during the anti-capitalist protests of 1968, which put a stop to the Biennale’s prizes for 18 years). This year’s protests were also unprecedented in terms of the level of collaboration between ANGA — which comprises artists, curators, and precariously employed workers — and rank-and-file labor unions.

The Biennale is resolutely global, unfolding in the “anywhere or not at all” of the international art circuit. Against the false globalization of the art market, our internationalism was grounded in local organizing against Israel’s Zionist genocide.

The first of this year’s strikes, on May 8, was built locally in Venice, with Biennalocene, Sale Docks, and other local grassroots unions whose participation provided legal protection for striking art workers. On May 8, 30 of the Biennale’s 100 national pavilions closed partially or fully, and thousands of people marched from Via Garibaldi to the Arsenale, where they were met with police brutality. Many artists in the main exhibition draped or altered their works.

The second strike, on June 12, was national in scale. It was the result of a year of organizing assemblies and actions in about 20 cities all around Italy, with organizations including Mi Riconosci?, Vogliamo Tutt’Altro, Biennalocene, and grassroots unions through the Cultural Strike platform.

The third strike, on 30 October, responded to a call that emerged from outside the art world from the World Federation of Trade Unions and, in Italy, was taken up by militant dockworkers, including the Autonomous Collective of Port Workers, and the grassroots Unione Sindacale di Base. A network of port unions at 113 in 22 countries, including Italy, France, Greece, Turkey, and Morocco, refused to load weapons bound for Israel. This was the biggest ever coordinated action of its kind, and our role was to help build local participation in Venice.

Today, November 20, as this piece is being published, the International Student Strike brought further strike action to Venice, demonstrating the political convergence between students and the Biennale — students in the city are often employed in the cultural sector under precarious conditions.

Our ability to do this grew out of the work we began in 2024, in adherence to the guidelines of the Palestinian Campaign for the Academic and Cultural Boycott of Israel (PACBI) and Boycott, Divestment, and Sanctions (BDS), when our open letter demanding Israel’s exclusion from the Biennale was signed by more than 24,000 cultural workers. That year, the Israel pavilion closed.

Between 2024 and 2026, our campaign connected more substantively with the Italian labor movement. As the June 12 strike articulated, the political atomization of artists is a labor issue, related to the art world’s reliance on precarity, which shifts the risk of political action onto individuals. The Biennale provides no fees or production money to artists, leaving them to fundraise their participation. Those whose work is not sufficiently market-friendly to claw back payment in future sales have to content themselves with being paid in the false currency of prestige.

ANGA refuses the oppressive logic that allows the Biennale to support genocide and profit from precarious labor while claiming to rise above politics. This year, we have made clear that the alliance we have created has the power to disrupt the Biennale’s functioning and change its nature.

Today, the contradictions of the present political order — the indigestible, white-supremacy-coded cocktail of Europe’s simultaneous opposition to Russia and support for Israel; the flailing, ever-dying economy; the oceans of money poured into technologies of death and destruction — explode messily into the supposedly morally hermetic market-world of the Biennale.

The farcical “Visitors’ Lions” are the Biennale’s wager that the walls can always be painted pure white again. But this year’s massive actions have demonstrated that we will not allow Israel’s unspeakable assault on Palestine to be art-washed and ignored. ANGA’s members and supporters are no less steadfast than we were three years ago, when our campaign first began. It remains obvious and undeniable that Israel, the United States-backed abattoir-state, must not be allowed to bring its logic of butchery to be art-washed into neutral “complexity” at the Biennale. And we are now considerably more organized and better connected to the wider struggle against Zionism.

When we look back to ANGA’s beginnings, in early 2024, we remember a time when the shock of total Zionist impunity was still raw enough to feel unreal. We could not imagine that friends or colleagues could disagree with this basic principle. We were soon disabused, as many cultural workers washed their hands of blood in service of their careers.

But our collective efforts have transformed this anti-political status quo. The new compositions of struggle made possible by the mass revolt against Zionism offer a model for how the art world can reach beyond the isolated precarity of cultural labor into new alliances — with students, with dockworkers. The power of boycott as a tool has never been greater. We continue to remake the Venice Biennale, the modern world exhibition, from Walter Benjamin’s “place of pilgrimage to the commodity fetish” into a site of struggle.

Liverpool are starting to catch onto the trend. They have lost one French player in Ibrahima Konate but gained three this summer.

The 16 Most Expensive Artworks Ever Sold at Auction

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By Daniel Cassady, Alex Greenberger November 20, 2026 5:00pm

Auction records make for great headlines, like the 2026 banner sale of Gustav Klimt’s Portrait of Elisabeth Lederer (1914–16), which topped $236.4 million at Sotheby’s. There’s a thrill in watching numbers climb into the stratosphere, each sale another reminder that, in the art market, money has a way of bending reality. But the price of a painting has never been the most interesting thing about it.

Look at the list of the most expensive artworks ever sold at auction, and you’ll see more than just a parade of billionaires with good taste (or at least expensive taste). You’ll see a history of ideas—what mattered to the artists who made them, what obsessed the collectors who chased them, and how we decide, decades or centuries later, which works still hold power.

Yes, Salvator Mundi sold for $450 million, but is it really a Leonardo? Picasso’s Women of Algiers, Pollock’s Number 7A, 1948, and Warhol’s Shot Sage Blue Marilyn all shattered records, with the Pollock work shooting far beyond its $100 million estimate when it came to auction at Christie’s in May 2026 in a sale of works from the S. I. Newhouse collection. But these works’ real value has nothing to do with what someone was willing to pay on a given evening at Christie’s. The best art—the kind that shifts culture, lingers in your mind, and changes how you see the world—exists in a space money can’t touch.

But of course, once a work crosses a threshold, there’s something almost inevitable about the price spiral. With each record-breaking sale, the bar gets set higher, the stakes get sharper, and the frenzy deepens. The game has changed, but in a way, the auction houses are just as much a stage for the spectacle as for the art itself. Every price tag opens the door to another possibility. If one work can sell for $450 million, what’s to stop the next from reaching half a billion—or more? With so many players in the game, all vying for the ultimate cultural trophy, the numbers have a way of multiplying faster than the logic behind them.

And yet, the more those numbers rise, the further they drift from the essential thing that makes art so valuable: the art itself. High prices can make us think we’re in the presence of something transcendent, but it’s the work that holds the power, not the price. So, here’s the list. The numbers are staggering, but don’t get too caught up in them. The real story is in the art itself.

A version of this article was first published on November 19, 2025. It was last updated on November 20, 2026.

Sold for $119.9 million at Sotheby’s New York in May 2012

When it sold for $107 million at Sotheby’s New York, Munch’s pastel The Scream (1895), one of the four versions—two painted and two in pastel—that he made of the same subject, became the most expensive work ever to be sold at a public auction, stealing the trophy from Picasso’s 1932 Nude, Green Leaves and Bust, which sold at Christie’s in 2010 for $106.5 million.

At the time, it was the only version of The Scream still in private hands; its seller was Petter Olsen, a Norwegian businessman whose father, Thomas, was Munch’s friend and patron. The picture, which opened at $40 million, sparked a 12-minute bidding war, with wide-eyed potential buyers both in the salesroom and on the phones. According to the Wall Street Journal, its buyer was investor Leon Black, a trustee at the Museum of Modern Art, where The Scream later went on view.

After the sale Olsen, described by Art in America as “the wild-haired and bespectacled seller,” read a statement that tied the work to climate change and global warming: “The Scream for me shows the horrifying moment when man realizes his impact on nature and the irreversible changes that he has initiated, making the planet increasingly uninhabitable. . . . More death, less life. And very few lifeboats left as we go down.”

Sold for $121 million at Christie’s New York in November 2024

The “crown jewel” of the auction market in 2024 (as Christie’s so humbly put it) was perhaps the finest example of a subject that Magritte revisited more than 25 times throughout his career: an otherworldly scene showing a house on tree-lined street at night. But instead being enveloped in darkness, the sky above the trees is impossibly blue, with big, cottony clouds floating by. This 1954 version of Empire of Light, which sold for $121 million and came from the collection of designer and philanthropist Mica Ertegun, set a new record for Magritte’s work at auction.

After the sale, Christie’s chairman of 20th and 21st century art Alex Rotter said the house that evening had adopted a “masterpiece approach” to this sale. “In a market that is not so easy to maneuver, we thought that if we present the greatest works we can get … these are the best examples,” Rotter said, admitting that the auction had its share of lots that didn’t catch collectors’ attention. But “the works that we put all the emphasis behind really proved us right. They had multiple bidders and showed that a market based on individual taste is on the rise.”

Sold for $137.7 million at Christie’s New York in November 2022

Cézanne’s deliciously rich landscape of the French countryside and his beloved Mont Sainte-Victoire is the second of five works from the Paul Allen sale at Christie’s to make this list. When the hammer fell, Mont Sainte-Victoire (1888–90) doubled Cézanne’s previous auction high of $60.5 million, for a still life from the collection of John Hay Whitney.

Like Cézanne’s best works, Mont Sainte-Victoire breaks aspects of the world—in this case, a French mountain—into shapes shaded with color, as though they were three-dimensional geometrical forms. In that way, Cézanne paved the way for Cubist artists such as Pablo Picasso and George Braque, who would push his innovations even further toward abstraction.

Sold for $139.3 million at Sotheby’s New York in November 2023

Woman with a Watch (1932), one of Picasso’s many paintings of his mistress Marie-Thérèse Walter, became the second most expensive work by the artist ever sold at auction when it fetched $139 million at Sotheby’s in 2023. The canvas, from the collection of late philanthropist Emily Fisher Landau, drew intense bidding before selling to a phone bidder represented by Sotheby’s head of global fine art, Brooke Lampley.

Woman with a Watch had a rarefied provenance, changing hands just a few times since Picasso painted it in 1932. It was acquired by Landau in 1968 from Pace Gallery, which had purchased it from Switzerland’s Galerie Beyeler two years earlier.

Pictures of Walter typically go for top dollar. In 2010, Nude, Green Leaves and Bust—also painted in 1932—broke records at $106.5 million. Still, the most expensive Picasso of all time is a work that does not depict Walter. But more on that in a bit.

Sold for $140.8 million at Poly International Auction Co., Ltd., Beijing in December 2017

Qi Baishi’s Twelve Screens of Landscapes (1925) shattered records in 2017, selling for RMB 931.5 million ($140.8 million) at Beijing Poly Auction and making it the most expensive Chinese artwork ever sold at auction. The bidding war lasted over 20 minutes, with more than 60 bid increments before a Chinese collector secured the 12-panel piece.

Each 70-inch panel captures a sweeping vista from Qi’s travels across China. Originally displayed at his solo exhibition in 1954 and later featured in a memorial show in 1958, the set of panels was kept by Qi’s pupil, Xiuyi Guo, before resurfacing at auction.

A similar version of Twelve Screens of Landscapes, painted around 1932 as a gift to Kuomintang general Wang Zuanxu, resides in the Chongqing Museum. The set sold in 2017 was the only one in private hands at the time, making it a rarity.

Sold for $141.3 million at Christie’s New York in May 2015

Alberto Giacometti’s Pointing Man (1947) became the most expensive sculpture ever sold at auction when it fetched $141.3 million at Christie’s New York in May 2015. The life-size bronze, standing at more than five and a half feet tall, was sold by real estate magnate Sheldon Solow, surpassing Giacometti’s previous auction record of $104.3 million, which was set at Sotheby’s London five years earlier by Walking Man I (1960).

One of six casts made by the artist (not counting an additional artist’s proof), this particular Pointing Man had been held privately for 45 years before the sale. Four of those casts reside in major museums, including the Museum of Modern Art in New York and the Tate in London, which made this auction a prime opportunity for collectors. Similar sculptures by Giacometti depicting skeletal, elongated figures have likewise sold for big sums.

At the time, Christie’s head Pylkkänen called the piece “one of the finest works of art I have had the honor to handle.” Whoever bought the piece clearly agreed about its quality.

Sold for $142.4 million at Christie’s New York in November 2013

Francis Bacon’s Three Studies of Lucian Freud (1969) briefly became the most expensive artwork ever sold at auction when it sold for $142.4 million at Christie’s New York in November 2013. The triptych surpassed the previous record, held by Edvard Munch’s pastel The Scream, which sold for $119.9 million at Sotheby’s in 2012 (and which also appears on this list, above).

Bacon’s three-panel portrait—each part stands six and a half feet tall—depicts Bacon’s longtime friend and artistic rival, Lucian Freud, in a series of contorted poses. The piece had an unusual history: its panels were separated by a dealer in the 1970s and remained apart for 15 years before being reunited by a collector in 1989.

The work was expected to sell for over $85 million, but after a five-minute bidding war, it was secured by New York’s Acquavella Galleries. In a strategic move, Christie’s shifted the Bacon painting to the ninth lot of the evening to allow unsuccessful bidders a chance at other works—an approach that proved successful, contributing to the auction house’s record-breaking $691.6 million total for the night.

Shortly after the sale, the anonymous buyer lent the triptych to the Portland Art Museum for public viewing from December 2013 to March 2014.

Sold for $149 million at Christie’s New York in November 2022

In November 2022, an early 20th-century masterpiece by Georges Seurat, Models (Small Version) (1888), sold for a staggering $149 million at Christie’s New York. The painting, depicting three nude figures in Seurat’s signature pointillist style, was one of the rarest privately-owned works by the artist. It came from the collection of Microsoft co-founder Paul Allen and was expected to surpass $100 million at auction.

The bidding opened at $75 million, drawing competition from four bidders, including specialists representing clients in New York and Asia. After several minutes of heated back-and-forth, the work hammered at $130 million, with Christie’s Asia chairman securing the piece for a final total of $149 million, including fees. The sale set a new auction record for Seurat, shattering his previous high of $34 million, achieved in 2018 for The Harbor at Grandcamp(1885).

The last time Models (Small Version) appeared on the market was in 1970, when it fetched just over $1 million at Christie’s. Prior to Allen’s ownership, it had belonged to the influential collector John Quinn, a champion of modernist artists in the early 20th century.

Sold for $157.2 million at Sotheby’s New York in May 2018

Amedeo Modigliani’s Reclining Nude (on Her Left Side) (1917), one of the artist’s most celebrated nudes, sold for $157.2 million at Sotheby’s New York in 2018. The total price, including the buyer’s premium, followed a hammer bid of $139 million, which fell just short of its undisclosed estimate of $150 million. Despite this, the sale set a record for the most expensive artwork ever sold at Sotheby’s at the time.

The large-scale canvas—depicting a nude woman gazing over her shoulder at the viewer—was the star lot of the auction house’s Impressionist and Modern art sale. Bidding opened at $125 million with auctioneer Helena Newman presiding over the room. The winning bid came from a client on the phone with Simon Shaw, Sotheby’s co-head of Impressionist and Modern art worldwide, after a subdued and measured exchange.

The work belongs to Modigliani’s groundbreaking series of reclining nudes, painted between 1916 and 1919. When first exhibited in 1917 at Parisian dealer Berthe Weill’s gallery, these erotic paintings caused such a scandal that police shut down the show after just two days. At the time, only two drawings were sold.

While the sale price was extraordinary, it did not surpass Modigliani’s auction record, which remains with Reclining Nude (1917–18), sold at Christie’s in 2015 for $170.4 million to Chinese collector Liu Yiqian. Sotheby’s, however, positioned Reclining Nude (on Her Left Side) as a transformative work, with Simon Shaw stating, “There is the nude before Modigliani, and there is the nude after Modigliani.”

Sold for $170.4 million at Christie’s New York in November 2015

Amedeo Modigliani’s Reclining Nude (1917–18) shattered expectations at Christie’s New York in 2015, selling for $170.4 million, more than doubling the artist’s previous auction record of $70.7 million. The work, which carried an on-request estimate of over $100 million, became the second-most expensive painting ever sold at auction at the time.

The sale took place during Christie’s curated evening auction, “The Artist’s Muse,” which aimed to build buzz around 20th-century masterpieces. Despite a mixed night—with several major lots, including works by Willem de Kooning and Lucian Freud, failing to sell—the Modigliani nude electrified the room. Bidding was fierce, ultimately concluding with a Chinese collector securing the work via telephone, as revealed by Pylkkänen after the sale. (The collector was later revealed to be Chinese billionaire businessman Liu Yiqian.)

Reclining Nude is part of Modigliani’s celebrated series of reclining nudes, painted between 1916 and 1919. These works, revolutionary in their sensuality and bold in their approach to form, scandalized Paris when first exhibited at Berthe Weill’s gallery in 1917, leading to the show’s abrupt closure by police. Today, they are considered among the most important contributions to the modern nude tradition.

The sale of Reclining Nude was a high point in an otherwise uneven auction, which totaled $491.4 million—above its $440 million low estimate but falling far short of the $705.8 million brought in by a similar sale earlier that year. While other records were set that night—including for works by Gustave Courbet, Balthus, and Roy Lichtenstein—the Modigliani painting stood out as the undeniable star of the evening, solidifying the artist’s status as one of the most sought-after names in the art market.

Sold for $179.4 million at Christie’s New York in May 2015

Pablo Picasso’s Women of Algiers (Version ‘O’) (1955) set a new auction record in 2015 when it sold for $179.4 million at Christie’s New York, becoming the most expensive painting ever sold at auction at the time. The painting, the final and most intricate piece in Picasso’s 15-work “Women of Algiers” series (1954–55), had an on-request estimate of around $140 million but soared past that figure after deep bidding.

The competition for the masterpiece played out entirely over the phone, with then-Christie’s executives Loic Gouzer and Brett Gorvy representing rival bidders. Pylkkänen, serving as auctioneer, declared “Still many people in the game” as bidding climbed past $140 million. Eventually, Gorvy’s bidder emerged victorious with a hammer price of $160 million, drawing cheers from the audience.

The “Women of Algiers” series was inspired by Eugène Delacroix’s 1834 masterpiece of the same name. Created in response to the Nationalist uprising in Algeria against French colonial rule, the series reflects Picasso’s fascination with both European Orientalist art and the evolving political landscape of the time. The version here is considered the most elaborate and fully realized of the set.

The painting had a notable provenance, originally acquired by New York collectors Victor and Sally Ganz directly from legendary dealer Daniel-Henry Kahnweiler in 1956. It first appeared at auction in 1997, when it sold for $31.9 million—meaning its value increased nearly sixfold in less than two decades.

While Christie’s maintained confidentiality regarding the winning bidder, reports later suggested that the buyer was former Qatari Prime Minister Hamad bin Jassim bin Jaber Al Thani. The sale reinforced Picasso’s dominance in the art market, cementing Women of Algiers (Version ‘O’) as one of the defining auction moments of the decade.

Sold for $181.2 million at Christie’s New York in May 2026

This 11-foot-wide painting by Jackson Pollock came to auction at Christie’s with both a first-class provenance and remarkable historical cachet. Pollock painted the work in 1948, the year after he first devised his drip painting technique, in which the artist flung his paint at the canvas rather than applying it directly using his brush. The technique distanced Pollock’s hand from his painting, leaving the application of his paint to chance.

Before he owned Sotheby’s, businessman A. Alfred Taubman owned the painting, selling it to publishing magnate S. I. Newhouse in 2000. In 2026, nearly a decade after Newhouse’s death, the painting headed to sale at Christie’s with a $100 million estimate, poising it to break Pollock’s record. At the time of the Christie’s auction, the painting hadn’t been seen publicly since 1977.

The work ended up shooting far beyond its estimate and becoming one of the most expensive paintings ever sold. Bidding opened at $80 million, and following 60 bids, the painting hammered at $157 million. With fees, the total rose to $181.2 million.

On November 18, 2026, Vincent van Gogh’s Blossoming Chestnut Trees (Châtaigniers en fleurs) (1890), a picture of sunlit trees set against oncoming storm clouds, sold for $190.4 million (including fees) at Sotheby’s. The cover lot for the auction house’s Blaquier-Arrieta collection sale, which the house had billed as “legendary,” it set a new auction record for the Dutch painter. The reported buyer was dealer William Acquavella.

The artist’s previous record of $117.2 million was set at Christie’s in November 2022 when Orchard with Cypresses (1888), a landscape from the collection of Microsoft cofounder Paul Allen, sold for $102 million. The new result beat that by $73.2 million, a jump of 62.4 percent in just four years.

That the sale happened at all was a victory for Sotheby’s. Just days before the auction, descendants of Samuel Fischer, the German Jewish publisher who once owned the painting, challenged its sale, arguing that their family had been forced to part with it in Amsterdam in 1936 because of Nazi persecution. Sotheby’s disputed the claim, pointing to archival records showing the painting had sold for 68,000 Swiss francs, around $22,000 at the time, which it considered a fair market price. After several days of talks, the Blaquier-Arrieta family and the Fischer heirs reached an agreement, clearing the painting’s title.

Painted shortly after Van Gogh arrived at Auvers-sur-Oise in May 1890, Blossoming Chestnut Trees is among the 70-plus paintings (and more than 50 drawings) that the artist made there before taking his own life only two months later. It was also one of only eight Auvers landscapes still in private hands, according to research cited by the Art Newspaper.

The electric scene, with its thick brushwork, also hadn’t been seen in decades. It was last exhibited at the Solomon R. Guggenheim Museum in 1964 before Argentine collectors Carlos Pedro and Nelly Arrieta Blaquier, bought it in 1975—the kind of provenance and scarcity that fuels record-breaking prices like this one.—Daniel Cassady

Sold for $195 million at Christie’s New York in May 2022

Andy Warhol’s Shot Sage Blue Marilyn, one of the most iconic images in modern art, became the most expensive 20th-century artwork ever sold at auction when it fetched $195 million at Christie’s New York in 2022. The silkscreen portrait, based on a publicity still of Marilyn Monroe from the 1953 film Niagara, is part of Warhol’s famous “Shot Marilyn” series—named after a 1964 incident when performance artist Dorothy Podber fired a gun at a stack of Warhol’s canvases.

The work was the star lot of the evening, drawing competition from four bidders, including three on the phone and one in the room. Art dealer Larry Gagosian, bidding in person, ultimately secured the painting with a hammer price of $170 million, well below its $200 million estimate.

The “Shot Marilyn” works are among Warhol’s most coveted pieces, with only five versions in existence. This particular portrait had been in a private collection for five decades, coming from the estate of Swiss dealers Thomas and Doris Ammann. With no financial guarantee, its sale marked a major moment for both the Warhol market and the broader contemporary art market.

In surpassing the $179.4 million record set by Picasso’s Women of Algiers (Version ‘O’) (1955) in 2015, Shot Sage Blue Marilyn reaffirmed Warhol’s place at the pinnacle of modern art valuations. While some experts saw the sale as a watershed moment, others noted that privately brokered transactions for blue-chip artworks have long exceeded the $200 million mark.

Gustav Klimt’s Portrait of Elisabeth Lederer (1914–16) became the second-most expensive artwork ever sold at auction when it achieved $236.4 million at Sotheby’s in November 2025, surpassing Klimt’s previous records and setting a new high for any work of modern art. The full-length portrait, one of only two named Klimt commissions in private hands, was the undisputed centerpiece of the sale of the Leonard A. Lauder Collection.

Bidding opened at $130 million and unfolded over 20 tense minutes, narrowing to two phone bidders represented by Sotheby’s specialists David Galperin and Julian Dawes. Dawes’s bidder ultimately won with a hammer price of $205 million, prompting applause in the salesroom and marking the most expensive lot ever handled by Sotheby’s. The result vaulted past Picasso’s Les Femmes d’Alger (Version ‘O’), which had held the record for a modern artwork at $179.4 million since 2015. Andy Warhol’s Shot Sage Blue Marilyn, meanwhile, made global headlines when it skirted the $200 million mark in 2022.

The painting was commissioned by one of Klimt’s most important patron families, survived wartime confiscation, and was restituted in 1948 before entering Lauder’s collection in the 1980s. Estimated in excess of $150 million, it had been widely expected to challenge the artist’s previous high. Instead, it rewrote the record books—cementing Klimt as one of the few early-modern painters consistently capable of commanding nine-figure prices in today’s market, and potentially opening the door for even more masterpieces to break the $200 million barrier.

Sold for $450.3 million at Christie’s New York in November 2017

In a landmark moment in the art world, Leonardo da Vinci’s Salvator Mundi (c. 1500) set a new record for the most expensive artwork ever sold at auction, fetching an astonishing $450.3 million at Christie’s in New York in November 2017. The sale, which took place in a packed salesroom at Christie’s Rockefeller Center headquarters, was driven by an intense 19-minute bidding war.

Bidding began in earnest at $100 million—which was also the guarantee—and escalated rapidly from there, with cheers and applause breaking out as the painting crossed the $200 million threshold. Pylkkanen, again serving as auctioneer, skillfully kept the crowd engaged as bids climbed incrementally in the final moments. At one point, the bidding reached $350 million, before finally settling at $400 million, with an additional $50 million in fees for the buyer.

The Salvator Mundi’s journey to the auction block was filled with intrigue, including debates over its authenticity and a controversial restoration by conservator Dianne Modestini. Despite these uncertainties, the painting’s remarkable backstory and its connection to Leonardo da Vinci—whether authentic or not—captivated buyers. The work had once sold for just $1,175 in 2005, when it was attributed to a follower of Leonardo, but was later restored and re-attributed to the master himself.

The sale marked a culmination of a carefully orchestrated marketing campaign by Christie’s, which included a global tour and celebrity endorsements, such as Patti Smith and Leonardo DiCaprio. The Salvator Mundi’s new auction price far eclipsed the previous record-holder, Pablo Picasso’s Women of Algiers (Version O) (1955), which had sold for $179 million earlier that year.

This sale marked not only a record-breaking moment for the auction house but also highlighted the increasingly influential role of market value in the art world, where the price tag may overshadow the painting’s historical and artistic significance.

The buyer of Salvator Mundi was revealed a month after to the sale to be Saudi Arabian crown prince Mohammed Bin Salman, with quiet plans for it to eventually go on view in Riyadh.

Trump Says His Private Pilot Used To Use Foreign Air Traffic Control Because The American System Is So Bad

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Donald Trump says his private pilot used another country’s air traffic control because the American system was so bad. He wouldn’t identify the country, as though his pilot had discovered a better provider for approaches into Teterboro and didn’t want everyone else signing up. Pilots don’t get that choice. And this isn’t just an ambiguous reference to flying overseas. Trump has made this claim before and described landing in New York, Florida and Tennessee. At his Friday press conference, Trump said his “pilot would use the air traffic control from a totally different country.” This was supposedly before his first term, 11 or 12 years ago. Then he declared, “I know a lot about air traffic control.” Watch Trump describe his pilot using another country’s air traffic control — Aaron Rupar (@atrupar), November 20, 2026 Air traffic control assigns clearances and separates aircraft sharing the same airspace. The controller responsible for your flight changes as you move between sectors and facilities. Your pilot can request a different route or altitude. They can reject an unsafe instruction. They can’t decide to use a different country’s air traffic control service because they like it better. And there’s a clear division of responsibilities. Pilots fly the aircraft and request changes to clearances when needed. Controllers coordinate the traffic. Could There Be A Real Story Behind This? I’ve tried to come up with something plausible, to create the most generous interpretation of these remarks. The best I can do is that Trump is confusing navigation equipment with air traffic control. Perhaps his pilot told him about a preferred navigation system, approach procedure or a piece of equipment he liked from overseas, and that became a story about choosing another country’s controllers. There are also real circumstances where an American flight uses foreign air traffic services: Crossing the North Atlantic. A flight to Scotland could pass through airspace handled by Nav Canada’s Gander operation and Britain’s Shanwick operation. Taking a route through Canada. A pilot could request a route that avoids congested U.S. sectors and spends more time in Canadian airspace (but it doesn’t give Canadian controllers responsibility for a landing in Tennessee). Flying in U.S. airspace assigned to Canadian controllers. “American airspace means American controllers” isn’t always true. Under a 1963 agreement, the United States delegates control of certain portions of its airspace within 50 nautical miles of the Canadian border. But the government assigned that responsibility, the pilot doesn’t choose their preferred controller. None of this would reconcile his comments with what he claimed on May 8, 2025. So, if I’m landing in New York or Florida or Tennessee, they’re using a system from another country. He repeated the story on June 27, 2025, too. So this is a recurring explanation of how he thinks flying works. The U.S. System Really Does Need Fixing I’ve been writing about the FAA’s antiquated technology and failed modernization efforts for years. Last week’s nationwide ground stop followed a contractor’s router installation that disrupted communications and radar information across much of the country. I’ve also pushed back when Trump claimed credit for the 2017 aviation safety record, when he blamed diversity hiring after the Washington crash without evidence, and when he suggested departing air traffic controllers could be quickly replaced. Training qualified controllers takes years. In fact, I supported the direction of Trump’s 2017 air traffic control proposal. He wanted to move operations into a separate nonprofit, with the FAA retaining safety regulation. A Canadian-style organization could finance upgrades without depending on annual congressional appropriations, and the FAA would stop regulating its own service. This year I challenged FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford’s attempt to rewrite that history as though airlines had hijacked Trump’s modernization plan and turned it into a debate over spinning off the system. Trump had announced the separate nonprofit himself. Maybe he think it actually existed, before he was President. Pilots don’t get that choice. And this isn’t just an ambiguous reference to flying overseas. Trump has made this claim before and described landing in New York, Florida and Tennessee.

At his Friday press conference, Trump said his “pilot would use the air traffic control from a totally different country.” This was supposedly before his first term, 11 or 12 years ago. Then he declared, “I know a lot about air traffic control.”

Watch Trump describe his pilot using another country’s air traffic control — Aaron Rupar (@atrupar), November 20, 2026

Air traffic control assigns clearances and separates aircraft sharing the same airspace. The controller responsible for your flight changes as you move between sectors and facilities.

Your pilot can request a different route or altitude. They can reject an unsafe instruction. They can’t decide to use a different country’s air traffic control service because they like it better. And there’s a clear division of responsibilities.

Pilots fly the aircraft and request changes to clearances when needed. Controllers coordinate the traffic. Could There Be A Real Story Behind This? I’ve tried to come up with something plausible, to create the most generous interpretation of these remarks. The best I can do is that Trump is confusing navigation equipment with air traffic control. Perhaps his pilot told him about a preferred navigation system, approach procedure or a piece of equipment he liked from overseas, and that became a story about choosing another country’s controllers. There are also real circumstances where an American flight uses foreign air traffic services: Crossing the North Atlantic. A flight to Scotland could pass through airspace handled by Nav Canada’s Gander operation and Britain’s Shanwick operation. Taking a route through Canada. A pilot could request a route that avoids congested U.S. sectors and spends more time in Canadian airspace (but it doesn’t give Canadian controllers responsibility for a landing in Tennessee). Flying in U.S. airspace assigned to Canadian controllers. “American airspace means American controllers” isn’t always true. Under a 1963 agreement, the United States delegates control of certain portions of its airspace within 50 nautical miles of the Canadian border. But the government assigned that responsibility, the pilot doesn’t choose their preferred controller. None of this would reconcile his comments with what he claimed on May 8, 2025. So, if I’m landing in New York or Florida or Tennessee, they’re using a system from another country. He repeated the story on June 27, 2025, too. So this is a recurring explanation of how he thinks flying works. The U.S. System Really Does Need Fixing I’ve been writing about the FAA’s antiquated technology and failed modernization efforts for years. Last week’s nationwide ground stop followed a contractor’s router installation that disrupted communications and radar information across much of the country. I’ve also pushed back when Trump claimed credit for the 2017 aviation safety record, when he blamed diversity hiring after the Washington crash without evidence, and when he suggested departing air traffic controllers could be quickly replaced. Training qualified controllers takes years. In fact, I supported the direction of Trump’s 2017 air traffic control proposal. He wanted to move operations into a separate nonprofit, with the FAA retaining safety regulation. A Canadian-style organization could finance upgrades without depending on annual congressional appropriations, and the FAA would stop regulating its own service. This year I challenged FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford’s attempt to rewrite that history as though airlines had hijacked Trump’s modernization plan and turned it into a debate over spinning off the system. Trump had announced the separate nonprofit himself. Maybe he think it actually existed, before he was President. I’ve been writing about the FAA’s antiquated technology and failed modernization efforts for years. Last week’s nationwide ground stop followed a contractor’s router installation that disrupted communications and radar information across much of the country.

I’ve also pushed back when Trump claimed credit for the 2017 aviation safety record, when he blamed diversity hiring after the Washington crash without evidence, and when he suggested departing air traffic controllers could be quickly replaced. Training qualified controllers takes years.

In fact, I supported the direction of Trump’s 2017 air traffic control proposal. He wanted to move operations into a separate nonprofit, with the FAA retaining safety regulation. A Canadian-style organization could finance upgrades without depending on annual congressional appropriations, and the FAA would stop regulating its own service.

This year I challenged FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford’s attempt to rewrite that history as though airlines had hijacked Trump’s modernization plan and turned it into a debate over spinning off the system. Trump had announced the separate nonprofit himself. Maybe he think it actually existed, before he was President.

One fund manager says the continued uncertainty makes Nvidia vulnerable to more selling, even as the stock has erased $1.16 trillion in market value in the past seven trading sessions.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/cms-issues-medicare-advantage-proposals-aimed-boosting-competition" hreflang="en">CMS issues Medicare Advantage proposals aimed at boosting competition</a>

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
CMS issues Medicare Advantage proposals aimed at boosting competition" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration has proposed a slew of changes to Medicare Advantage and Part D designed to "revitalize care" in these markets.

For one, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services said late Friday that it will update application and network adequacy requirements to make it easier for new entrants. The agency proposed to require organizations to demonstrate network adequacy during their first year in operation, rather than during the application process.

This shift would eliminate a significant barrier for new entrants, driving greater competition in the market.

CMS has also proposed combining three specialty areas in behavioral health — clinical psychology, clinical social work and outpatient behavioral health — into one specialty group for the purposes of network adequacy. The changes will give plans greater flexibility in building out behavioral health networks.

CMS will also make adjustments that seek to boost competition for special needs plans.

In addition, the agency said it will make adjustments to the star ratings program, focusing its ratings on 15 measures. The proposed measure set would emphasize "clinical outcomes and patient experience while retaining select process measures in high-priority areas where performance remains low."

CMS is also aiming to update the calculation methodology that will "raise the bar for achieving high ratings," and thus quality bonus payments.

"People with Medicare should be able to choose coverage based on what matters most to them — quality, affordability, and access," said CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., in the announcement. "These proposals are about creating a Medicare Advantage marketplace that rewards better care and better value while empowering beneficiaries with the options and knowledge they need to make informed decisions about their health."

The proposed rule also includes provisions that align with Department of Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy's Make America Healthy Again agenda by setting a "healthy food" standard that MA plans should follow for supplemental benefits.

The goal, CMS said, is to ensure that Medicare dollars are spent on food benefits that align with MAHA.

"Medicare food benefits should pay for nutritious food that helps people improve their health and manage chronic disease," Kennedy said in the announcement. "This proposal would require those benefits to meet the Dietary Guidelines for Americans while preserving access to foods and nutritional products that providers recommend for patients’ specific medical needs."

"Under President Trump, we are putting prevention at the center of health care and demanding more from every Medicare dollar," Kennedy said.

Old-School Whitetail Wisdom for Filling Your Tag After Opening Day

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Early Black Friday deals are here — Shop hunting gear, fishing tackle, optics, and more!

Published Nov 20, 2026 5:30 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

These days, a buck can’t grow to trophy proportions on hard-hunted public lands by folding up under the pressure exerted by the opening-day crush of hunters. A whitetail lives the 3½ years necessary to grow a bragging set of antlers by learning to cope with the annual influx of hunters during the season’s first days. Every opening day that the buck survives increases his chance of surviving the ensuing openers, as well.

Some big bucks live in locations that allow them to avoid the opening weekend crowd by slipping into the sanctuary offered by state parks, game refuges or private posted land. Others crawl into the most inaccessible and gosh-awful cover their “territory” has to offer — the kind of place hunters walk around, rather than through — and stay there until the crowds leave. And on more than one occasion, I have found a bruiser buck in a tiny, secluded patch of isolated cover — the kind of place most hunters never think to look in.

But the whitetail is a homebody at heart, and he is most comfortable on his loosely defined home turf. You can bet that once the steady crack of opening day rifles slackens to only an occasional pop, those big bucks will be right back to business as usual. And that is the precise reason why your best chance for bagging a wall-hanger occurs after the opening day madness has subsided.

Where you begin your search for that after-the-opener buck hinges upon one factor: the rut. Many states plan their firearms seasons to coincide with the rut, offering their hunters every opportunity to harvest the surplus bucks. Other states, bound in tradition, open the season after the main rut has ended. If you are planning to travel out of your home state to hunt whitetails, it makes sense to hunt a state where the season coincides with the rut, all other criteria being equal.

A mature whitetail buck is a different character during the rut than he is after it. A buck’s sole purpose at that time is to procreate, and this he does with obvious enthusiasm and the stamina of a teenager. There are stories abounding in every deer camp in the country of the crazy antics performed by bucks under the spell of the “rutting moon,” such as lovesick bucks strolling across open fields in broad daylight. Others tell tales of bucks putting their noses to does’ tracks and following those tracks, even after being shot at and missed several times. Rut-crazed bucks, while under the influence of a powerful rush of hormones, have even been known to either pick fights with or attempt to mate with assorted farm livestock.

So rampant are the stories that I’m afraid there exists a whole contingent of deer hunters laboring under the false assumption that all a hunter needs do to down one of these crazy buggers is to walk into the woods, lean up against a stump and wait for one of the poor, sex-addled dummies to saunter on by. Although such tactics work just often enough to keep the myth perpetuated, plenty of hunters have been sitting against stumps for more unsuccessful years than they will probably admit, and they’re still waiting for a buck to wander by.

Hunting whitetails during the rut and after the opening weekend of the season isn’t easy. You must employ all of the skill and woods savvy you have. Any breaks you receive because of a buck’s overactive glands should be considered a bonus.

After the opener, I refine my hunting strategy for big bucks by determining what stage the rut is in. This is important because the stage of the rut will determine where I concentrate most of my midweek hunting efforts.

The whitetail buck is ready to breed long before the first doe enters estrus. During this period, the buck runs off excess energy by beating up saplings, sparring with bushes and, on occasion, duking it out with other bucks. In the process of making rubs on bushes and trees, the buck deposits scents that tell the rest of the whitetail community who was there.

Scrapes begin to show up in increasing numbers as the level of testosterone builds in the buck’s system. The buck paws out scrapes and leaves his scent both in the bare earth of the scrapes and on overhanging branches above the scrapes. Again, this is a means of communicating with other whitetails. The message to does is that the buck is now standing at stud, and it tells other bucks that they had best move along if they know what’s good for them.

A buck will paw out most of his scrapes in areas where they are most likely to be seen or smelled by does. Openings in woods, old logging roads and the edges of woodlots are common places to encounter scrapes. A buck may make a dozen or more scrapes, and he’ll check some of them daily, making sure they are free of debris and liberally doused with musk/urine. These are the scrapes to hunt over.

However, once the does begin cycling into heat, the dominant bucks that handle the bulk of the breeding chores are often so busy accompanying, tracking and servicing does that they neither have the time nor the need to freshen their scrapes. But if you find a string of scrapes that have not been freshened for several days, it doesn’t necessarily mean that the rut is over or that the buck that made the scrapes is hanging from someone else’s game pole. It may mean that the buck is out doing his job.

The best way to determine what stage the rut is in is to spend a day or two in the woods prior to the hunt. If fresh scrapes abound, you will be hunting the pre-coital period, and a stand near a scrape line is going to be hard to beat. If, on the other hand, you find that more than half of the scrapes are old and abandoned, it’s a good bet that the big bucks are out taking care of the does, while the lesser bucks are still hanging around their scrapes, waiting.

If you just don’t have time to get into the woods prior to the season, you can get a good idea about the stage of the rut by speaking with area game managers, milk haulers, rural school bus drivers, postal carriers, loggers, trappers, farmers, grouse hunters, bowhunters or others who spend most of their time in deer country. If the bucks are chasing the does, chances are these people will have observed some of the activity.

Why all of the fuss over determining what stage the rut is in? Because it makes a big difference when you have to decide how you want to hunt after the opening weekend of the season.

When the season begins, if the actual breeding season has not yet begun but bucks are busy pawing out new scrapes and freshening old ones, chances are the bucks will curtail this activity during the high-pressure days at the beginning of the season, resuming their rounds once the hunters thin out. I like to have at least a half-dozen scrape lines pinpointed prior to the opener, and I check them after the opening weekend for any sign of activity. I then concentrate my hunting efforts near the “hottest” scrapes.

If your pre-season scouting and snooping indicates that the actual breeding season is in progress, then hunting over scrapes may not be your best option for tagging a really good buck. Remember, it is often the big, dominant bucks in the area that handle the bulk of the breeding chores. Even in an area with an excellent buck-to-doe ratio, a breeding buck can be kept so busy that he has little time or need to tend his scrapes. Often, a buck will spend 24 hours with the same doe, breeding her several times before her cycle ends. At this time, it is the doe, not the buck, that determines where the pair will travel. Though I would not totally rule out hunting in the vicinity of inactive scrapes at this stage of the rut, there are other options that I’ve found give me a better chance at tagging a big buck.

One of my favorite methods during this period is to concentrate my hunting efforts in areas where I have encountered good numbers of does in the past. Does and fawns tend to hang around together, and they routinely congregate in specific areas, often bedding as a loosely knit group in a favorite swale or sunny hillside. I can’t direct you to these “ladies-only bedrooms,” but I’ll bet there is such a place in the chunk of woods you hunt.

You can locate these places that hold special favor with the does by simply noting those areas where you encounter the most doe activity. After a few years of hunting the same ground, a familiar pattern becomes apparent.

On several occasions, I have located a hotbed of doe activity by simply listening to other hunters. When you hear a hunter complain that he sat on his stand and saw 15 does but not a single buck, find out where he was sitting. You can bet that when the time is right, there will be a buck or two sniffing around the vicinity.

A dominant buck ranges well out of his home area during the actual breeding season. I don’t think he goes out of his way looking for does so much as he is drawn out of the territory when following the tracks of a doe that has visited his scrapes or just wandered through the area. A buck engaged in this activity moves with a single-minded purpose, shuffling along, nose to the ground, seldom looking up, intent upon finding the maker of the tracks he is following. I once missed a buck that trailed a doe past my tree stand in this manner, my arrow slicing harmlessly over the buck’s back only to skitter noisily through the carpet of dry oak leaves a few yards beyond. The buck lifted his head and looked in the direction of the commotion. Apparently satisfied that whatever had made the racket was not what he was looking for, he then ambled on his way while I frantically fumbled around trying to nock another arrow.

When a buck finally tracks down a doe, as often as not, she will then lead the buck on a little chase that, in all likelihood, will take the buck still farther from his home base. By the time the buck actually mates with the doe, he may be miles from his original starting point. On the way back to his core area, it is not unlikely that the buck will cut the track of another “hot” doe and repeat the scenario.

All of this rambling makes it impossible to pattern a buck during the height of the breeding season, prompting many knowledgeable whitetail hunters to claim that taking a good buck during the peak of the rut is mostly a matter of luck. I disagree.

Hunting near a known “doe hangout” can help. And you can stack the deck in your favor even more by hunting a funnel area near a concentration of does. A funnel is simply any natural or man-made feature of the terrain that restricts the movement of deer. An hourglass-shaped section of woods is the classic funnel. Deer moving from one wide portion of the hourglass to the other must pass through the narrow waist. In farm country, the funnel may be a brushy fenceline connecting two woodlots. In the rugged country of the West, where whitetails are becoming increasingly common, a jutting jaw of sheer rimrock can force whitetails into a confined passage. Funnels work well because even though a buck with a snootful of “hot” doe scent wouldn’t care if he was standing in the middle of Madison Avenue, the does do not lose their heads. Does will stick to cover when on the move, even when there is a grunting, panting Romeo dogging their every move.

In many whitetail states, hunters are left with no choice but to hunt well after the main rutting period is over. At first, hunting after the opening weekend under these circumstances appears to be a futile endeavor. Without the promise of the rut to keep the bucks on the move, and with the absence of hunters to jar deer loose, what chance does the midweek hunter have of scoring on a decent buck? He has a pretty good one, I would say.

First, although the bulk of the breeding chores are completed in about a two-week period, there is still some activity after the main rut is over. Does that were not impregnated will cycle back into heat 28 days after their first estrus cycle ends. In some states, this is just about the time the rifle season rolls around. In addition, in some of the best whitetail areas, it is not uncommon for female fawns of the year to breed. This often applies to as many as 40 percent of them. Frequently, these young breeders experience their first estrus cycle later than older does. So, don’t strike out the possibility of the rut working in your favor even though the peak has passed. In that part of Minnesota where I live, the bulk of the breeding is completed by mid-November, yet each year, I find active scrapes late into November and even December. Last season, during an especially mild December, I found a pair of bushel-basket-size recently freshened scrapes while hunting grouse on New Year’s Eve day. If you can find these “late scrapes,” concentrate your hunting efforts near them.

But even if you fail to find any of these scrapes later in the year, you still stand a good chance of tagging a buck during the middle of the week if you let the after-effects of the rut work in your favor. During the rut, a buck takes little time out to eat. By the end of the breeding season, a buck that began the rut in peak condition can be drawn and thin, actually haggard looking. Some bucks lose up to 20 percent of their body weight during the course of the rut. Across the Northern states and Canada, where winter-kill is an annual problem, the big, breeding bucks follow the fawns in death each winter simply because they run off all of their reserve fat during the rut and are often unable to replenish it before the onset of winter snows.

Although the largest bucks are often nocturnal in their feeding habits at other times of the year, when the rut is over, dominant bucks cannot afford to limit their foraging to the hours of darkness alone. In order to regain their strength and put on the layer of fat necessary to see them through the winter, they must forage during the day. This gives the after-the-opener hunter the chance he needs to find bucks up and about during shooting hours.

This need to feed heavily does not necessarily mean that a big buck will be found sauntering around an alfalfa field at high noon, scarfing down the last frost-tinged greens of the year. It does mean that rather than tucking himself safely away in a thicket long before the morning sun ever peeks over the ridge, a buck will now tend to linger at the feeding grounds longer in the morning. Does and fawns often feed for an hour after sunrise, and now, bucks will begin to do the same. In the evening, when they normally would not begin feeding until complete darkness had fallen, bucks will now arrive at the feeding sites earlier — often in that last precious minute of shooting light.

Even at midday, the big boys will not be content to simply lay in their beds and chew their cuds. I have noticed that once the rut is over, bucks are much more likely to rise from their beds and spend an hour or two at midday — often between 11 a.m. and 2 p.m. — browsing near their bedding sites.

All of this daylight activity is a big plus for the after-the-opener hunter who must depend upon natural deer movements, rather than other hunters, to put a buck in front of him. The natural inclination is to hunt from a stand near the feeding area, but this is a mistake when you’re after mature bucks. A big buck is constantly alert, using each of his senses to detect the slightest hint of danger. Never is a buck more alert than when he is feeding during shooting light because he knows he is most vulnerable then. The bucks I have observed feeding during the season have been wound-up tighter than a watch spring and ready to snap at the slightest provocation. One miscue on your part, whether it is movement or scent or whatever, will send him running. I try to avoid known feeding areas, and I concentrate my efforts near the bedding areas.

Hunting near the bedding area means that any shot I take will most often be attempted in good light. That’s important for a clean kill. A buck may not arrive back at his bedding area until the sun is an hour high in the morning, and he may leave his bed to begin a dawdling trip to the feeding area in late afternoon. Instead of risking a poor shot at a shadowy form in the gray gloom of dusk or dawn, I take my shot when the light is in my favor.

Also, by concentrating my efforts in the vicinity of the buck’s daytime haunts, I have a better chance of catching him up and about during the midday hours. If I am forced to move into the bedding area and take a stand at mid-morning, I risk detection by the buck. But if I move into my stand in the dark and set up quietly, I double my chances of scoring. If I fail to take the buck as he returns from breakfast, I will be there waiting when he gets up for lunch or dinner.

But you don’t have to confine yourself to a stand. If conditions allow for quiet stillhunting, I like nothing better than to either prowl the edges of a buck’s daytime haunts or simply tread my way along a ridge littered with the acorns of the white oak, moving only a few yards at a time and stopping often to look, listen and feel. Often after the crowds of opening day are gone from the woods for another year, it is just me and the deer — one-on-one. And that’s the way I like it best.

Thomas Frank shares his feelings on Crystal Palace appointment

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Thomas Frank is back, and he sounds exactly like a man who has had enough time away to think clearly. Crystal Palace have appointed the 53-year-old on a two-and-a-half-year deal, and his first message was simple. He is refreshed, reflective and ready for another go in the Premier League.

Palace need that. They are bottom of the table, short on momentum and heading into a survival battle that will demand clarity more than noise. Frank arrives after a bruising end to his previous spell at Tottenham, where he was dismissed in February, less than eight months after taking charge. He could have stayed away longer. He chose not to.

There was no attempt to dress it up. Frank admitted the break had been enjoyable. He travelled to Japan and Australia, spent time in Denmark and relaxed with family and friends. As he put it, he had been "visiting friends and family, eating too well [and] drinking too much wine".

He had every reason to extend that time off too. "I was very aware I didn't want to do anything in the summer. I put a statement out there, I needed more time," he said. Then came the line that tells you where his head is now, "I can actually, honestly say I would have been fine if it was three, four, five, six, seven months more, because it's good to be back in the circus, but it's also unbelievably good not to be in, especially if you enjoy life, which I do."

Still, Palace convinced him. The club's internal pitch clearly mattered. Frank singled out chairman Steve Parish, along with Matt Hobbs and Iain Moody, as key figures in persuading him this was the right job at the right time.

That matters because sentiment gets you nowhere at the bottom of the Premier League. Palace need points, quickly. Frank's reign starts with a trip to Coventry City on Saturday, and there is no gentle easing-in period. The task is obvious, steady the team, sharpen the structure and lift belief.

Frank also made it clear that his time out of football was not spent feeling sorry for himself. "If you don't learn from any of your jobs, I don't think you reflect, I don't think you improve, and I don't think you get better," said Frank. "Of course there's a lot of things I have been reflecting over. The details, that will be for another time, but I can say I've learned, definitely."

This is where experience matters. Frank knows the scrutiny, understands the noise and appears entirely unbothered by it. "I think we all have an ego," he said. "Some are better at controlling it than others. For me, it's not the critics, it's more what I can learn, what I can prove, how I can get better."

He was even more direct when discussing outside opinion. "There's a lot of clever people out there, but the interesting thing is they only know 10 per cent of the truth. That's a tenth of the truth at Brentford, there's a tenth of the truth at Tottenham, and that's just the way it is.

"If you can't handle that as a coach or a player, then you shouldn't be in this business, and I'm very happy to be back in the game."

That is the point. No melodrama, no self-pity, no grand promises. Crystal Palace have hired a manager who sounds realistic about the mess in front of him and calm about the pressure around it. Right now, that is a sensible start.

Report: €20m will be enough for Liverpool to complete sensational transfer for playmaker

Liverpool are at the centre of an exciting move, and it could be for a bargain.

If there is one thing Andoni Iraola will be disappointed by during his start to life at Anfield it's the lack of creativity in his attack.

The club's recruitment team has assembled an exciting cohort of attackers. Yet aside from Alexander Isak, none of them appear to be thriving too much in front of goal.

In fact, Liverpool could be a lot worse off if it was not for Isak's efficiency who is very much putting away most of his chances at the moment.

But statistically, Liverpool are struggling to create chances, and this is due to the accumulation of various factors.

It's something that has to be addressed in the market, but there could be internal solutions as well.

Why are Liverpool struggling to create chances?A lot of people have pinned the blame on Florian Wirtz. He is after all the chief architect at the club. But Liverpool's problems are not simply down to Wirtz not pulling his weight. If you're looking at Liverpool's attackers with the exception of Bradley Barcola, most of them are attackers who want to cut into the middle.
