MPLX Raised Its Payout 12.5%. Here’s How Much $10,000 Invested Now Pays Each Year.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The master limited partnership (MPL) again raised its dividend by 12.5%, meaning $10,000 is generating even more income for investors.

By Todd Shriber – Nov 20, 2026 at 2:30AM EST

One of the epicenters of above-average dividend yields and, in many cases, alluring dividend growth is the energy sector, specifically pipeline stocks.

MPLX (MPLX -1.55%) is certainly part of that conversation. This master limited partnership (MLP) stores and transports natural gas and oil in the Marcellus Basin in eastern North America and the Permian Basin in Texas, among other locations.

It's one of the most impressive dividend growth stories among large-cap midstream equities, and that's saying something because that asset class is ripe with high yields and stout payout-growth trajectories.

MPLX made good on a promise to lift its distribution by 12.5% and the payout growth could continue. Image source: Getty Images.

In conjunction with its third-quarter earnings report released on Nov. 3, the company announced its third consecutive 12.5% annual dividend increase, putting it in a strong position to honor its previously announced pledge to grow its distribution by 12.5% annually through at least 2027.

With the latest dividend increase announcement, MPLX yields an eye-catching 8.5%, and its yearly payout in dollar terms vaults to $4.84 per share. So that means investors who owned $10,000 (and all shareholders, for that matter) of this midstream stock before this month are getting raises.

Based on the Nov. 13 closing price of $57.25, $10,000 buys 174.6 shares. Let's simplify the math and call that 175 shares. With the new payout of $4.84 a share, 175 shares generate $847 in yearly income. That's pretty good.

It's even better for investors who don't need that income right now. They can -- and probably should -- consider the merits of dividend reinvestment, particularly if the pipeline operator makes good on its pledge to grow its payout by 12.5% again next year.

Figure it this way: With a $10,000 stake, even if the stock were to trade flat for a year, the $847 in dividends investors will receive over the next 12 months would buy another 14.7 shares based on the Nov. 13 closing prices.

It might not sound like much, but that's nearly another $72 added to an investor's compounding mix with MPLX. Plus, based on the company's $1.5 billion in third-quarter distributable cash flow, it's generating enough cash flow to cover its shareholder rewards obligations.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 790%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 202% for the S&P 500.

Why Are Bitcoin, Ethereum and XRP Prices Still Falling and What’s Next?

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Bitcoin fell to $61,496.58, down 0.7% in 24 hours and 11.5% over the past week. Ether dropped 1.3% on the day to $1,724.13 and is down 12.3% for the week. XRP held up better than both, edging 0.3% higher to $0.9593. It has lost 5.5% over seven days.

A short rebound earlier in the week ran out of steam at resistance, and sellers took control again.

Bitcoin climbed off a low near $62,000 last week but stalled in the $65,000 to $67,000 zone. That band had worked as support before the crash, and once broken it turned into resistance. Rejections there were expected, and the price has since slid back toward its lows.

According to analyst Josh of Crypto World, several factors are still weighing on the market:

At $61,496, Bitcoin is back inside the $61,000 to $62,100 support zone, where it bounced last week. This is the most important level on the chart in the near term.

The base case calls for choppy sideways trading, with Bitcoin holding above its recent low for a week or two rather than staging a sharp recovery. Even a move above $67,000 would not guarantee a quick run to $74,000 while momentum stays weak.

Ether has fallen to $1,724, just below the bottom of its $1,730 to $1,800 support zone. Several tests of this area in recent days had failed to produce a daily close below it. A daily close under $1,730 would be the first confirmed break.

The daily RSI had started to recover from oversold levels, pointing to a short pause in the crash. That recovery is now being tested.

If the break below $1,730 holds, Ether could slide toward $1,650 sooner than expected.

XRP Holds Up Better but Faces a Wall at $1

XRP is the strongest of the three this week. The token climbed toward the $0.99 to $1.02 resistance zone but struggled there, as expected, and has eased back to about $0.96.

The daily RSI is close to leaving oversold territory, which leaves a little room for a further mild bounce.

Busan-Selected Director Anoop Lokkur Turns His Own Tenant War Into Bengaluru Dark Comedy ‘Melgade Mane Kelgade Mane’ at India’s WAVES Film Bazaar

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Indian-Australian filmmaker Anoop Lokkur, whose debut “Don’t Tell Mother” premiered at the Busan International Film Festival in 2025, is turning his own standoff with tenants who wouldn’t leave his family home into “Melgade Mane Kelgade Mane” (Upstairs Downstairs), a dark comedy in the Co-Production Market at WAVES Film Bazaar.

The Kannada and English-language film follows Karthik, who returns from Melbourne, along with his wife and young daughter, to look after his ailing father. He expects to take over the top floor of the house in an older Bengaluru neighborhood where his family has lived for three generations. The tenant, government contractor Ramesh, had agreed to move out, then changes his mind. Polite negotiation gives way to a tit-for-tat war as legal routes go nowhere, the power gets cut, property is damaged and access is blocked. Related Stories Hybe Names Melissa Victor-Burkhardt Executive VP of Global Artist Services Former Cineworld CEO Mooky Greidinger Leads Acquisition of Showcase Cinemas in U.K., Inks Major Imax Deal

Lokkur says that in 2025 he returned to Bangalore with his wife and their 1-year-old daughter, planning to settle upstairs in his parents’ home, but the tenants stayed on after their lease ran out and the family ended up crammed into his parents’ part of the house. “The whole situation was frustrating and unpleasant. What followed were months of tension, passive aggression and even thinking of ways to get them to vacate. The irony, though, was that only a couple of years earlier, my wife and I had been forced out of a rented home ourselves.”

Having been on both sides of the door, he says, he grew curious about ownership, about what it means to call a place home and about how hard people will push to come out on top. “I realized there was a dark comedy in this situation that was universal, and the best way to deal with my frustration was to turn it into a film.”

The project is in development, with Lokkur and Mikayla Henke producing through Papunu Films. The budget is $1.3 million, with $156,000 in place, and the team is looking for international co-producers, financiers and sales agents in Goa.

“The film is built like a tennis match. Your sympathies keep switching between the two families, until you’re no longer sure who to cheer for,” Lokkur says. “I’m interested in how ego quietly takes over and how ordinary decent people justify each small act of revenge until they no longer recognize themselves. It’s also a film about Bangalore, a city changing so fast that old neighborhoods and family homes are disappearing.”

Henke says she went through the dispute alongside Lokkur, and that with the family living it every day, it was the obvious choice for their next film. “‘Don’t Tell Mother’ was a tender and very personal film. On the surface, ‘Melgade Mane Kelgade Mane’ (Upstairs Downstairs) might look like the opposite: darker, bolder and funnier. But it comes from the same place, a close eye on family and the things people struggle to say to each other. This time, we just want to take audiences on a much wilder ride.”

“Don’t Tell Mother” played in the A Window on Asian Cinema section at Busan, was nominated for best youth film at the 2026 Asia Pacific Screen Awards and had its Australian premiere at this year’s Sydney Film Festival. Lokkur’s short “Long Distance,” which Henke produced, screened at the Melbourne International Film Festival and Palm Springs International ShortFest before Canal+ acquired it. Henke was also the first producer on “Don’t Tell Mother.”

“While it’s set in Bengaluru, it’s a universal story about housing that people everywhere can relate to and we believe it can travel well beyond India,” the producers say.

WAVES Film Bazaar runs as the market component of the International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa.

→ Baca artikel asli

Christie’s tepid 21st-century sale caps week of blockbuster auctions in New York

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Christie's auctioneer Yü-Ge Wang sells the top lot of the sale auction house's 19 November auction in New York, Jean-Michel Basquiat’s Untitled (Leonardo and His Five Grotesque Heads) (1983) Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Wrapping up this autumn’s week of marquee evening sales in New York, Christie’s 21st-century auction on Thursday (19 November) proved rather lacklustre, bringing in $117.7m ($145.9m with fees). That tally fell short of Christie’s pre-sale expectations, which do not account for fees, and projected a total take in the range of $126m to $192m. It was also a lower result compared to the auction house’s last two equivalent sales in New York—namely the $132m ($162.6m with fees) generated across 42 lots last May, and the $99.5m ($123.5m with fees) brought by 45 lots one year ago.

Just five of the 56 lots offered Thursday night failed to sell, for a slim buy-in rate of 9%. Two lots were withdrawn at the last moment, including a Jeff Koons sculpture estimated to bring between $3m and $5m. Twenty-four lots were backed by third-party guarantees and nine were backed fully by house guarantees, representing around 59% of all the works offered. Auction records for three artists were broken over the course of the night.

Roy Lichtenstein, Small Wall Explosion, 1965 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Business began with a bang at Christie’s Rockefeller Center salesroom: the first lot was Roy Lichtenstein’s Small Wall Explosion (1965), executed in porcelain enamel on steel and perforated steel relief to mimic the Ben-Day dot style from his paintings. It sold to a telephone bidder for $625,000 ($800,000 with fees). From an edition of six, it hailed from the collection of Antonio Homem Sonnabend, the adopted son of the late and legendary dealer and collector Ileana Sonnabend (1914-2007) and who ran his mother’s namesake gallery in its waning years. Antonio Homem died earlier this year, at age 86.

Another of the 19 Sonnabend lots from Sonnabend, Ashley Bickerton’s expansive wall relief Commercial Piece #3 (1990), covered with gallery and fashion-house logos and comprised of anodised aluminium, wood, leather, acrylic and rubber, sold for $120,000 ($153,600 with fees). Both the Lichtenstein and the Bickerton were backed by third-party guarantees.

Andy Warhol, Campbell's Soup Can (Turkey Noodle), 1962 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Price points for the Sonnabend trove zoomed higher with Andy Warhol’s iconicand hand painted Campbell’s Soup Can (Turkey Noodle) (1962), which sold for $7.3m ($9m with fees) against an estimate of $6m to $8m. Another Lichtenstein, the painting Large Spool (1963)—a razor-sharp depiction of the titular object that has been featured in multiple gallery and museum exhibitions—squeaked by on the tails of a third-party guarantee at $7m ($8.6m with fees) against an ambitious estimate of $10m to $15m.

Still in the Sonnabend lots, Jeff Koons’s toy-like Hulk (Friends) (2004-12), in polychromed bronze and standing nearly 6ft tall, found a new home for $3.3m ($4.1m with fees). And another Warhol, redolent with Hollywood celebrity, The Kiss (Bela Lugosi) (1963) in silkscreen ink on white paper, sold to another telephone bidder for $3.3m ($4.6m with fees).

A petite Jasper Johns oil painting on cardboard, Numbers (1957), measuring just 8.5in by 6in and rich in shades of grey, made $2.6m ($3.2m with fees). It was featured in the artist’s first solo exhibition at the Leo Castelli Gallery in 1958. Ileana Sonnabend acquired it from the Castelli Gallery in 1959, the same year of her divorce from Leo Castelli.

The Sonnabend group of 19 lots, including three examples of important 20th-century French decorative art—among them a Jean Prouvé Trapéze table from around 1956 that hit $1.3m ($1.6m with fees)—all in tallied $33.3m ($41.9m with fees), compared to Christie’s pre-sale expectations for the group of $35m to $55m.

Illustrious consignor names continued with works from the collection of the late Museum of Modern Art trustee and patron Agnes Gund sold to benefit the Gund Art Foundation. They included Robert Ryman’s immaculate, small, square all-over white composition Untitled (1961), which brought $2.9m ($3.6m with fees). Vija Celmins’s photorealist oil painting Untitled (Ocean) (1990), which featured in the San Francisco Museum of Modern Art’s traveling retrospective in 2018-2020, went for $5.7m ($7.1m with fees) against an estimate of $6m to $8m. It also was backed by a third-party guarantee.

Vija Celmins, Untitled (Ocean), 1990 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Gund acquired Alexander Calder’s hanging metal mobile The Eye in the Black (1963) from the Perls Galleries in New York in in 1967. On Thursday night it went to an online bidder for $2.8m ($3.5m with fees). Cindy Sherman’s Untitled Film Still #5 (1977), also widely exhibited and written about by major critics, sold for $320,000 ($409,6000 with fees). Gund had acquired it at Sotheby’s New York in November 2000 for a hammer price of $75,000.

Gund’s contemporary taste bordered on the encyclopaedic, as evidenced by an early Robert Rauschenberg work, Untitled (Gold Painting) (1953), in gold leaf on fabric and paper collage on canvas. The diminutive work, at 8in by 8in, fetched a sizeable sum, $1.7m ($2.1m with fees). Gund acquired it at Sotheby’s in New York in May 1994 for a $55,000 hammer price.

A modestly scaled Lichtenstein work on paper in coloured pencil, graphite and paper collage, Artist’s Studio with Model (Study) (1974), sold to the art adviser Ralph DeLuca for $950,000 ($1.2m with fees).

Jim Nutt, Loom, 1999 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

A standout from the Gund trove, a work she described as “my Mona Lisa”, was another modestly-scaled work, by Jim Nutt, founder of Chicago’s Hairy Who group. Titled Loom (1999) and measuring 15in by 14in, the portrait of a woman with a penetrating gaze set a new auction record for Nutt’s work when it more than doubled its high estimate of $350,000 and ultimately sold for $850,000 ($1m with fees). The ten lots sold to benefit Gund’s charitable foundation tallied $20.3m ($25.7m with fees), squarely within Christie’s pre-sale estimate of $16m to $24m.

Though not part of the Gund Art Foundation offerings, Alice Neel’s double portrait David and Catherine Saalfield (1982) hailed from Agnes Gund’s family and brought $1.6m ($2m with fees). Gund acquired it directly from the artist the year it was painted—not surprising since the sitters were her young adult children.

Alice Neel, David and Catherine Saalfield, 1982 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

The appeal of buying a piece of the legacy of storied dealers and collectors did not fare so well for a work that had belonged to the late gallerist Marian Goodman. Bidding on Gerhard Richter’s Abstraktes Bild (1990) stalled at $5.7m, against an estimate of $6m to $8m, and the lot failed to sell. As Richter’s longtime New York dealer, Goodman acquired the lushly surfaced squeegee painting directly from the artist and exhibited the painting in a gallery group show the year it was made. (A second Richter from another consignor fared better, with that somewhat larger Abstraktes Bild from 1991 bringing $7.6m, or $9.4m with fees.)

Among the works from more anonymous consignors, Richard Prince’s famous Nurse series made a splash when Everglade Nurse (2003), executed in acrylic and inkjet print on canvas, sold for $3.1m ($3.9m with fees). It last sold at Sotheby’s New York in November 2008 for $3.4m with fees, a rather flat return after 18 years.

Large-scale paintings destined to test living room wall space were also in evidence with Christopher Wool’s untitled black-and-white abstraction from 2009, measuring more than 8ft tall. It brought $2m ($2.5m with fees). George Condo’s more than 9ft-wide Double Heads in Blue and Silver (2014), realised $2.4m ($3m with fees). The Condo hailed from the collection of Stefan Edlis and his wife Gael Neeson, who donated 44 works cumulatively valued at more than $400m to the Art Institute of Chicago in 2015.

The top lot of the evening, unsurprisingly, was Jean-Michel Basquiat’s quirky and text-rich composition, Untitled (Leonardo and his Five Grotesque Heads) (1983), which hammered to another anonymous telephone bidder for $28m ($32.8m with fees) against a sky-high estimate of $30m to $50m.

Jean-Michel Basquiat, Untitled (Leonardo and His Five Grotesque Heads), 1983 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

Evidently inspired by Leonardo da Vinci’s 1493 pen-and-ink drawing on paper A man tricked by Gypsies (also known as Five grotesque heads), from the Royal Collection Trust at Windsor Castle in London, Basquiat’s freestyle interpretation in oil stick and acrylic against a burnt orange background crackles with the artist’s terse way with wordplay and actually features eight heads in all.

Measuring 80in by 80in, the painting has no exhibition history, at least none acknowledged in Christie’s online catalogue entry—though it was included in the Whitney Museum of American Art’s 1992 Basquiat retrospective. The catalogue entry for the Whitney exhibition listed the owner as Zurich’s Bruno Bischofberger, an early and important champion of the artist. It last sold at auction at Sotheby’s London in October 2005, when it was the top lot at £736,000 ($1.3m) including fees against an estimate of £400,000 to £600,000. Sotheby’s at the time identified the buyer as “US Trade” and the current consignor acquired it from that entity in 2006.

Julia Jo, One Fell Swoop, 2026 Courtesy Christie's Images Ltd

The evening ended on a sweet note with Julia Jo’s large-scale abstraction One Fell Swoop (2026), donated to benefit the New York-based Coalition for the Homeless. It set a new auction record for Jo’s work, fetching $320,000 ($409,600 with fees). (The night’s other new auction record was for the work of the Spanish artist Antonio Santín.)

Apart from that feeling, the outlook was not as rosy. “It’s been a very long week, with a lot of art, and I think people are tired,” DeLuca said. “The theme of the week is that estimates are a little aspirational.”

The long week’s last burst of activity will be a pair of day sales at Christie’s on Friday (20 November), after which the US art industry will mark a pause to observe Thanksgiving before migrating south for Art Basel Miami Beach.

With estates and collections accounting for some of the season’s biggest consignments, auction houses are betting wealthy collectors will shrug off market volatility

Despite the underwhelming result, the sale notched new auction records for Barkley Hendricks and Alma Thomas

New auction records were set for Pollock, Rothko, Brancusi, Miro and Neel

Swan song of veteran auctioneer Jussi Pylkkänen made $640.8m—the house's highest total for a non-single-owner sale in six years

$500 Invested in GE Aerospace (GE) Stock Today Could Be Worth This Much in 3 Years

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The stock has been volatile, but its price is appealing, and its growth prospects good.

By Selena Maranjian – Nov 20, 2026 at 2:05AM EST

10 stocks we like better than GE Aerospace ›

So you're thinking of investing $500 in GE Aerospace (GE -2.81%) and you're wondering what kind of growth you can expect over the coming years. Well, no one can really know just how a stock will perform in the future -- though we can make some educated guesses about it.

Let's take a look at GE Aerospace, which leads in designing, building, and servicing aircraft turbine engines. It recently boasted an installed base of approximately 50,000 commercial and 30,000 military aircraft engines.

Here's how the stock has grown over time:

Data source: Morningstar.com, as of Nov. 17, 2026.

Looking at the table, it's hard to guess how much it will grow in three years. One rough approach might be to take its longest-term average of close to 9% and boost it a bit, conservatively, perhaps to 11%. If the stock grows by 11% annually for three years, it will grow by about 37%. So from the recent stock price of $329, it could reach $450 per share. If you spent $500 on the stock (buying fractional shares), your stake would grow to $684.

But should you buy into the stock? Well, the recent stock price of $329 is down about 15% from its 52-week high to of $389. Still, its shares look somewhat overvalued, with a recent price-to-sales ratio of 6.5, well above the five-year average of 2.2, and its forward-looking price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of 35 is a bit above the five-year average of 32. If you buy now and plan to hang on for many years, you could do well. But if you hold out for (and snag) a lower entry point, you could do even better.

There's a lot to like about GE Aerospace, such as its $170 backlog of commercial services and its third-quarter year-over-year revenue jump of 49%, with profit margins rising, too.

22 Years Later, the Greatest Quote in TV Western History Is Still Untouchable

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Yellowstone may have pop culture in its grasp, but how soon fans forget about the best Western series that graced television. That honor, of course, goes to David Milch’s Deadwood, the gritty three-season masterpiece on HBO. Milch famously wanted to do a series about Ancient Rome, but when HBO’s Rome went into production, his hopes were dashed. Instead, he delivered the now cult classic series about the real town in the Old West, full of debauchery, crime, and gold prospecting.

If there was one thing that Deadwood was famous for, however, it was the dialogue. The Western has been compared to the likes of Shakespeare, though somehow even more swear-riddled. The controversial quotes have cemented the series into an unmissable classic, though there is one line of dialogue to rule them all.

In a series that is defined by its dialogue, there are many memorable quotes, but only one gets to the heart of the vicious world the series takes place in. Deadwood follows a former Marshal, Seth Bullock (Timothy Olyphant), who wants to start a hardware business with his business partner, Sol Starr (John Hawkes). Deadwood is the new land of opportunity, but Bullock is immediately blown back by the owner of the Gem Saloon, Al Swearengen (Ian McShane). Al swears by the idea that this world is cruel and he will respond in kind.

Deadwood is, of course, a lawless town full of murder, laudanum, and sex work, but all Bullock wants to do is run his small business. Despite his insistence on remaining impartial, his principles can’t help but continue to crop up. Bullock is characterized by these seemingly contradictory personality traits. As principled as he is, he is also about the angriest man in the gold mining town.

This puts him in the crosshairs of Al and all others looking to take advantage of the townspeople. Bullock resists his destiny for quite a long time before it is impossible to ignore any longer. Corruption is so rampant in the town that he has no choice but to take control.

“Fine, I’ll be the f***ing sheriff,” Bullock swears in a character-defining quote. His reluctance not only defines the character but also the complexities of the mining camp as a whole.

Good is very hard to come by down in these here parts. Deadwood becomes so rife with corruption that there is really only one person who can be entrusted to keep the peace. With the profanity that the series is known for and Bullock’s no-nonsense attitude, this quote distills Deadwood into the gold standard for Western television series.

Bullock accepting this new post was also the end and start of a new era. The lawlessness made Deadwood famous, but his acceptance of the post signaled that some civility would be coming to the town. Bullock sets the tone for Deadwood, which is a place in between being civilized and no man’s land. This is the sweet spot for one of the best HBO series, which has always played by its own rules. Seth Bullock began what the series was in earnest and created an iconic character that no one is likely to forget.

Order, method, and a quiet confidence that you are the cleverest person present. Poirot is a retired Belgian police officer who solves murders by sitting still and thinking, and who is vain enough to make the reveal a performance every single time.

People tell you things because they cannot imagine you being a threat. Marple solves murders by comparing everybody to somebody she once knew in St Mary Mead, which sounds like a joke until you notice it works every time, because human beings are not as original as they think.

Applying for Spousal Social Security Benefits in 2027? 3 Things You Need to Know.

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Spousal benefits have some unique rules that are worth knowing before you sign up.

By Kailey Hagen, CFP® – Nov 19, 2026 at 7:00PM EST

Spousal Social Security checks tend to be a lot smaller than retirement benefits, averaging just $988 in October 2026 compared to $2,092 for retirement checks. But they can still make a meaningful difference to your household's budget in 2027.

Spousal benefits have a few rules that make them different from retirement benefits, and it's important to understand them if you plan to apply for these checks next year. Here are three of the biggest differences you should be aware of.

You must be at least 62 or older to claim a spousal Social Security benefit. But even at this age, you can’t sign up if your partner is not yet receiving the partner's own retirement benefit. If you're eligible for a retirement benefit on your own work record, you may claim this in the meantime. Otherwise, you have to wait for your partner to apply first.

It's important that you and your spouse are both on the same page about when each of you will apply so that you can maximize your household income. If you haven't already talked through your plans, now is a great time to do so before you try to apply for Social Security benefits in 2027.

Spousal benefits are also available to ex-spouses if you were married to a qualifying worker for at least 10 years before divorcing and you haven’t remarried. Your ex's remarriage will not affect your eligibility to claim benefits on the ex's work record.

Benefits for ex-spouses work almost exactly the same as benefits for current spouses, except you don't need to wait for your ex to sign up for benefits first. As long as you've been divorced for at least two years and are 62 or older, you can claim a spousal benefit on your ex's work record, provided it's worth more than your own retirement benefit.

Social Security retirement benefits continue to grow for every month you delay your application until you qualify for your maximum checks at age 70. But spousal benefits only grow until you reach your full retirement age (FRA). This is 67 if you were born in 1960 or later.

Make sure you sign up by this point at the latest, unless you can’t because your spouse hasn't applied yet. If you fail to sign up by your FRA, contact the Social Security Administration right away to get the process started and to see if you qualify for any retroactive benefits.

You Don’t Need 20% Down – Set Aside Closing Costs and an Emergency Fund First

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Buying a home doesn’t take 20% down. Many loans take as little as 3% or 3.5%, and VA loans for veterans and USDA loans for some rural buyers need no down payment. Set aside your cash for closing costs, moving, and emergencies first, then put down what’s left and weigh the mortgage insurance a smaller down payment adds.

Most first-time buyers put down less than 20%. Their median down payment was 9% in the National Association of Realtors’ 2026 buyer survey.

Twenty percent is only the point where a conventional loan, one without government backing, stops requiring private mortgage insurance (PMI), which protects the lender if you stop paying. A smaller down payment can also mean a higher interest rate. You can ask to drop PMI once you owe 80% of the home’s original value. Government-insured FHA loans keep their mortgage insurance for the life of the loan when you put down less than 10%.

On a $350,000 home, 5% down is $17,500, against $70,000 for 20%. Freddie Mac estimates PMI at $30 to $70 a month per $100,000 borrowed, or roughly $100 to $230 a month on the $332,500 loan, a cost to weigh against the $52,500 you’d keep. Waiting costs something too. Home prices rose 2.4% in the year through August 2026, the Federal Housing Finance Agency reports. At that pace, if saving the other $52,500 takes three years, the same house will cost about $376,000, and 20% of it about $75,000. Buying sooner with less down avoids that rise, as long as your savings stay intact.

Start from the lowest down payment your loan allows, on a home that appraises for at least its price.

Then set aside closing costs, typically 2% to 5% of the price, money for moving and repairs, and an emergency fund covering three to six months of essential bills. Whatever is left goes toward the down payment, and down payment assistance programs can help you reach your loan’s minimum. Ask lenders to quote your monthly payment at 5% and 10% down.

PMI can come off later, but cash you put into the house comes back out only if you borrow against it or sell.

Compare the best mortgage lenders for a loan that fits the down payment you choose.

Her coach, Trillium Rose, director of Instruction at Woodmont Country Club in Rockville, Maryland, started with short game instruction for the 55-year-old.

"I think as an Olympic gold medalist and world champion, people may kind of brush it off and say, oh yeah, she's a good athlete, of course she can do that, and it's just not true. You don't go from one sport and then automatically be good at another sport," Rose said. "No, you go to the bottom, you go to the back of the line with everybody else, and you have to start and grind out with the fundamentals. There's no skipping steps."

I spoke to Rose after she returned from the LPGA's Annika event at Pelican Golf Club in Belleair, where she played alongside Scurry in the Nov. 9 Pro-Am. Cameras documented the round and Scurry's progress for the second episode of the series.

Rose said Scurry never owned a pair of golf clubs until this year.

"To be vulnerable and debut a new golf swing in a professional LPGA-sponsored event when you've only had five hours of golf is remarkable. It takes a lot of courage," she said.

Brandi Chastain (6), goalkeeper Brianna Scurry, and team captain Carla Overbeck (4) of the USA take part in the pre-match ceremony prior to their semi-final game against Brazil during the 1999 Women's World Cup tournament played on July 4, 1999, at Stanford Stadium on the campus of Stanford University in Palo Alto, California. (David Madison/Getty Images)

The "Breaking 99" series was created after Scurry served as a celebrity golf caddy for WNBA player Caitlin Clark during last year's Annika tournament. Clark and Scurry are both paid ambassadors for Gainbridge, the financial company that sponsors the Annika tournament.

"I've always loved a challenge. It's what drove me throughout my soccer career, and that competitive spirit hasn't gone anywhere," said Scurry in an LPGA.com report. "I was so inspired by my experience last year … I'm excited to take on a new sport with the goal of breaking 99 in honor of the '99ers ahead of the 2027 Women's World Cup."

The soccer star/golf collaboration serves as a promotion for the game of golf as well as the upcoming World Cup. Episodes will air periodically until the FIFA Women's World Cup in Brazil starts on June 24.

Scurry, a National Soccer Hall of Famer, made a huge save when the USWNT won the 1999 World Cup at the Rose Bowl in Pasadena, Calif., in a penalty shootout against China in front of 90,000 fans.

Scurry's memoir, "My Greatest Save: The Brave, Barrier-Breaking Journey of a World-Champion Goalkeeper," gives insight into why Scurry is uniquely built for this challenge.

In the book, she candidly speaks of her recovery from severe head trauma and depression following her retirement from soccer.

Scurry's brain injury from a knee to the head in a professional soccer match caused her severe physical and emotional stress. She was unable to get her health insurance to pay for her ongoing concussion treatment and fell into debt. She pawned her gold medals.

Briana Scurry looks on after the United States beat Mexico 1-0 at Red Bull Arena in Harrison, New Jersey, on July 13, 2024. (Elsa/USSF/Getty Images for USSF)

Her greatest save came with the help of a woman who would become her wife. Chryssa Zizos, working as her hired publicist, helped her retrieve the medals eight months after she sold them for $18,000. Zizos also successfully pressured Scurry's insurance company to cover surgery and therapy that aided her recovery.

In her book, Scurry noted that she overcame adversity as a Black, gay woman throughout her career. Her national soccer team jersey is in the Smithsonian's National Museum of African American History and Culture. She's also a concussion awareness advocate who has testified before Congress.

She and her golf coach are an ideal fit. Rose is recognized among the Top 50 Teachers by Golf Digest and the LPGA. She volunteers her time as an instructor for the Howard University men's and women's golf teams, helping student-athletes grow through mentorship and performance development. She also volunteers her time for veterans and individuals with disabilities through her involvement with the Wounded Warriors and adaptive golf communities. She works with youngsters in the First Tee program.

"It's so important to me. I've just gotten so much from the game and so many doors have opened for me thanks to golf," she said. "I really truly believe that women can benefit from playing golf … and if you can play well enough to get invited to a pro-am or a business outing, then you have a seat at the table, you're in the conversations."

The same is true for minorities. Her work with Howard University students, in addition to instruction, is designed to help young minorities feel welcome on the golf course.

"Golf is for everyone and it's important for me to help beginners as well as high-level players. I mostly work with people who are under a 10 handicap and I'm proud of myself that I can simplify it and explain the basics to someone who hasn't heard them before," she said.

During the broadcast, Scurry gives a shoutout to Rose's techniques as a certified PGA and LPGA teaching professional.

"Trillium is fantastic. I mean, she has great eye contact with me, which is important for me when I'm trying to learn something," Scurry said.

Scurry's show is also recognizable to older golfers because her swing has to account for the wear and tear her body sustained during her professional soccer career. She suffers from spinal issues with "crushed discs" in her back, which viewers find out during the first episode.

"That's something we can all relate to, right?" Rose said.

Eintracht Frankfurt vs Hoffenheim - prediction, team news, lineups

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By Anthony Nolan | 19 Nov 2026 23:51

Hoping to pick up where they left off in the Bundesliga, Hoffenheim will travel to face Eintracht Frankfurt at Deutsche Bank Park on Saturday.

Die Adler could make it four games unbeaten this weekend, though Die Kraichgauer are searching for back-to-back wins.

Adi Hutter's Frankfurt kicked off the 2026-27 campaign in lacklustre fashion, winning just one of their first four league outings, but they found their feet prior to the international break.

The 2-2 draw with SC Paderborn on November 7 may seem lacklustre on paper, but Die Adler were reduced to 10 men that day, and were still able to come from behind to snatch a point thanks to Jonathan Burkardt's 93rd-minute equaliser.

That stalemate left Hutter's side 10th in the table with 12 points, where they are level with Saturday's opponents and just three shy of the top six.

Additionally, Frankfurt have now lost just one of their last five matches across all competitions, winning three times in the process, including a pair of 3-1 victories over Hamburger SV, one at home and one at Volksparkstadion in the DFB-Pokal.

However, Die Adler have conceded 20 goals in just nine top-flight games this term, giving them the second-worst defensive record in the Bundesliga, with only relegation-threatened Union Berlin (21) letting in more so far.

Meanwhile, Christian Ilzer's Hoffenheim also struggled at the start of the season, but have stabilised amidst a resurgence.

Die Kraichgauer began with four defeats from their first five Bundesliga games, as well as six losses from their opening seven across all competitions, a dire return that had fans fearing the worst.

Thankfully for Ilzer's men, they were able to escape their shocking form with three consecutive victories in mid-October, including a 3-0 triumph over Ligue 1 giants Lyon in the Europa League and another 3-0 success against Bayer Leverkusen three days later.

Hoffenheim signed off for the international break by collecting all three points in a 2-1 win against Schalke 04 at SNP Arena, a performance inspired by Fisnik Asllani, who scored the opener before assisting Tim Lemperle's second with just 26 minutes on the clock.

As a result, Die Kraichgauer's European ambitions are alive again considering the club are currently ninth with 12 points, a mere three behind sixth-placed Werder Bremen.

Eintracht Frankfurt form (all competitions):

Frankfurt will be without striker Jessic Ngankam due to an adductor strain, while attacking midfielder Can Uzun is a doubt with illness, and will face a late fitness test.

If the latter is unavailable, then expect to see Noel Aseko-Nkili and Raphael Onyedika start in the centre of the park, flanked by Ritsu Doan and Fares Chaibi, who will be supporting a partnership of Jonathan Burkardt and Younes Ebnoutalib up top.

Elsewhere, versatile full-back Elias Baum is suspended after being sent off against Paderborn, though Ansgar Knauff and Keita Kosugi should be on hand to start either side of Robin Koch and Lilian Brassier.

As for Hoffenheim, they are missing Robin Hranac as he recovers from meniscus surgery, while fellow centre-back Albian Hajdari is a doubt with a plantar fascia injury, and right-back Valentin Gendrey is sidelined after suffering a fibula fracture late last month.

In their absence, Ilzer could opt for Koki Machida and Ozan Kabak at the heart of his defence once again, with Mats Rots and Vladimir Coufal at full-back.

Die Kraichgauer also have doubts about the fitness of midfielders Bambase Conte (groin) and Nathan De Cat (knee), as well as wingers Alexander Prass (thigh) and Patrick Wimmer (muscle).

If none of the four are ready to feature, then Leon Avdullahu and Wouter Burger look set to operate in the visitors' double pivot, platforming Andrej Kramaric, Fisnik Asllani and Adam Daghim behind striker Tim Lemperle.

Eintracht Frankfurt possible starting lineup:

Atubolu; Knauff, Koch, Brassier, Kosugi; Doan, Aseko-Nkili, Onyedika, Chaibi; Ebnoutalib, Burkardt

Baumann; Coufal, Machida, Kabak, Rots; Avdullahu, Burger; Kramaric, Asllani, Daghim; Lemperle

Frankfurt have scored eight goals in their last three matches, and have proven resilient in recent weeks.

However, Hoffenheim are likely to pose a threat given Die Adler's lack of defensive solidity, so expect to see a close-fought contest even if the hosts prevail.

Liverpool transfer news: 'Tempting' £34m striker target from Serie A scouted as Andoni Iraola looks to strengthen attack

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

By Lewis Nolan | 19 Nov 2026 23:51

Liverpool have sent scouts to watch Monza striker Gustavo Varela on multiple occasions, the latest report has claimed.

The Reds are readying themselves for a crucial Premier League clash against Manchester United on Sunday, and a win could propel the club forward this season.

Andoni Iraola will hope that his side's attack can make the difference on the weekend, especially as the forwards in the squad struggled in previous games to make an impact.

The Merseysiders have scored just 14 goals in 10 league fixtures so far, with Alexander Isak the only attacker to have netted more than two goals in the top flight.

A new report from Corriere dello Sport claims that Liverpool have frequently observed Monza striker Varela, who could be signed for €40m (£33.51m).

The 21-year-old has been in fine form for Monza in Italy, with the striker having scored 11 goals in 11 games, while also proving an assist.

Varela only moved to the Serie A club in the summer transfer window, joining from Benfica, who sent him out on loan to Gil Vicente for the 2025-26 season.

Though committing €40m (£33.51m) to one player is not a particularly expensive outlay for a club with access to the riches of Liverpool, it would be risky to sign someone with such a limited history in top divisions and ask them to deputise Isak.

The likelihood that Liverpool are in the market for a striker is slim considering they not only have Isak in their squad, but also Hugo Ekitike.

While the former may be 27, the latter is only 24, and the club should not be looking to address the number nine spot for years to come.

It would be sensible to recruit younger stars to prepare for any potential decline among first-team stars, but there are great hopes for teenager Will Wright.

Adding yet another centre-forward to the equation would needlessly bloat the squad, and arguably block the pathway of academy talents.

Double Zero Days During More Arizona Trail Rain & Flash Flooding

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This morning I actually slept in pretty late at our room in Strawberry. It was nice knowing that there was no pressure to go anywhere or do anything. My only goals for the day are to get some editing done, eat a bunch of food, and resupply for this next stretch of trail. By the time that I did finally get up, Slide was very ready to eat. So, we decided to get our rain gear on and walk down the road to the breakfast farm stand right by the Strawberry Inn. I checked the hiker group chat and saw that a group of hikers from the Airbnb had walked over there and was hiding out from the rain in some kind of a shed. The place pretty much only has outdoor seating, but apparently the staff showed the hikers where they could hide out and eat at the same time.

Grabbing breakfast from the farm stand place in Strawberry.

When we got over there a handful of hikers was hanging out and ordering some more food. We got in line and ordered some stuff to take back and eat at our room, since it was right down the road. Everyone else finished up and wound up going back to their Airbnb to hang out. I figure we’ll head over there at some point today. But I also have a mountain of editing to get done, and it will definitely be easier to do it somewhere quiet. My eyes were way bigger than my stomach this morning, so I wound up ordering two things. The first was a lemon ricotta pancake, which sounded absolutely divine. And I also got a breakfast burrito to eat later on, just in case. On trail I always feel like I order way too much food or not nearly enough. Which is likely why I usually wind up ordering way too much.

The farm stand breakfast spot in Strawberry, AZ served one of the most incredible pancakes that I’d ever had.

We took the food to go and brought it back to the Strawberry Inn to eat and relax. And that lemon ricotta pancake was one of the best things that I’d ever had. I love ricotta but have never had it in a pancake before! Prior to getting on trail I was cooking a lot at home and making a ton of homemade pizza. Oftentimes I would top it with ricotta because it is so delicious. But this just opened my eyes to a whole new use for the stuff. I didn’t wind up eating everything right away but figured I’d just graze on it for the next couple of hours.

After I ate, I decided to get right into business and start doing some writing and video editing. I also had an exciting call with a brand that I’ve worked with in the past and am going to be working with next year. My plans for 2027 are finally starting to come together and I’m really excited to be able to share it with you all eventually. The tiny home that we were staying in had a separate room which was pretty much the size of a walk-in closet. That was the perfect place to do some audio recordings and get certain work done for a while. Once all of that was done, I could get the rest of the work done more comfortably, while also watching a little bit of TV.

In the afternoon I also had to place a Walmart delivery order to the inn. I had a lot of food, but definitely not enough to be comfortable for the next 71 miles. Our plan is to hike out of town and continue 71 miles to the next highway crossing, where we can go into the larger town of Payson. That will be about 3 days of hiking including the day out of town, and I’ve had a big appetite lately. It also sounded nice to get some drinks and snacks to enjoy this evening while we were at it. Before I placed the order, I messaged the hiker group chat to see if anyone needed anything random. Hikers always do! And sure, enough one hiker needed some sunblock and things like that. Which was easy enough to add on to the order. Plus, we’re going to be getting a ride back to trail tomorrow with a few of these hikers, so we’ll be seeing them.

Figuring out that you can do things in small towns such as place an online food order to be delivered is certainly a thru hiking hack. I had never even thought about doing anything like that prior to hiking the CYTC last year. Time was my most valuable resource and I was willing to do a lot to save it when I could. Oftentimes you’ll spend an hour walking to a store in town, resupplying, and then perhaps another hour walking back to the room. Some trail towns are spread out with miles between all of the essential locations. Oftentimes stores like Walmart won’t even charge a delivery fee for orders if you spend over a certain amount. Today I’m grateful to be able to do this and not have to hitch into the town of Pine to go back to the grocery store. Then hitch back once we’re finished.

While the Walmart order got delivered, I got a bunch more editing work done. I’ve also been sending a lot of emails and setting plans into motion for my upcoming hike next year. It feels a bit like Deja vu, honestly. When I was hiking the CDT in 2024, I did the bulk of all of my planning for the following years border to border CYTC hike. There are so many logistics involved in a hike like that. Plus, you need a lot of gear! I remember dedicating serious time in towns to do video editing and also all of that coordinating back then on the CDT. Then when I finished up the hike I went home and had about 6 weeks to finish all of the planning and work as much as I could to save up money for the endeavor. I guess this is just the season for it. But this all feels very familiar.

This wound up being the perfect space to spend a couple of days off in town. I got most of my work done in that little room by the couch. And there is another bed on the other side of that wall.

Eventually our Walmart order got delivered, but not without minor issue. The driver had some trouble finding the place, which I was expecting. The address for our location was pretty confusing. But then after the groceries were delivered, I noticed an item that we hadn’t ordered. Then I realized we were missing a few things that we had ordered. Luckily, they were all drinks, so it wasn’t anything essential to us getting back out on trail. But they were sugary drinks that I was excited to enjoy. There isn’t any kind of a store or gas station in Strawberry, which is really the only thing that this town is missing. My favorite thing to do in town is to buy a bunch of different beverages and drink them all over the course of the day. Typically, on a double zero such as this, I would probably have bought a small case of soda or something like that. Liquid calories are my secret to not losing too much weight on trail.

By now the day was already starting to wrap up. Zero days on trail have such a miraculous way of slipping right through your fingers. It happens every single time without a doubt. At least I did get a lot of “work” done today and I also felt like I relaxed a lot. There really isn’t anywhere to go in this town, so there was no reason to be anywhere except for the room all day. A little after 5PM we started figuring out what to do for dinner. Everyone at the Airbnb had gotten so many groceries that they were just going to be staying in and trying to eat through everything. I wasn’t starving at this point, but I knew that later on I would be. Over the course of the day, I ate the rest of my lemon ricotta pancake and the whole breakfast burrito. That little farm stand in Strawberry was fantastic.

Surprisingly our options for dinner this evening were not limited. There were actually a few places open in town tonight because it was later in the week. One place sold empanadas, but they actually did wind up closing right before we were ordering food. Then there was also a steakhouse and an Italian restaurant. We wound up getting a big white sauce pizza to split for dinner and were able to just walk down the road and pick it up. It was hardly raining at this point, though it had rained heavily for most of the day. It was pretty nice out when we walked to grab the pizza. The moon is incredibly full right now which is exciting moving into this next section of trail. I love night hiking when the moon is full.

Once we picked up the pizza we retreated back to the room for the rest of the night. The gang was just over at the Airbnb watching a movie. And while it would have been nice to hang out with everyone, I wasn’t in the mood to walk another mile just to watch a movie somewhere when I could just stay in bed and watch a movie there. It was too easy. The pizza was delicious and just what I needed. After we ate, I was contemplating going in the hot tub again. I went in this morning after breakfast and it was wonderful. But I think that going in first thing tomorrow will be even better. After eating I just relaxed for the rest of the night. I got a little bit more writing done and we watched TV for a while. Then I did my stretching routine and laid up on my phone for longer than I should have before finally calling it a night.

If you enjoyed this blog and would like to support my hike by buying me a soda or a snack along the way, there is a “Tip the Author” button below! It links directly to my Venmo. Any and all support is incredibly appreciated!

In 2023 I became the 2nd known woman to hike the Eastern Continental Trail from Key West, Florida to Newfoundland Canada. I completed my Double Triple Crown between 2022-2025.

Protect Your Car’s Resale Value – Keep the Records and Fix What Raises Its Grade

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Your car’s model and age set its starting resale price, but buyers then grade its condition and check its records, and you control much of both. Keep every service receipt, fix a flaw only when the repair costs less than the next condition grade adds to the price, and sell before a redesign or a big scheduled service.

Kelley Blue Book values a used car at one of four condition grades, Excellent, Very Good, Good, or Fair, and each step down lowers the price. Half the cars it values land at Good.

Tires and paperwork help set the grade. Kelley Blue Book’s Very Good grade calls for matching tires with at least 75% of their tread and most service records, while Good asks for at least half the tread and some records.

So the grades tell you which repairs pay. Price the car in Kelley Blue Book’s value tool at its honest grade and one grade up, as a trade-in or a private sale, whichever you plan. Spend on dent repair, professional detailing, or new tires only when the work costs less than the difference.

Until you sell, three habits protect the price:

Brand and color are set for good, but fuel prices move. With fuel costs high in the third quarter of 2026, Cox Automotive found the wholesale prices dealers paid for large pickups and SUVs falling and those for EVs and smaller, fuel-efficient cars rising. So list a large pickup or SUV while gas is cheap.

Sell before a big scheduled service, such as replacing the timing belt, comes due, since a buyer will subtract its cost from the offer.

Automakers reveal a redesign months before it goes on sale, and the new version draws buyers from yours, so list your car before it arrives.

The movie reps Hader’s feature directorial debut.

They Know also stars Linda Cardellini, who recently won an Emmy in supporting actress limited series for HBO’s DTF St. Louis.

Emily Carr and E.J. Hughes lead the way at Heffel's $10m autumn auctions

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

E.J. Hughes, Wharves at Chemainus, 2000 Courtesy Heffel Fine Art Auction House

The Heffel Fine Art Auction House held its marquee autumn evening sales on Wednesday (18 November) in Toronto, attracting a packed house of bidders and spectators to the city’s swank Yorkville district and generating C$14.4m ($10m) in sales, works by Canadian masters taking top dollar. As things got underway, a Heffel representative noted that any US tariffs were “the responsibility of the buyer”, a reflection of these often-testy times.

“At a time of strong national pride, it is especially meaningful to see collectors celebrating Canada’s artistic achievements and our distinct national culture,” David Heffel, the auction house’s president, said at the end of the evening.

Heffel's evening auctions in Toronto on 18 November Courtesy Heffel Fine Art Auction House

The back-to-back sales (41 lots of post-war and contemporary art followed by 47 works of Canadian, Impressionist and modern art) got off to a flying start when a watercolour by the Vancouver-born artist E.J. Hughes hammered for C$475,000 ($331,700)—nearly 12 times its high estimate. Titled Wharves at Chemainus (2000) and depicting the titular logging community on Vancouver Island, the painting is modest in size at 20in by 24in; with fees, the price came to C$571,250 ($398,900).

Hughes had made waves at Heffel’s auctions last spring, when his Coastal Boats Near Sidney, BC realised a whoppingC$5.7m ($4.1m), including fees. Several more Hughes works were up for sale Wednesday night including two that significantly outperformer their estimates: The Hope River at Chilliwack, BC (1961), which took in C$253,250 ($176,800) with fees; and The Freighter “World Ares” at Crofton (1980), which hauled in C$229,250 ($160,000) including fees.

Emily Carr, Alert Bay (with Welcome Figure), around 1912 Courtesy Heffel Fine Art Auction House

The second sale’s cover lot, Emily Carr’s luminous canvas Alert Bay (with Welcome Figure) (around 1912), brought C$1.3m ($922,000) including fees, comfortably within its estimate. It was the only seven-figure result of the night. Some of the proceeds from its sale will benefit the Audain Art Museum in Whistler, British Columbia, where it was shown in the context of a major traveling exhibition of Carr’s work in 2019-20.

A work on paper by the Carr, Music in the Trees (around 1935), topped its high estimate to achieve an impressive C$421,250 ($294,100) with fees. In all, six works by the Victoria-born artist were sold at Heffel on Wednesday night, fetching a combined, fee-inclusive total of C$2.5m ($1.7m).

Jean Paul Riopelle, Herbeuse, 1967 Courtesy Heffel Fine Art Auction House

A handful of works by Jean Paul Riopelle were on offer during the post-war and contemporary portion of the night, generating around C$1.3m ($907,800) in sales. His large-scale, wintry abstraction Herbeuse (1967),proved particularly appealing. Swift bidding pushed it to around double its high estimate. With fees, the price came to C$571,250 ($398,900). It was shown in the Carnegie International the year Riopelle painted it, and later in Paris, where the Montréal-born artist, a master of the palette knife, spent decades. The work with the highest estimate in the post-war and contemporary sale, Riopelle’s Brumaire (1953), was expected to bring between C$1.5m and C$2m, but failed to sell.

Among the night’s other big-ticket items was Paul-Émile Borduas’s oil painting Empâtement (1957), from the artist’s black-and-white period—and punctuated with splashes of red. It realised C$841,250 with fees ($587,400), easily topping its high estimate of C$500,000. It had been acquired directly from the artist by London’s Arthur Tooth & Sons gallery in 1957 and, more recently, was in the collection of Québecois lyricist and songwriter Luc Plamondon.

Paul-Émile Borduas, Empâtement, 1957 Courtesy Heffel Fine Art Auction House

J.E.H. MacDonald’s oil sketch Magnetawan River Rapids (1912), a preparatory work for a major canvas that is in the collection of the Art Gallery of Hamilton, achieved C$451,250 with fees ($315,000), tripling its high estimate of C$150,000.

Arguably the surprise of the night was Ivan Eyre (1935-2022), who was long a professor at the University of Manitoba and is best known for his prairie landscapes. His intensely autumnal acrylic-on-canvas landscape Golden Rod (1981) realised C$391,250 with fees ($273,200), more than tripling its high estimate of C$120,000 while setting a new auction record for the artist. That record was smashed in turn just one lot later, when Eyre’s Yellow Wood (around 1995) realised C$481,250 with fees ($336,000), five times its high estimate of C$90,000.

Ivan Eyre, Golden Rod, 1981 Courtesy Heffel Fine Art Auction House

The beloved William Kurelek, who was raised on a farm near Winnipeg, also made a splash.His snowy landscape painting I Knew How the Clouds Arise (1969), which was featured on the cover of the catalogue for the artist’s first retrospective in 1970, realised C$313,250 including fees ($218,700), just over its high estimate, while the evocative campfire scene Wiener Roast on My Father’s Farm (1974), exceeded expectations to sell for C$265,250 with fees ($185,200).

The Carr painting was one of the star attractions at the Heffel Fine Art Auction House’s marquee autumn sale in Toronto, alongside works by Tom Thomson, Marcelle Ferron, Kenojuak Ashevak and Chief 7IDANsuu James Hart

Across the day’s four sales in Toronto, the auction house set new secondary-market records for 16 artists’ work

High demand for Canadiana, from a historic ceramic dessert set to Group of Seven paintings, drove Heffel’s major autumn sale in Toronto

Works by Group of Seven members Franklin Carmichael and Arthur Lismer, plus Emily Carr, topped the Canadian auction house’s two-part evening sale

The Altruists True Story: 5 Biggest Questions After The Netflix Show’s Ending

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Altruists unpacks a chaotic partnership between Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison, but the story doesn't end after the credits roll on episode 8. Based on the true story of the rise and fall of Sam Bankman-Fried's business ventures, his foray into crypto exchanges, and his tumultuous relationship with his colleague and on-again, off-again, polyamorous partner, Caroline Ellison.

Over the course of eight episodes, the series moves at pace through years of these characters' exploits, with the founding of FTX and Alameda Research being two core parts of the wider narrative, and ultimately a major part of the downfall of people like SBF. However, the series does leave some things unanswered, with regard to other characters, continued relationships, and outcomes for the customers whose money was lost by SBF in his high-risk approach to business.

Thankfully, due to the very public nature of these events and the individuals involved, there are updates and details about many of the burning questions that audiences may have once the limited series wraps up. With that in mind, these are some of the biggest things left unanswered in The Altruists.

Along with the reckless approach to business displayed in The Altruists, one of the most pivotal plots of the entire narrative revolves around the complex relationship between Sam Bankman-Fried and Caroline Ellison. As shown on screen, it appears that neither one was able to effectively communicate their true feelings often.

This is reflected in real life with reports about the coupling, and Bankman-Fried's own biography, Going Infinite: The Rise and Fall of a New Tycoon, providing even more context about how SBF sent Ellison a list of pros and cons for why they should continue sleeping together.

Ellison provided further accounts talking about how SBF would be extremely hot and cold, engaging in romantic relations in one moment and then keeping his distance for long periods. Now, with SBF currently serving a sentence in prison, and Ellison recently being released in January 2026, it appears that the two are not engaged in any sort of active relationship.

One of the other central characters from The Altruists was CZ, or Changpeng Zhao, a Chinese-Canadian billionaire who was painted as a criminal and money launderer in the series. In real life, CZ did end up being sentenced to 4 months in prison after pleading guilty to a handful of financial crimes, and he served out that time, before eventually receiving a presidential pardon from Donald Trump.

During his brief stint in prison, CZ was actually serving time in an adjacent facility to the one where SBF is still enrolled, Lompoc II in California. At present, it appears that CZ is not the public face or central figure in any major organizations, but he remains an active investor and majority shareholder in companies like Binance.

The Altruists spends the majority of its runtime following the impressive rise of Alameda Research, and in turn the FTX trading platform, before both came crashing down in the wake of revelations about the conduct of both SBF and Caroline Ellison and how money was being mismanaged.

The series ends with Bankman-Fried about to be captured by the FBI, and both of his businesses in free-fall. Today, Alameda Research and FTX remain defunct businesses, with both closing their doors and wrapping up operations after filing for bankruptcy in 2022. With the severe damage to public images due to SBF's misconduct and the sentencing, neither company had any legs left to stand on.

At the height of Sam Bankman-Fried's success and popularity, he was ranked as the 41st wealthiest person in the Forbes 400 list. At the time, his wealth was estimated to be somewhere north of $30 billion. But, when SBF was sentenced, part of his punishment was to forfeit 11 billion dollars towards repairing damages, and his companies were liquidated without his seeing any returns from those ventures.

That means that today, SBF's total net worth is close to zero. Interestingly, Caroline Ellison was also forced to pay $11 billion in repairs, though her prison sentence was considerably lighter due to her cooperation. Ultimately, with a few decades of time still to serve, it appears that there is a long way to go before SBF could ever dream of being a billionaire again.

Finally, the entire contention that led to SBF being imprisoned and his businesses falling apart, the $8 billion reallocation of customer funds to shore up Alameda Research, was not addressed in The Altruists' ending. While Bankman-Fried did end up finding the money thanks to a lucky break in the markets with an investment in Anthropic, the bankruptcy and the trials resulted in delays for customers recovering their money.

As of October 2024, the repayments have officially been approved, meaning that customers will receive their money back in full, in accordance with where their balance stood in November 2022, when FTX collapsed. For any customer with less than $50,000 due, they will be repaid between 118% and 119% of that value, though this has led to some frustration from those who believe they should receive more.

In some ways, that makes sense, because crypto markets have continued to thrive in the wake of the FTX's fall, but due to how funds were mismanaged, it seems somewhat miraculous that there is enough money to repay everyone who had investments tied up with the platform in the first place.

Ultimately, The Altruists did a solid job of sticking to the facts of the story, and managed to pack a lot of detail and truth into an eight-episode limited series. Outside of the more personal details of what was said in a room, or the ideas and motivations shared and expressed between individuals, The Altruists manages to deliver a compelling and concise true story that cuts to the heart of one of the biggest financial scandals in modern history.

Johnson & Johnson Delivered 'Immaculate' Prostate Cancer Data at ESMO. Here's Why Investors Should Care

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Johnson & Johnson's $3.05 billion Halda bet is already paying off in the clinic.

By Prosper Junior Bakiny – Nov 19, 2026 at 6:45PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Johnson & Johnson ›

Last year, Johnson & Johnson (JNJ -2.04%) acquired a smaller biotech, Halda Therapeutics, in a cash transaction valued at $3.05 billion. The key asset from that transaction was JNJ-6143, an investigational, early stage candidate for prostate cancer. It will take time to determine whether this acquisition was worth it for Johnson & Johnson, but recent clinical developments suggest that the $3.05 billion the pharmaceutical giant spent may have been well spent. Let me explain.

JNJ-6143 is part of an experimental class of cancer medicines called Regulated Induced Proximity Targeting Chimera, which use proteins found in cancer cells to disrupt certain functions those cells need to survive. This approach could lead to much stronger efficacy than existing standards of care for certain cancers. Johnson & Johnson's JNJ-6143 showed the potential of these therapies in a Phase 1 clinical trial, the results of which it presented at the European Society for Medical Oncology 2026 Congress, held in Madrid in October.

The medicine was tested in patients with metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer. Notably, these patients had received a median of four prior therapies, so we aren't dealing with an easy-to-treat cancer. Yet, JNJ-6143 performed extremely well. It led to an overall response rate (the percentage of patients who respond to treatment) of 75% among patients with measurable disease. This data is highly encouraging. Of course, this was only an early stage study. There is a long road ahead before JNJ-6143 earns approval, that is, if it performs well in future clinical studies.

However, this once again highlights the strength of Johnson & Johnson's pipeline. The company has successfully expanded its lineup of approved drugs in recent years, with newer launches such as Imaavy, which treats generalized myasthenia gravis, and Icotyde, a therapy for plaque psoriasis.

These newer launches could help the company overcome headwinds, such as government-led drug price negotiations and patent cliffs, that it will face over the medium term. Over the next five years, Johnson & Johnson should make significant progress across its pipeline, including with JNJ-6143. Johnson & Johnson's innovative abilities are one of the key reasons the stock is attractive.

And it's worth noting that the company's innovative medicines division could perform even better over the long run, given that it has spun off its slower-growing consumer health division and can focus more on its higher-growth pharmaceutical business. Johnson & Johnson's medtech unit adds even more depth and diversification to the business.

Finally, Johnson & Johnson remains one of the best dividend stocks on the market. With its streak of 64 consecutive years of payout increases, the healthcare leader is a Dividend King, or a stock with 50 or more straight annual dividend increases. Income seekers should stick with this stock for the long term.

Patrick Stewart’s Cause of Death Revealed After Water Cremation

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Less than a month after Patrick Stewart died at the age of 86, his official cause of death was confirmed to be Alzheimer's disease and Lewy body dementia, according to his death certificate.

More details about Patrick Stewart’s death have come to light.

Less than a month after the X-Men actor died at the age of 86, his official cause of death was determined to be Alzheimer's disease and Lewy body dementia, according to his death certificate obtained by TMZ.

It also listed cardiopulmonary arrest—also known as cardiac arrest—as well as a neurocognitive disorder with Lewy body dementia, which is “a brain disorder that can lead to problems with thinking, movement, behavior, and mood,” per Alzheimers.gov.

The Star Trek: The Next Generation alum’s death certificate stated that his remains underwent alkaline hydrolysis—also known as water cremation—which according to the Cremation Association of North America “uses water, alkaline chemicals, heat, and sometimes pressure and agitation, to accelerate natural decomposition.” The process leaves behind bone fragments and a liquid that can then be safely disposed of.

One day after his Oct. 28 death, Stewart’s family shared that he had passed away at his Los Angeles home “in the loving care of his beloved wife" Sunny Ozell.

“He has been held in the warm embrace of family and friends, surrounded by live music, prayer, tears, laughter, and the kind of profound emotion that only he could inspire after a lifetime of devotion to us all,” the Oct. 29 post on Stewart’s Instagram read. “We will forever cherish the peaceful ease and reflection on his face, an enduring reminder of his invincible grace.”

“He will be remembered for the dignity, compassion and humanity he brought to every role,” the statement continued, “in his art and life.”

In the weeks following his death, Ozell—whom Stewart married in 2013—shared her own heartbreaking tribute to her late husband, writing on Instagram Nov. 11, “We left nothing unsaid. He knew I loved him, and I know he loved me.”

“We shared nearly two decades together, and we filled those years with so much *living*,” the 47-year-old added. “I’ve been overwhelmed by the public response to Patrick’s death, and while I’m not at all surprised that he touched so many, I’m sincerely moved by the outpouring of sadness and loss.”

For a look at more fond remembrances of Stewart, keep reading.

"We first met as ambitious young actors with the Royal Shakespeare Company half a century ago but we weren’t in the same productions. My first strong memory was a few years later, meeting him in the street in central London when he told me about an offer to take over a leading part in Star Trek. 'But Patrick think of your stage career in the UK. Do you really want to risk—getting lost in Hollywood for years and years?' Well, he took the risk and thank goodness for us all that he did for not just his devoted Trekkies but film and theatre audiences, who marvelled at his using international celebrity to star in a string of cinema successes (X-Men of course) and in great classical plays by Shakespeare, Ibsen, Arthur Miller, Peter Shaffer, Pinter and Beckett at Stratford-upon-Avon, in London’s West End and on Broadway."

"We lost one of the greats of stage and screen today. And, in my book, a tremendous friend and man. I learned so much from him. I will miss you, Patrick Stewart, all of my days."

"Patrick was one of those icons who we somehow all took for granted would always be with us. I grew up as a fan, and was later gifted the privilege of becoming a colleague and friend, working with him for many years on American Dad!, Family Guy, and Ted. He was that rare actor who could take any bit of dialogue and elevate it, making a good writer sound great, and a great writer sound masterful. He was also as humble as they come. I remember exchanging emails with him a number of years ago when he was beginning day 1 of a new film. He commented to me that he always walked onto a new set feeling like the biggest novice there. My stunned reaction was the same as anyone’s would be. I said, 'Patrick, if you feel that way, the rest of us are screwed.' We have lost a legendary actor today, and for many of us, a good friend. He will be deeply missed by the entire planet."

"What a heartbreaking week it has been. Sir Patrick Stewart was a friend, a colleague, and a magnificent actor. From the stage to the bridge of the Enterprise, he brought intelligence, grace, and a deep sense of humanity to everything he did. Captain Picard stood for the best of us, and so did the man who played him. I was filled with affection and admiration for him and his work."

"I feel so grateful to have known him for the last 25 years and to have been in his orbit for the last 25 years, both as a fellow 'mutant' and as a fellow member of Starfleet. He was positively delicious, hilarious, naughty, incredible sense of humor, and that booming voice of his that always spoke to the very back of the room—as every classically trained Shakespearean great needs to master—has always been an Inspiration. I'm gonna send my condolences to his family, and I'll miss my friend dearly. Rest in peace, Patrick."

"My friend, we shared some memorable times…from the deserts of Arrakis to the mysterious jungles of Thailand and onto the New York stage. I already miss you. You will always be my Gurney Halleck and I, your young pup."

"Patrick Stewart is a legend. His acting talent was not only innate but built from his professionalism, his dedication to craft, his enthusiasm, his generosity, his gentlemanly way of being. He was mischievous and game, always made me laugh and always made a day better. He leaves a treasure trove of work behind and I’m so grateful he came to play w me on Charlie’s Angels. Rest peacefully Patrick. Sending love."

"Patrick Stewart was one of the greats, not just because of his extraordinary talent, but because of the man he was. It was a privilege to work alongside him and an even greater pleasure to know him personally. He was loved by all and will be deeply missed. My heart goes out to his family and loved ones."

"Dearest Patrick. Such sad news and a huge loss for us all. RIP."

"PStew, lover of fresh produce and classic american western songs. a man who even at 80 could recite every work of shakespear[e] he’s ever done. a man who once handed me his drink so he could do five pull ups on the branch of a tree. a man with so much love and respect for his craft and fellow artists. one of the first people in this business to take a chance on me. so grateful to have witnessed his greatness on and off the stage and screen. rest in peace."

"Thank you, Pstew, for being my friend, for being so kind and generous with your heart, for being so funny and vulnerable, and for being so talented and inspiring. Getting cast as Raffi on Star Trek Picard has been a gift that keeps on giving, but the best part was meeting YOU, working with you, and becoming your friend. I am, and always will be, grateful for this amazing experience and adventure we shared. I love you and will miss you, my dear sweet friend. The world lost a great one today... Rest now, Captain, my captain."

"Ouch. This one hurts. Rest well, Patrick."

"He was such a kind, genuine, funny, unique, caring and charming man. I remember his endless jokes, nurturing nature and colourful reading glasses. Every single day spent working by his side was an honour and a privilege I can’t quite believe I had. He was a walking master of his craft, and of life. One of the greatest of our time, on stage and on screen. I have always looked up to him, and always will. As an actor and as a man. He had a wonderful way of wrapping every ancient lesson he imparted in wonder, fun and excitement. I will always remember his warmth and the respect he showed to everyone around him. I will forever hold those months we spent together close to my heart, and will always think of him as a pivotal part of how I view the world around me. So rest in peace, you giant, you legend amongst men. The beautiful work, humanity and kindness you leave behind will live on forever."

"Fans across the world mourn the passing of a true and irreplaceable legend. As Captain Jean-Luc Picard, Patrick Stewart took the starship Enterprise to places it had never gone before, and he inspired a whole next generation of star-gazers who looked longingly ahead to a brighter future for all humanity. Our hearts are heavy, indeed. Rest in the heavens, Captain."
