Real Estate Syndications – Vet the Sponsor Before You Trust the Projected Returns

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Before you invest in a real estate syndication, vet the sponsor, not just its projected returns. Ask how its past deals did when rates jumped, how much of its own money is in this one, and whether the loan’s rate can rise with the market. Commit only money you can leave in for five to seven years.

A syndication’s projected returns are the sponsor’s own forecast, and the sponsor collects fees whether or not they come true.

A syndication pools money from passive investors, the limited partners, to buy one large property, such as an apartment complex, and borrows the rest of the price. The sponsor runs it and typically sells after five to seven years. It earns an acquisition fee when it buys, an asset management fee each year, and a promote, often 20% to 30% of the profit above a set yearly return investors get first, called the preferred return. Only the promote depends on how investors do, so the sponsor’s skill and honesty matter more than the projections.

While rates were low, the syndicator Tides Equities bought apartments with floating-rate loans, whose interest rises with market rates. Its rate caps, which limit how high the rate can go, lasted only two or three years. When its first loans came due, rates had jumped, and Tides couldn’t afford new caps or a refinance, The Real Deal reported. Lenders foreclosed on some of its complexes, and anyone with $50,000 in one of those deals lost all of it.

Vet the sponsor yourself, even when a real estate investing platform lists the deal. Ask it to show you:

Then check the deal. For a floating-rate loan, ask when the rate cap runs out and when the loan comes due. Compare the rents and sale price the sponsor assumes with what similar buildings rent and sell for now. Also find out whether the sponsor can make a capital call, a demand for more money, and what happens to investors who can’t pay.

Size your stake so you could leave it alone for seven years and still afford a capital call.

The 101 money moves that matter most, from the foundations to protecting what you’ve built.

NFL puts product over principles with Puka Nacua suspension | Opinion

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The NFL is tarnishing its precious shield with its suspension* of Puka Nacua – even more than the Los Angeles Rams wide receiver did with his reprehensible behavior.

The league has known since March that Nacua was being sued by a woman who said the player had bitten her on the shoulder during a New Year's Eve dinner, where he also allegedly said something antisemitic. But it wasn't until Thursday, Nov. 19, when Nacua is conveniently already on injured reserve, that the NFL saw fit to suspend him.

Follow along while I do the math: NFL players on IR have to miss a minimum four games before they can return. Nacua, who missed the first of those four games last week while dealing with a knee injury, is now suspended for the next three weeks.

Which means Nacua won't miss any more time than the Rams were already accounting for, and they – and the league – will get one of their biggest and most-productive stars back, healthy and rested, just in time for 6-4 Los Angeles to make a push for the playoffs.

Unless you're a Rams fan, that stinks to high heaven.

Ricky Pearsall, SF (knee) - Out for seasonRicky Pearsall of the San Francisco 49ers works out during training camp practice at SAP Performance Facility on July 27, 2026, in Santa Clara, California.

Chris Brazzell, CAR (knee) - Out for seasonChris Brazzell II of the Carolina Panthers warms up during the Carolina Panthers Training Camp at Carolina Panthers Practice Fields on July 23, 2026, in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Jayden Higgins, HOU (knee) - Out for seasonJayden Higgins of the Houston Texans catches a pass for a touchdown during the second quarter of the game against the Indianapolis Colts at NRG Stadium on January 04, 2026, in Houston, Texas.

Ricky Pearsall, SF (knee) - Out for seasonRicky Pearsall of the San Francisco 49ers works out during training camp practice at SAP Performance Facility on July 27, 2026, in Santa Clara, California.

Chris Brazzell, CAR (knee) - Out for seasonChris Brazzell II of the Carolina Panthers warms up during the Carolina Panthers Training Camp at Carolina Panthers Practice Fields on July 23, 2026, in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Jayden Higgins, HOU (knee) - Out for seasonJayden Higgins of the Houston Texans catches a pass for a touchdown during the second quarter of the game against the Indianapolis Colts at NRG Stadium on January 04, 2026, in Houston, Texas.

What Nacua did was apparently bad enough to merit a three-game suspension, but not bad enough for the NFL to shut him down when it would actually matter? So what, exactly, is the point of suspending Nacua at all? Because it sure doesn't tell him, or anyone else, that there are consequences for unacceptable behavior.

(Nacua's suspension* will cost him close to $1 million in lost salary. That'll be pocket change when he signs a new contract in the offseason, to say nothing of the endorsement money he's still getting.)

Apparently, the story the NFL is spinning is that if Nacua messes up again, the NFL disciplinarians are really going to let him have it! They pinky swear it!

Yes, the leagues' collective bargaining agreement with its players allows players to serve suspensions while on IR. That's not the point. The point is everyone has known for almost nine months that Nacua was going to be suspended, and the NFL just so happened to choose the least-inconvenient time possible, for both Nacua and the Rams, to do it. A time that helps preserve Los Angeles' hopes for a Super Bowl 61 appearance at SoFi Stadium, its home arena.

Other suspensions have been dragged out because of ongoing legal cases. That's not an issue, here, however. Nacua was never charged criminally. The civil lawsuit against him has not been resolved. If the NFL has already waited nine months to hand down punishment everyone was expecting, what difference would it have made for the league to wait another three weeks?

Except for the impact it would have on the Rams' playoff chances, that is, and the all-important postseason intrigue for a major-market NFL team.

It is also true that other players have served suspensions while on IR. According to Adam Schefter, Daniel Ekuale was given five games for violating the performance-enhancing substances policy after suffering a season-ending injury last year. In 2024, Sam Williams was suspended three games for violating the personal conduct policy while out for the season with a torn ACL.

Mike Woods got six games in 2023 for violating the personal conduct policy when he was already out with a torn Achilles. In 2018, David Parry was suspended for four games for violating both the personal conduct and PED policies when his season had already ended because of an ankle injury.

What's the difference between them and Nacua? Ekuale, a defensive lineman, spent seven years in the league, making 21 starts in 64 games and recording six sacks. Williams, a defensive end, has five starts in 58 games over four seasons. Woods, a receiver, had 12 catches in 15 games. Parry, a two-season starter at nose tackle in Indianapolis, played in four games his final two seasons.

Nacua is a two-time Pro Bowler who last year led the league with 129 catches and also had 1,715 yards receiving and 10 touchdowns. He's started all but one game he's played in his first four seasons, including seven this year. Despite missing last week, Nacua is fourth in the NFL with 70 catches.

Nacua shouldn't be punished more harshly because he's one of the NFL's top players. But neither should his star status earn him any favors. The NFL is insulting everyone's intelligence by claiming to take anything that reflects poorly on the league very, very seriously and then turning around and making sure Nacua's punishment won't cause anyone any real hardship.

The NFL's justice system has always been arbitrary. Turns out, it's not very just, either.

Follow USA TODAY Sports columnist Nancy Armour on social media @nrarmour.

Japan core inflation soars to highest this year as energy subsidy expiry pushes up consumer prices

📅 2026-11-19  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Core inflation in Japan soared to a fresh high in 2026, coming in at 2.2% in October as higher energy costs and their knock-on effects pushed up prices in the wider economy.

Core inflation, which strips out prices of food, was lower than the 2.3% expected by Reuters-polled economists, but was higher than the 2.1% in prior month.

Headline inflation came in at 2.3% compared with September's 2.1%, while the so-called "core-core" inflation, which strips out prices of fresh food and energy, dipped slightly to 2.2% from 2.4% in the month before, government data released on Friday showed.

Energy subsidies for households expired in September, exposing consumers to higher prices brought about by the Iran war. Energy prices climbed 2.2% in October, a sharp spike from the 0.6% decline in September. Gas prices jumped 5.8% compared to the 2.4% rise the month before.

Fuel, light and water charges also saw a considerable acceleration, increasing 3.4% compared to last month's 0.8%.

Japan has reportedly approved reintroduction of energy subsidies in December to stave off higher imported energy prices as higher prices crimp growth, hitting consumption and net exports.

October inflation reading is likely to push the Bank of Japan toward another rate hike in December, despite an unexpected contraction in economic growth in the third quarter. Data from LSEG indicates that there is a 75% chance of a 25 basis point hike from the Bank of Japan."The BOJ is likely to look through the temporary weakness in 3Q GDP and focus instead on underlying inflation and wage dynamics," Ma Tieying, senior economist at DBS Group Research, wrote in a Monday note.

Why didn't the Colts trade Jonathan Taylor? RB spared in Indy's fire sale

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Indianapolis Colts were the NFL's biggest sellers ahead of the 2026 NFL trade deadline. They offloaded numerous veteran players, including wide receivers Keenan Allen and Josh Downs, defensive lineman DeForest Buckner and long-time, stalwart left guard Quenton Nelson.

But even despite their activity level, the Colts didn't trade their biggest chip. Running back Jonathan Taylor remained in Indianapolis even after being the subject of persistent trade rumors leading up to the deadline.

Taylor has long been a star running back since being selected in the second round of the 2020 NFL Draft. Still, many thought the Colts might cash in and unload the 27-year-old in a blockbuster deal to continue to add to their coffers of draft assets ahead of what figures to be a challenging rebuild.

So, why did the Colts decide to keep Taylor? Here's what to know about their decision as they move forward with the star running back in tow.

The Colts didn't trade Taylor because they view the 27-year-old running back as a part of their future.

Colts coach Shane Steichen spoke glowingly of Taylor when asked what it mean to keep him in the fold during a media availability the day following the 2026 NFL trade deadline.

"He means a ton. He's a hell of a football player. I'm excited that he's here," Steichen told reporters Nov. 11. "It's awesome for the organization to keep, you know one of the best running backs in the league in your franchise moving forward."

Taylor has certainly thrived across his seven seasons to date with the Colts. He has twice led the NFL in total touchdowns during his career, earned an All-Pro first-team nod in 2021, and is pacing for his fourth season with at least 1,500 scrimmage yards in 2026.

Taylor is also under contract through the 2028 NFL season, so the Colts won't have to worry about locking him down to another deal while he remains in the prime of his career.

That may have been part of the reason the Colts were only willing to consider moving Taylor in a "blockbuster offer," per The Ringer's Tom Pelissero. The Colts received packages including a second-round pick and more for Taylor, but he remains under team control and can continue to serve as Indianapolis' offensive engine as the team rebuilds.

It's also worth noting that Steichen expressed no worries about the relationship between Taylor and the franchise even after the persistent trade rumors surrounding the star running back.

"I can't speak for him, but I know that last week, he said he's proud to wear the horseshoe," Steichen said. "And so like I said, I'm excited that he's here. He's a great freaking football player."

And Taylor reiterated in a media availability of his own that he was happy to remain in Indianapolis.

"No," Taylor replied when asked whether he wanted to be traded before the deadline. "Just regular scheduled programing Tuesday. Like it's all water under the bridge now."

While Taylor remained with the Colts, the 27-year-old may not handle his usual workload down the stretch of the 2026 NFL season.

The Colts are sitting at 3-7 entering Week 11 and are highly unlikely to make any sort of playoff push during the second half of the season. As a result, the Colts have seemingly started to limit Taylor's workload, as he has averaged 17.1 touches per game since Week 7 while playing no more than 69% of the offensive snaps in any game.

By comparison, Taylor averaged 22.8 touches per game and never played fewer than 71% of the team's offensive snaps across the first six games of the season.

Taylor's slight downtick in production has coincided with an uptick in production from rookie backup Seth McGowan. The seventh-rounder averaged just four touches per game across the first six games of the season but has seen that number balloon to nine per game in his last four outings.

Against the Dolphins, Taylor saw 18 touches while McGowan had 12 and scored a touchdown. That 60-40 workload split could end up being closer to the norm moving forward as the Colts look to keep Taylor fresh and healthy during what looks like a lost season.

Below is a look at the Colts' running back depth chart, which remains headlined by Taylor even amid his recent downtick in touches per game.

Giddens, a fifth-round pick in the 2025 NFL Draft, has played sparingly this season, logging six carries for 43 yards. All of those touches game in Indianapolis' 42-7 blowout loss to the Jacksonville Jaguars in Week 8.

Meanwhile, Booth is an undrafted free agent who currently resides on the Colts' practice squad. He spent the offseason with the Cleveland Browns before joining Indianapolis' practice squad on Sept. 1.

Taylor is playing the 2026 NFL season on the final year of a three-year, $42 million extension he inked with the Colts in 2023. After this season, the two-year, $44 million extension he signed with Indianapolis on Aug. 6, 2026, will officially kick in.

Below are the full details of the latter agreement, which will keep Taylor under contract with the Colts through the 2028 season. All details are via Spotrac.com.

Taylor's $22 million AAV ranks third league-wide among running backs. Only Jahmyr Gibbs ($22.5 million) and Bijan Robinson ($22.5 million) are making more.

‘Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping’ Cast Guide: All the Tributes, Mentors and Younger Versions of Characters in Prequel

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Before Katniss Everdeen ever volunteered as tribute for the Hunger Games, another District 12 victor made his way out of the arena, but with much fewer odds in his favor.

“The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” is set 24 years before the first “Hunger Games” film (and 40 years after “The Ballad of Songbird and Snakes), following the 50th Hunger Games that crowned Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada) as victor. To “celebrate” the second Quarter Quell, a higher-stakes edition of the games held every 25 years, the Capitol required twice the amount of children to participate, sending 48 tributes to the arena instead of the traditional 24.

With original stars Jennifer Lawrence, Josh Hutcherson and Woody Harrelson returning, the “Sunrise on the Reaping” brings together a star-studded lineup to play younger versions of some of the franchise’s well-known characters, including President Snow (Ralph Fiennes), Effie Trinket (Elle Fanning), Plutarch Heavensbee (Jesse Plemons), Caesar Flickerman (Kieran Culkin), Wiress (Maya Hawke) and Beetee (Kelvin Harrison Jr.). The film also introduces new characters such as fellow tribute Maysilee Donner (Mckenna Grace); District 12’s escort, Drusilla Sickle (Glenn Close) and stylist, Magno Stift (Billy Porter); and Haymitch’s girlfriend, Lenore Dove (Whitney Peak).

Part of Suzanne Collins’ best-selling book series, “Sunrise on the Reaping” is the sixth film of the franchise and arrives in theaters Nov. 20. Francis Lawrence and Nina Jacobson, who have collaborated on every “Hunger Games” film, return as director and producer, respectively. The most recent film, the President Snow prequel “The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes,” was released in 2024 and starred Tom Blyth and Rachel Zegler.

See the full cast of “The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping” below.

Joseph Zada stars as Haymitch Abernathy, a tribute from District 12 who is the future mentor to the main series’ protagonist, Katniss Everdeen. Despite being born on the same day as the Reaping, Haymitch’s birthday luck runs short when he is selected for the second Quarter Quell and soon finds himself on President Snow’s radar after publicly defying the capitol.

Throughout the film, Haymitch is thrust into several intense moments, whether they are grounded in emotion or consist of demanding action sequences. One of Zada’s favorite scenes is a particular battle near the end of the film, which he tells Variety makes him “look like a fucking action star.”

“In terms of acting, I just want to throw myself completely in the deep end with every project. And it helps so much having that attitude in the arena, because that’s just my character the whole time,” Zada says. “He’s really going for it, and I was like, ‘There’s no way for me to do this believably unless I’m throwing myself down that mountain or pushing myself to the edge of death and almost injuring myself every single day. There was no other way for me to do that and make it believable without just completely diving in the deep end.”

Haymitch Abernathy was played by Woody Harrelson in the original films.

Joining Haymitch in the second Quarter Quell is District 12 tribute Maysilee Donner, who’s played by Mckenna Grace and is the original owner of Katniss’ mockingjay pin. Initially at odds with her fellow District 12 tributes, Maysilee eventually joins their alliance with the Newcomers and grows particularly close to Haymitch, encouraging him to defy the Capitol.

As a long-time fan of the “Hunger Games” franchise, Grace tells Variety she wanted her portrayal of Maysilee to be accurate to Collins’ source material. One of the things she prioritized was keeping the character’s anger consistent throughout the film instead of having her arc of “learning to be kinder diminish her anger.

“While she finds happiness in being kind to the people around her and protecting them, she’s still a very angry character. And I think that it’s really important to show young women and anyone in general that the two can coexist,” she says. “You can be very angry at the world, or you can be very loud about the things that you believe in and in standing up for yourself and the people around you. But you can also be kind throughout that.

Grace also portrays Merilee Donner, Maysilee’s twin sister. Her previous credits include “I, Tonya,” “Regretting You,” “The Handmaid’s Tale” and “Ghostbusters: Afterlife.” She is next set to star in “Scooby Doo: Origins” as a young Daphne Blake.

Lenore Dove Baird is Haymitch’s girlfriend and a descendant of the Covey, which Lucy Gray Baird (Rachel Zelger) was a part of in “Songbirds and Snakes.” After her mother died during childbirth, Lenore Dove was raised by her uncles Tam Amber and Clerk Carmine Clade, whom audiences met in the previous prequel. Whitney Peak plays the character after starring in films like “Molly’s Game” and series such as “Chilling Adventures of Sabrina.”

Ralph Fiennes stars as Coriolanus Snow, the cruel and tyrannical president of Panem who previously mentored Lucy Gray Baird, a District 12 tribute who won the 10th Hunger Games. After Haymitch publicly defies the Capitol, Snow marks him as a target and stops at nothing to suppress his resistance. No stranger to playing the villain, Fiennes has also starred as the antagonist in “The Menu” and as Voldemort in the “Harry Potter” franchise. Snow was played by Donald Sutherland in the original films and by Tom Blyth in “The Hunger Games: The Ballad of Songbirds & Snakes.”

Elle Fanning plays Effie Trinket, a Capitol stylist who steps in to help dress the District 12 tributes for their interviews. Effie ultimately becomes the district’s escort in the original films, where she was played by Elizabeth Banks. Earlier this year, Fanning received an Emmy nomination for “Margo’s Got Money Troubles” after previously starring in “A Complete Unknown,” “Maleficent” and “Super 8.”

Plutarch Heavensbee is a cameraman assigned to cover District 12 during the Hunger Games. Although he serves as a trusted advisor to President Snow, Plutarch is secretly involved in the growing rebellion against the Capitol and helps Haymitch execute his plan to break the arena. He’s played by Jesse Plemons, who also starred in “Bugonia,” “The Power of the Dog,” “Kinds of Kindness” and “Game Night.” Plutarch ultimately becomes a gamemaker for the third Quarter Quell in “Catching Fire,” where he is played by Philip Seymour Hoffman for the remainder of the original films.

As the host of the Hunger Games, Caesar Flickerman is responsible for keeping Capitol citizens entertained and invested throughout the annual event. Typically known for his bold outfits and quick-witted humor, the role was originated by Stanley Tucci and is now played by Kieran Culkin, whose previous credits include “Succession” and “A Real Pain.” Caesar’s grandfather Lucky (Jason Schwartzman) was the announcer in “Songbirds and Snakes.”

Drusilla Sickle is a Capitol chaperone for the District 12 tributes. Openly disdainful of the poor district, particularly Maysilee, Drusilla hopes to earn a promotion to a higher-ranking group of tributes and takes extreme measures to ensure her presentations are nothing short of perfection. Glenn Close stars as the vain escort after playing a similarly eccentric character in “101 Dalmatians.” She is also known for her roles in “Fatal Attraction” and “Dangerous Liaisons.”

Billy Porter plays Magno Stift, a reptile-loving Capitol stylist unwillingly assigned to the District 12 tributes. Magno puts little effort into dressing the tributes on the rare occasion he decides to show up. Porter is largely known for his stage career, having starred in Broadway shows including “Kinky Boots,” “Cabaret” and “Shuffle Along.”

After winning the 34th Hunger Games, Beetee secretly conspired against the Capitol, which President Snow eventually discovered. Now, Snow is punishing the District 3 victor by reaping his son, Ampert, for the second Quarter Quell. Kelvin Harrison Jr. plays the character after starring in films including “Mufasa: The Lion King” and “Elvis.” Jeffrey Wright originated the role in the previous films.

Also a fan of the franchise, Harrison Jr. tells Variety that, while he would refer to the books and Wright’s performance, he primarily focused on the world and character that the screenwriters of “Sunrise on the Reaping” created for that specific moment in time.

“What they [the screenwriters] gave me the real estate for was really fleshing out a sense of knowingness that there is no space for grief for him right now. He is in a position where there isn’t self-preservation,” Harrison Jr. says. “There is overcoming in order to have a larger cause to take care of a larger party, and what’s at stake is his own flesh and blood. And I wanted to figure out how to navigate the feeling of grief as something you can’t really control, but you’re trying to outrun it. But you really can’t.”

Harrison Jr. and Wright previously played father and son in 2018’s “Monster” and will do so again in “Samo Lives,” the upcoming Jean-Michel Basquiat biopic.

Wiress is the exceptionally intelligent victor of the 49th Hunger Games who, alongside Mags, mentors Haymitch, Maysilee, Wyatt and Lou Lou for the second Quarter Quell. She is played by Maya Hawke, who is known for her roles in “Stranger Things,” “Do Revenge” and “Inside Out 2,” and was originated by Amanda Plummer in “The Hunger Games: Catching Fire.”

Little is known about Wiress beyond the fact that her arena was filled with mirrors and that she won without killing a single tribute. Hawke tells Variety she “relished in” every piece of information she could find about the character, hoping to portray a version of Wiress who was “beautiful, curious, kind of shy and uniquely capable.”

“I imagine that she probably lost some allies and some friends in her games, and that emotionally attaching to these young people now would be a living nightmare,” Hawke says about Wiress’ relationships with the District 12 tributes. “It’s like I’m trying to see them as pigs, and could one of these pigs work for my protest? I’m trying not to see them as human, and what I tried to build in was that she slowly starts to see them as human, and that’s how she gets so messed up.”

Mags, the winner of the 11th Hunger Games from District 4, mentors the District 12 tributes for the 50th games. She is played by Lili Taylor, who also starred in “The Conjuring,” “Mystic Pizza” and “American Crime,” and was originated by Lynn Cohen in “The Hunger Games: Catching Fire.”

Taylor tells Variety she avoided relying too heavily on Cohen’s performance, instead focusing on who Mags was at this particular point in her life rather than the character’s eventual fate. Working with director Francis Lawrence, the two created a version of Mags that served as a motherly figure to her tributes.

“In some ways, Mags just really cares about those young mentees and will do whatever she can to help them, even under the circumstances,” she says. “It’s so strange when you really think about it deeply, but it’s sort of like they [the tributes] have a job to do, and I really like them, and I want them to do well.”

Jennifer Lawrence reprises her role as Katniss Everdeen, the protagonist of the original “Hunger Games” films. Reaped for the 74th games, the District 12 tribute became the face of the rebellion with her mockingjay pin, which was passed down from Maysilee Donner. Since joining the franchise in 2012, Lawrence has starred in films including “Silver Linings Playbook,” “Die My Love” and “No Hard Feelings.”

Peeta Mellark is Katniss’ fellow District 12 victor of the 74th Hunger Games and her love interest in the original films, making him a key role in the rebellion. Josh Hutcherson returns to the role 11 years after the release of “The Hunger Games: Mockingjay – Part 2″ and has since starred in the “Five Nights at Freddy’s” films and “I Love LA.”

The role of Haymitch Abernathy was originated by Woody Harrelson in the original films, where he served as the heavy-drinking, hard-shelled mentor to Katniss and Peeta. Secretly conspiring with Capitol insiders, Haymitch helped launch the start of the rebellion and oversaw the dethroning of President Snow. Harrelson’s additional credits include “Cheers,” “Brothers” and “Now You See Me.”

Proserpina Trinket is Effie’s younger sister and a Capitol prep team member assigned to Haymitch for the Quarter Quell. Played by Iris Apatow, the university student is constantly worrying her grade will be affected due to Magno’s failure to dress the District 12 tributes. Apatow’s previous credits include “Funny People,” “Knocked Up” and “This is 40.”

Working alongside Proserpina, Vitus is also a part of Haymitch’s Capitol prep team who is similarly apprehensive about how his grade will be affected. He is played by Edvin Ryding, who previously starred in “28 Years Later” and “Mother Goose.”

Ben Wang plays Wyatt Callow, the first male tribute from District 12 to be reaped. He and his family are widely known throughout the district as the “booker boys,” a name that reflects their illegal gambling business that takes bets on the annual Hunger Games. Wang plays the character after appearing in films like “The Long Walk,” “Karate Kid: Legends” and “Mean Girls.”

Molly McCann stars as Louella, Haymitch’s young neighbor in District 12 who is also selected to fight in the Quarter Quell. Often styled in two braids and having earned the nickname “sweetheart” by Haymitch, Louella offers many similarities to Katniss.

Iona Bell plays Lou Lou, a lookalike of Louella McCoy assigned by President Snow to serve as a District 12 tribute. Though her origins in the film are unknown, she was said to be from District 11 in Collins’ novel. This is Bell’s first major film role.

Ampert is a District 3 tribute who is also the son of former victor Beetee Latier. He created the Newcomers alliance that would outnumber the Careers and helped Haymitch with his plans inside the arena. Ampert is played by Percy Daggs IV, who has also acted in “Never Let Go” and “Paradise.”

Silka is one of the female Careers — a tribute who’s trained to compete in the games from a young age — from District 1. Played by Laura Marcus, Silka is set on targeting Maysilee from their early interactions during training. Marcus’ credits also include “Death by Lightning” and “The Jetty.”

Panache is one of two male tributes from District 1 and a member of the Careers who personally targeted Haymitch since their first interaction, physically attacking him during training and vowing to kill him in the arena. He’s played by Jhaleil Swaby, who also appeared in “Neagley” earlier this year.

Rada Rae plays Wellie, one of the two female tributes from District 6. Though her role in the film is relatively limited, Wellie forms a trusting friendship with Haymitch in the novel, with the protagonist vowing to protect her as much as he could in the arena. Rae previously starred in Netflix’s “Exterritorial.”

Ringina is a District 7 tribute who is a part of the Newcomers alliance. She is played by Tatyana Muzondo, who is making her film debut.

Salimou Thiam plays District 9 tribute Clayton, who joins the Newcomers alongside Haymitch and Ampert.

Smylie Bradwell stars as Haymitch’s younger brother, Sid, who is two years short of having his name entered in the annual reaping. Largely introduced to audiences via “Hamnet,” Bradwell has also starred in “Faith” and “The Crow Girl.”

Burdock is the future father of Katniss Everdeen and Haymitch’s best friend in District 12. Played by Scot Greenan, Burdock first began dating Asterid around the second Quarter Quell and is also Lenore Dove’s distant relative. Phillip Troy Linger played the character in the original films.

Asterid is the future mother of Katniss who first began dating Burdock during the events of the second Quarter Quell. She is also a close friend of the Donner twins, standing with them at the reaping and sharing an emotional goodbye after Maysilee’s name was called.

Opportunity Zone Funds Defer Capital Gains Tax – Tax-Free Growth Takes 10 Years

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Put a capital gain into an opportunity zone fund only if you’d reinvest that money anyway and can leave it there for 10 years. For money invested from 2027, the tax on the gain is put off for five years and trimmed, not erased. The fund’s own growth goes tax-free only after a decade.

The 2025 tax law that made opportunity zones permanent set those terms, and they start on Jan. 1, 2027, when the Treasury’s new zones take effect.

After you sell stock, a business, or property at a gain, you have 180 days to put that gain into a qualified opportunity fund, which invests in designated low-income areas called opportunity zones. The tax on the gain is deferred for five years. Then 10% of the gain goes untaxed, or 30% if the fund keeps at least 90% of its assets in rural zones. Hold the fund at least 10 years, and its growth is tax-free when you sell, though growth after its 30th year is taxed.

Say a fund at least holds its value, and you put a $200,000 gain from a 2027 stock sale into it. Your 2032 return reports $180,000 of that gain, and that tax comes due while the fund still holds your money. Sell after 10 years, in 2037 at the earliest, and the fund’s growth is tax-free.

The switch to the new rules makes timing matter. Gains already deferred under the old rules become taxable on Dec. 31, 2026, and IRS guidance bars rolling them into a new fund. A 2026 gain whose 180 days run into January 2027 can wait and go in under the new rules.

Only the gain qualifies, so any extra money you add counts as a separate investment with no tax break. Getting the full break also means leaving your money in projects in low-income areas for a decade, and the manager’s fees cut your return. The state securities regulators’ association warns that you could escape the tax and still lose your whole investment.

So judge the fund as an investment first. Read what it will build and where, look up its manager and anyone selling it to you on FINRA’s BrokerCheck, and get every fee in writing.

Before you sign, ask whether you’d buy the fund with no tax break, and walk away if the answer is no.
